PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES,
RECOVERABLE COSTS OF
LITIGATION, AND SANCTIONS

INFORMATION NOTE
What this Part is about: This Part deals with 3 matters:
- lawyers’ retainer agreements (including contingency fee agreements),
and lawyers’ charges - how each can be reviewed for reasonableness
by a review officer at the request of a client or lawyer
-
how the Court and assessment officers assess the costs of litigation
that are payable by one party to another
-
the sanctions the Court may impose for contravention of the rules
and the Court’s authority to declare a person in civil contempt of
Court, including the penalties that may be imposed as a result of the
declaration.
Words and phrases used in this Part that have defined meanings in
the Appendix [Definitions] include: assessment officer; claim; client;
commencement document; contingency fee agreement; costs award; Court;
court clerk; defendant; enactment; expert; file; judge; judgment; lawyer;
lawyer’s charges; medical examination; order; party; personal representative;
plaintiff; pleading; prescribed form; property; record; retainer agreement;
review officer; rules; trustee
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES,
RECOVERABLE COSTS OF
LITIGATION, AND SANCTIONS

10-3
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.1
DIVISION 1
LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RETAINER AGREEMENTS
AND RIGHT OF REVIEW
Deﬁnitions
10.1 In this Part,
(a) “assessment ofﬁcer” means the court clerk for the judicial centre in
which the action is located;
(b) “review ofﬁcer” means an assessment ofﬁcer who, in the opinion of
the clerk of the Court, has for the purpose of reviewing contingency
fee or retainer agreements and lawyers’ charges
(i) an acceptable degree in law, and
(ii) sufﬁcient experience in the practice of law,
and who is designated as a review ofﬁcer by
(iii) the clerk of the Court for the judicial centre in which the
action is located, or
(iv) if there is no clerk of the Court for the judicial centre in which
the action is located, the Minister.
Subdivision 1
Lawyers’ Charges
Payment for Lawyers’ Services and Contents of Lawyer’s Account
10.2(1) Except to the extent that a retainer agreement otherwise provides, a
lawyer is entitled to be paid a reasonable amount for the services the lawyer
performs for a client considering
(a) the nature, importance and urgency of the matter,
(b) the client’s circumstances,
(c) the trust, estate or fund, if any, out of which the lawyer’s charges are
to be paid,
(d) the manner in which the services are performed,
(e) the skill, work and responsibility involved, and
(f) any other factor that is appropriate to consider in the circumstances.¹
(2) A lawyer may be paid in advance or take security for future lawyer’s charges.²
(3) Every lawyer’s account must
(a) contain a reasonable statement or description of the services
performed,
(b) show the fee for the service, and

Related Provisions
2.17 (lawyer as litigation representative) ; 10.35 (contents of bill of costs) ; 2.25 (duty of

lawyer); 2.27 (limited retainers) ; 10.4 (charging order)
There are reference works about lawyers’s charges to their clients.²
A. Contract vs. Default Mode
The lawyer and client can contract for payment on any basis which they wish,³
subject to the
power of the review ofﬁcer to intervene if a contingency fee contract was unreasonable.⁴
On what
a retainer agreement cannot do, see R.10.5n.
Rule 10.2(1) is only a default mode, operating in absence of different contractual provisions
between the lawyer and client.⁵
Rule 10.2 also applies if a contrary contract is unenforceable, e. g.
a contingency agreement with serious deviations from R.10.7.⁶
So in such a case the client does
not escape making any payment.⁷
If there is no express contract, then the amount of the fees is initially ﬁxed by the lawyer’s bill,
but the client has the right to have the review ofﬁcer reduce it.⁸
On a review where there is no
express contract, or the contract seems to invoke R.10.2, the factors in R.10.2 govern.⁹
It will
be noted that the hours spent are only one of about 11 factors listed there. But if the time was
properly spent, in many cases it will be the most important of those factors.¹⁰
Generally speaking, the lawyer should not charge for unnecessary steps, nor steps taken
which did not justify their cost.¹¹

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.2(3)
10-5
The criteria for fees settling an estate may differ somewhat.¹
B. More Than One Payor
This Rule also applies where someone not the client (such as a mortgagor) has contracted to
pay the lawyer’s bill, but the contract does not set the basis for payment.²
If a law ﬁrm works for two clients at once, it is presumed that they share the bill and that the
ﬁrm does not get paid twice.³
C. Misconduct
If a lawyer is negligent, that may lead to a set-off of any resulting loss, but is not usually
enough in itself to deprive the lawyer entirely of any fee.⁴
A conﬂict of interest by the lawyer need not disentitle him or her to the usual fee, if the work was
properly done and no prejudice is shown.⁵
Nor is breach of the Law Society’s Code of Conduct in
itself ground to reduce the fee; but failure or refusal to communicate with the client, or rudeness
to the client, may be.⁶
D. Instructions Govern
In litigation between siblings where all sides seem to be carrying questions of principle beyond
what is really practical, one cannot cut back the fees of the lawyer to his client on one side to
the amounts in issue or some proportion to them.⁷
A client can demand Cadillac treatment, and
then will have to pay for it, not merely pay for basic or minimal help. Lawyers can only give time
estimates and seek instructions; they cannot judge whether a suit is important to their clients,
especially where on its face the claim could yield a large sum. If the client tells the lawyer to leave
no stone unturned, the client must pay for that and cannot later complain of lack of economy.⁸
E. Security for Fees
It is common for a lawyer to get a deposit or even advance payment of a fee before he or
she acts. Most lawyers know that the accounting Rules of the Law Society, and the Code of
Professional Conduct, bear heavily on putting that payment into trust and when it can come out
of trust.
Fewer lawyers are familiar with R.10.1(2) or the possibility of taking other security such as a
mortgage.⁹
The expectation that fruits of litigation will pass through the hands of the winner’s lawyer is a
sort of security for that lawyer. Rule 10.31(4) might suggest that it can easily be defeated.¹⁰

R.10.3(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
F. Dangers for Lawyer
A lawyer must walk a tightrope in rendering an account. On the one hand, she would violate
codes of conduct if she rendered an account which she knew to be much too high, as a bargaining
device. On the other hand, the account usually binds the lawyer, so she cannot later substitute a
bigger account if the ﬁrst one was lower than need be. There is a fairly wide exception where the
bill or bills rendered were only interim ones, but the whole topic is intricate, and the numerous
cases conﬂict somewhat.¹
G. Miscellaneous
On the history of courts’ regulation of solicitors’ fees, see the Hamill case.²
There is no longer a requirement that an account initially be signed, but if a lawyer wants his
or her account reviewed, he or she must ﬁrst sign it.³
After settlement of a class action subject to approval in four provinces, counsel fees were
approved at an agreed $37.29 million. Criteria were discussed.⁴
Where the Supreme Court of Canada appoints counsel at public expense, the usual legal
aid rates for defence counsel are not a ceiling, nor even a presumed rate, even if the Legal Aid
Society administers the retainer. But usual rates are a signiﬁcant factor, and the court cannot
presume that resources will cover every need. Importance, complexity, and experience of counsel
are also relevant.⁵
A provincial “sales” tax on lawyers’ services is constitutional.⁶
Can the court authorize prepayment of costs out of a trust? See the Residential Warranty
case.⁷
Rule 10.2 does not apply to the question of costs to the opponent on a solicitor-client (full
indemnity) basis.⁸
Lawyer Acting in Representative Capacity
10.3(1) A lawyer may charge lawyer’s fees for services performed as a lawyer
whether or not the lawyer is also acting in the capacity of a guardian, mortgagee,
personal representative or trustee.⁹

(2) Despite subrule (1), a lawyer acting as a guardian, mortgagee, personal

an estate, a fund, mortgaged property or a mortgage loan for which the lawyer

trustee
A lawyer can act both as executor and as the lawyer for the estate, and bill for that; but if he
or she does, the roles have to be kept distinct, and careful records made to distinguish the two.³
A lawyer cannot justify a bill to an estate on the grounds that the deceased had agreed to the
method of billing, without corroboration of the lawyer’s evidence about the agreement, particularly
where on its face the agreement alleged seems to produce too high a fee.⁴
On an agreement about fees to an estate, see the Salmon case.⁵
If a co-trustee and guardian of an estate is a solicitor, the court must approve his or her fees
or disbursements, and if they are taken without approval, the court can review the charges and
order some paid back.⁶
If the solicitor does clerical work for the estate, the charges for that should
be lower than lawyers’ rates.⁷
Charging Order for Payment of Lawyer’s Charges
10.4(1) On application by a lawyer, the Court may declare property speciﬁed in
its order, including property that may be subsequently recovered in an action, to
be subject to a charge as security for payment of the lawyer’s charges.
(2) The order referred to in subrule (1) may only be made if
(a) the lawyer establishes to the Court’s satisfaction that the lawyer’s
charges will not be or are unlikely to be paid unless the order is
made, and

(4) On application, the Court may make any other order necessary to obtain the
amount required to pay the lawyer’s charges from the property subject to the
charge.
(5) An order must not be made under this rule if in all the circumstances the
Court considers that to make the order would be unfair.
(6) Nothing defeats or affects a charge on property imposed under this rule
unless the property is disposed of to a bona ﬁde purchaser for value without
notice of the charge.¹

Related Provisions
10.1(2) (agreement to provide security) ; 10.31(4) (set off of costs) ; 10.41(5) (set off of costs).

A. Types of Security
One must distinguish three different types of security which a lawyer may have: a common-law
(or equitable) right to hold, set-off, and the court order permitted by R.10.4.²
The common-law (or equitable) right is merely a passive right, not a right to sue or get an
order.³
It lets the lawyer hold onto papers, securities, or funds belonging to the client, until the
lawyer is paid. The lawyer may also have a common-law or equitable right to set off what is
owing to the lawyer against what money the lawyer owes to the client.⁴
It is doubtful that the
common law allowed the lawyer enforce his lien actively by selling or foreclosing on the security.⁵
Presumably the lawyer might be able to sue the client, get a judgment, and then proceed to

civil enforcement against what item the lawyer held, if it had any resale value. The common-law
lawyer’s lien is also weak because it extends only to what the lawyer physically holds, not e. g.
what that represents.¹
And courts often hold that the lien does not prevail against third persons,
even trustees in bankruptcy or receivers of the client.²
B. Charging Order
Rule 10.4 lets a lawyer apply to the court and get a charging order, creating a sort of mortgage
in favor of the lawyer over certain assets of the client.³
It is a good remedy where applicable,
because it prevails over the interests of all the persons who beneﬁt from the lawyer’s efforts,
and is a secured claim which prevails over unsecured claims and collusive settlements.⁴
But this
charging remedy can be given only in fairly narrow circumstances. The lawyer must prove that he
or she meets all these conditions:⁵
(a) The lawyer’s charges are unlikely to be paid without a charging order.⁶
(b) The lawyer ran or defended litigation, which may not include expropriation.⁷
(c) The lawyer’s efforts in the suit have recovered or preserved (defended) the same net
property sought to be charged,⁸
but that probably includes matrimonial property suits,⁹
or
suits to enforce or remove a lien, mortgage, or caveat.¹⁰
(d) The charge is limited to fees and disbursements incurred¹¹
in the same suit which
recovered or preserved this property; it cannot cover other accounts for other suits or for
non-litigious work.¹²

unfair to give it.¹
A charging order acts in rem, and is like a salvage claim.²
All property salvaged can be charged, unlike the case of a common-law solicitor's lien (which
is conﬁned to property of the solicitor's client).³
Even a creditor with (previously) higher priority. All
parties concerned know there is litigation and that the court may later make orders about costs.⁴
A Rule-10.4 solicitor’s charge does not always have priority over other registered interests.
Relevant is whether the secured creditor had beneﬁtted from the lawyer’s efforts. It is different if
giving the solicitor priority would negate entirely any beneﬁt of the lawyer’s work. Here the lawyer
did not beneﬁt the mortgagees, but recovered a loss from a fraud against the law ﬁrm. If the
dispute is priority, the competing creditor need not seek to upset the solicitor’s charge entirely.⁵
Subdivision 2
Retainer Agreements
Retainer Agreements
10.5(1) A lawyer may make an agreement with a client about the amount and
manner of payment of the whole or any part of past or future lawyer’s charges
for services performed by the lawyer.
(2) The amount a lawyer is to be paid may be determined in any appropriate way,
including
(a) a gross sum,
(b) commission,
(c) percentage,
(d) salary, or
(e) an hourly rate.
(3) The amount payable may be at the same or at a greater or lesser rate than the
rate to which the lawyer would be entitled under rule 10.2 [Payment for lawyer’s
services and contents of lawyer’s account] if no retainer agreement were entered
into.⁶

1 R.10.4(5). See the C. P. E. , Chapter 82, Part O.3. It was unfair where the class action settlement barred any
additional fees for any other services, and the retainer contract said no recovery by lawyer, no pay. The
settlement provided ample fees: Calf Robe (M. C. R. ) v. A. -G. Can. , supra. But this discretion to withhold a
charging order should not be exercised in favor of an unsecured creditor with no title, interest, or charge in
the property in question: Royal Bank v. Laughlin, supra. Delay in moving is a ground to deny a charging

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.5(3)

Related Provisions
10.7 (contingency agreements)

A. Basic Principle
If the client is of age and sound mind, and not a trustee in bankruptcy or other court ofﬁcer
or ﬁduciary, the client and the lawyer can validly make almost any contract which they wish,
governing the timing of and the basis for, the lawyer’s fees or disbursements.¹
An agreement for
periodic billing can govern whether such bills are tentative or ﬁnal, and so set the time for review
by the review ofﬁcer.²
Since the Fatal Accidents Act requires that the plaintiff be the executor or administrator, that
person has authority to make legal fee agreements (including contingency ones) covering the
funds later recovered under the suit, including funds for a child. Such tasks are common for
executors. The Public Trustee can make representations about a fee for a child; the court must
approve any fee.³
A review ofﬁcer cannot interpret such an agreement, but can say whether an agreement is
understandable enough to comly with the Rules.⁴
An oral retainer agreement is valid, so long as it is not a contingency agreement.⁵
The contract
can take almost any form (with a few express exceptions in these Rules), so long as it is clear and
express.⁶
A lawyer cannot charge higher hourly rates than those agreed to.⁷
A fee contract must
be very clear if it is to allow a fee higher than hourly rates, for success.⁸
B. Forbidden Terms
However, there are 4 things which the contract cannot do:
1. relieve the lawyer from liability, e. g. for negligence (R.10.6(1) (a) );
2. bar the power of the review ofﬁcer (within 6 months) to reduce the lawyer’s charges if
they are not in accordance with the contract, or maybe if the contract is unreasonable (R.
10.9);
3. remove the client’s power to ﬁre the lawyer (R.10.6(2) );⁹
4. remove the client’s power to discontinue or settle the suit over the lawyer’s opposition (R.
10.6(1) (b) ).

Hajduk Gibbs 2014 ABQB 632, 597 AR 254, and Khan v. Paul A. Kazakoff P. C.2019 ABQB 168, JCC

R.10.5(3) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
There is no bar to clauses setting what happens if the review is terminated, or providing for
arbitration.¹
C. Explanations to Client
It is doubtful that independent legal advice is needed before a contract is made between
solicitor and prospective client.²
It is not true that a large number of things must always be explained by the lawyer to the
client, so that if they are not all explained, any agreement between lawyer and client is void or
unenforceable. Instead, what has to be explained depends on all the circumstances, including
what the parties already know and what the deal is. If there is a clear and clearly-understood
agreement to bill on a certain basis, it is both unnecessary, and harmful, to explain the contrary. It
would be confusing to explain other possible bases for billing or other Rules of Court which do not
apply.³
A case where a client signed an unexplained written document could be different.⁴
Though there might be waiver of rights (needing explanation) where a lawyer wants to change
the basis of billing partway through the retainer, there is no waiver where there is one consistent
type or basis for billing from the outset, and it is clear that nothing else will be considered or
agreed to. There is then no need to explain waiver.⁵
If a lawyer contracts with his client to change the billing basis to hourly rate from some other
basis, the lawyer should tell the client what hours have already been accumulated and so would
be charged.⁶
D. Interpretation and Credibility
On evidence, see R.10.17 n.
There is a review of the objective nature, and rules of, interpreting fee or retainer agreements,
including the factual matrix.⁷
A fee contract is construed as of the date it is made, not later.⁸
Some courts suggest that in factual disputes between solicitor and client over fees, the client
should be believed and the solicitor stereotyped and disbelieved,⁹
but that is not the law in Alberta.¹⁰
If there is no fee contract, or the contract seems to invoke R.10.2, then the factors in R.10.2
apply.¹¹

10-13
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.5(3)
Since retainer agreements are treated contra preferentem,¹
may hints and indirect
suggestions in a written contract override an express sentence to the contrary?²
Though a review ofﬁcer cannot interpret a retainer contract, he or she can review it for
reasonableness. An unspeciﬁed term of an order or implied retainer contract can be read in by
the review ofﬁcer as reasonableness, for R.10.9 mandates that.³
It is extremely unlikely that a client had a contract with a lawyer for services under the client’s
direction, but no possible liability to pay any fees, even if there was an insurance company, when
the insurer did not select or instruct the lawyer and had no exposure to indemnify if the insured
lost the litigation.⁴
The party having to pay costs has an interest in the contingency agreement and is entitled to
full disclosure of it. The plaintiffs did not seek costs on Schedule C, and the disclosure order was
conditional on seeking costs based on the contingency agreement. Rule 10.15(b) on conﬁdentiality
does not apply. The paying party is not a party to the fee contract, which only becomes relevant
by being incorporated into the costs award. The winning parties were given costs from the losing
party of 75% of the actual amount of expenses incurred. The winning party had a contingency
agreement with their lawyer.⁵
A retainer agreement is presumed conﬁned to the initial suit in the trial courts and does not
cover appeals from that, which would ordinarily need a separate contract.⁶
On an agreement about fees to an estate, see the Salmon case.⁷
A contractual discount for prompt payment cannot be relied on by a client who has the court
review the account and does not pay promptly, even if he had previously given a (partial) deposit.⁸
E. Miscellaneous
A retainer agreement may not be applicable to legal work done outside of the scope set out
in the retainer. When there is no applicable written retainer, the Court will determine the scope of
the retainer governing the parties, and the terms of that agreement is largely a question of fact.⁹
Even if signatures on a retainer agreement were genuine, if the client corporation did not exist
at the relevant time, there could be no retainer.¹⁰
The court can “void” a retainer agreement between a lawyer and a client because of the
client’s incapacity, especially how much the lawyer knew of that or should have found out. The
tests are analyzed in the framework of contract law. If a lawyer knows certain things, that may give
the lawyer the duty to inquire further.¹¹

R.10.6(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
The court mildly criticized a client who did not appeal review of her lawyer’s account but then
later challenged the retainer, though not with any notice of motion, nor any transcription of the
taxation.¹
A lawyer can recover only expenses incurred and included in the retainer, or something which
would be incurred in the normal course.²
If one co-plaintiff waived privilege for a fee agreement, by seeking advance costs, that does not
waive privilege of another plaintiff who did not seek them. Nor is relevance of such an agreement
enough to override privilege. Nor does that change because one plaintiff (through poverty) drops
out, if it is possible that the same solicitor might continue to represent the remaining plaintiff. How
the plaintiff will pay his or her counsel is not really the defendant’s concern.³
Even if a contingent-fee agreement is unenforceable (for want of some of the mandatory
contents), the fairness of the fee to which the law ﬁrm is entitled involves looking at (inter alia)
the client’s reasonable expectations, and R.10.2 tells the review ofﬁcer to look at any retainer
agreement and all the surrounding circumstances, including work done and result achieved, and
anything else relevant. Reasonable expectations include what an agreement said.⁴
It was also
relevant that the client settled and took settlement proceeds, and then took an unsustainable legal
position contrary to the ﬁrm’s advice, and that made the fee charged reasonable.⁵
Void Provisions
10.6(1) In a retainer agreement or otherwise, a provision is void if the provision
(a) purports to relieve a lawyer from liability for negligence or any other
liability to which the lawyer might be subject as a lawyer, or
(b) purports to provide that an action, application or proceeding cannot
be abandoned, discontinued or settled without a lawyer’s consent.
(2) Despite any agreement to the contrary, a client may change the client’s lawyer
or act on the client’s own behalf before a retainer agreement ends.⁶
Information Note
The rules about self-representing are in rule 2.28 [Change in lawyer of record or self-
representation].

representation].

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.7(1)
Subdivision 3
Contingency Fee Agreements
Contingency Fee Agreement Requirements
10.7(1) A contingency fee agreement must
(a) be in writing, and
(b) be signed by the lawyer and the lawyer’s client or by their authorized
agents.¹
(2) To be enforceable, a contingency fee agreement must contain the following
particulars in precise and understandable terms:
(a) the name and address of each client;
(b) the name and address of the lawyer;
(c) a statement of the nature of the claim;
(d) a statement of the event or contingency on which the lawyer’s fees
are to be paid to the lawyer;
(e) a statement about
(i) the manner in which the contingency fee is to be calculated,
provided that no fee, calculated as a percentage or otherwise,
may be payable on disbursements or other charges recovered,
(ii) the maximum fee payable, or the maximum rate calculated,
detailing the disbursements and other charges that are
included in that fee or rate or indicating that disbursements
and other charges are in addition to that fee or rate, and
(iii) whether the client is responsible to pay disbursements and
other charges, and if so, a general description of the types of
disbursements and other charges likely to be incurred, other
than relatively minor disbursements;
(f) if the lawyer is to receive any amount from a costs award, a statement
that
(i) the cost award is intended to be a complete or partial
reimbursement of the lawyer’s charges to the client,
(ii) the cost award is owned by the client and that by signing
the contingency fee agreement the client is waiving the right
to any amount from the costs award that is payable to the
lawyer in accordance with subclause (iv),
(iii) the amount from the costs award retained by the lawyer will
be in addition to the lawyer’s percentage, ﬁxed fees or other
form of legal fees, and

Footnote
1 Quite similar to 1968 R.616.1968 R.616 was new in 1968. Heavily redrafted in 2000, with some substantive
changes.

10-16
R.10.7(3) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
(iv) the percentage of the costs award that the lawyer may
receive may not exceed the percentage of the judgment or
settlement that the lawyer is entitled to;
(g) a statement that, if the client gives notice in writing to the lawyer
within 5 days after the client’s copy of the contingency fee agreement
is served on the client, the client may terminate the contingency
fee agreement without incurring any liability for the lawyer’s fees,
but that the client is liable to reimburse the lawyer for reasonable
disbursements;
(h) a statement that
(i) at the request of the client, a review ofﬁcer may review either
or both of the contingency fee agreement and any lawyer’s
charges in an account rendered under the agreement, and
(ii) either or both of the contingency fee agreement or any
lawyer’s charges may be further reviewed by way of an
appeal from a review ofﬁcer’s decision to a judge.
(3) The contingency fee agreement must be witnessed by a person who sees
the client sign the agreement, and that person must then swear an afﬁdavit of
execution.
(4) The client must be served with a copy of the signed contingency fee
agreement within 10 days after the date on which the agreement is signed, and
an afﬁdavit of service to that effect must be executed by the person who served
the agreement.
(5) A client may terminate a contingency fee agreement without incurring any
liability for the lawyer’s fees under the agreement if the client, within 5 days after
service on the client of the copy of the contingency fee agreement, gives written
notice of the termination to the lawyer, but the client is liable to reimburse the
lawyer for reasonable disbursements incurred by the lawyer.
(6) If a contingency fee agreement provides that a lawyer is entitled to an amount
from a costs award, the lawyer is not entitled to receive from the costs award any
higher percentage of the judgment or settlement than the lawyer is entitled to
receive under the contingency fee agreement.
(7) Every account rendered under a contingency fee agreement must contain
a statement that at the client’s request a review ofﬁcer may determine both the
reasonableness of the account and the reasonableness of the contingency fee
agreement.
(8) An account that does not contain the statement required by subrule (7) is of
no effect unless the Court
(a) is satisﬁed
(i) that the omission of the statement was inadvertent, and
(ii) the client has not been misled or prejudiced,
and
(b) the Court waives the requirement for the statement.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;128/15]

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.7(8)
Information Note
The transitional provisions of these rules contain rules protecting former contingency fee
agreements entered into before these rules come into force. See Part 15 [Transitional
Provisions and Coming Into Force].

lawyer’s charges, review officer
Related Provisions
10.5 (retainer agreements) ; definition of “retainer agreement”; 15.5 (transitional rule)

A. Cross References
See also R.10.8 and commentary on it.
B. History
At common law, quite possibly an agreement could not make the lawyer’s payment depend on
results of litigation, but for years Alberta Rules have allowed such an agreement if the formalities
prescribed (referred to above) are observed.¹
C. Scope
The previous Rules contained a real deﬁnition of a contingency fee agreement, but the
deﬁnition in the 2010 Rules is superﬁcial and circular, evidently directed only to cross-reference
other Rules. This Rule has always been regarded as conﬁned to litigation retainers, given the
history of the Rule and the background of the common law or champerty. But one decision²
extends the Rule to a retainer on sale of land. Another decision reduced it on the ground that the
claim settlement with the government achieved was political, not lawyers' work!³
D. Form and Breaches
An agreement over legal fees need not be in writing unless it makes payment contingent on
results.⁴
If it is so contingent, it binds the client only if the formalities in R.10.7 are observed.⁵
Proof of timely service on the client is vital under R.10.7(4).⁶
And the notice on the bill of costs
about the right to review under R.10.7(7) is also important.⁷

10-18
R.10.7(8) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Minor non-compliance with R.10.7 is no bar to applying the contract.¹
The court can weigh the
effects of breaches, and forgive ones doing little or no harm.²
Even several technical breaches of
the Rules about conditional-fee agreements need not upset the agreement if the purpose of the
requirements in the Rules were met.³
In one case, lack of an afﬁdavit of service would have done
no harm.⁴
But in another case, giving no maximum fee, no description of how costs awards were
to be shared, and no afﬁdavit of execution, were too serious to overlook, so the agreement was
invalid.⁵
Or where it omitted the required provisions for excluding disbursements from fees (and
said the reverse), who owns party-party costs, if the fee is a complete payment of fees (partly),
how to terminate the agreement, and right to have the court review the fee (in the agreement or
the bill).⁶
A clause allowing the law ﬁrm in its discretion to move fees up or down depending on later
events, made it impossible for the client to know the maximum fee possibly chargeable, which
R.10.7(2) (e) requires, even if the client is fairly sophisticated, so the court would not forgive that
omission.⁷
If the agreement is silent about who keeps costs which the opponent is ordered to pay
this client, R.10.7(2) does not apply because it speaks only of cases where the lawyer is to receive
any amount from a costs award, so there could not here be any breach of that subrule.⁸
Rule
10.7(2) (h) (ii) calls for a statement of the right of appeal to a judge, and a mandatory arbitration
clause violates that, as it would probably bar an appeal; certainly the right to appeal was not
stated “in precise and understandable terms”.⁹
The subparas. of R.10.7(2) (g) about service and
right to cancel within a few days after service, and an afﬁdavit of execution, are important and the
three are interlinked. No service, no witness, no afﬁdavit of service, and not reciting the precise
terms of when the deal may be cancelled, were fatal.¹⁰
They were especially important as this client
and law ﬁrm had dealt with each other for years, but always on an hourly-rate basis, and the client
had to understand clearly how different was this conditional-fee agreement.¹¹
Subrules 10.7 (7), (8) require certain contents of an account from a law ﬁrm to its client, where
a conditional-fee agreement governs the fee. Not all were in this account, and a mere recital in
the account that it was pursuant to the Fee Agreement did not sufﬁce. There must be a speciﬁc
statement, not a mere reference back to the Fee Agreement. This was too important to forgive.¹²

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.7(8)
If the agreement is unenforceable, the court should be slow to award a fee about equal to
the amount which the agreement would have yielded, especially if minor children are among the
clients or beneﬁciaries.¹
E. Review
The court sets out a number of rules for a review ofﬁcer reviewing a contingency contract and
bill.²
The cases are reviewed in the Rusk decision.³
Even if the lawyer is the applicant before the review ofﬁcer or the court, the lawyer does not
have the onus of proving internal workings or procedures of the client organization to approve the
agreement, especially if there is no evidence that they were not followed, because only the client
knows that.⁴
Many proposed criticisms of a contingency agreement which have no legal force, because the
law does not impose any such duties, are reviewed.⁵
First the review ofﬁcer (or a judge) should interpret a retainer or a contingency fee agreement,
using ordinary principles of contractual interpretation.⁶
Only after that, should the ofﬁcer or judge
get to the question (if it arises) of whether a contingency fee agreement is unreasonable and so
should be varied.⁷
A retainer agreement is judged objectively, and as of the day it was signed, looking at the
interests and expectations of both parties. That includes their ﬁnancial circumstances, knowledge,
likely attitude of the opponent, likely duration of the tasks, risks and who pays disbursements.⁸
The size of the sum recovered is not the only test of quality of outcome; often counsel's
efﬁciency, and comparison with likely recovery, are more important.⁹
There is no rule of thumb
that a 20% contingency agreement cannot be too high; amount of recovery and work done are
also relevant.¹⁰
The review ofﬁcer should not assume that lawyers are unused to extensive negotiation, or that
smaller or rural law ﬁrms are, especially if neither side alleges that.¹¹
It overstates to say that the review ofﬁcer “owes deference” to the agreement?¹²

10-20
R.10.7(8) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
If a costs award makes any use of the winner’s expenses (e. g. x percent of them), that
incorporates into the award any contract about fees between the winner and his or her lawyer,
and the party paying costs has an interest in the fee agreement and a right to full disclosure of it.¹
F. End of Retainer
The lawyer's ceasing to act for good reason (e. g. client’s refusing to take advice) does not
affect reasonableness of fee or contingency agreement.²
G. What is Fairness
It is not helpful to speak of “heavy burdens” on the lawyer.³
The review ofﬁcer need not give effect to a contingency fee contract if it is found to be unfair.
The unfairness may come from how the contract was negotiated, or from the fact that when made
it set too high a rate of payment. That means either unfair at the time that it was made, looking only
at facts which were then known or should have been known, without hindsight; or unfair because
of later unexpected events.⁴
If the agreement was fair, it binds the review ofﬁcer.⁵
Though a contingency fee contract usually involves an imbalance of power and information,
the court should not set one aside without good cause; such contracts are valuable, and certainty
is necessary for both parties.⁶
It may be relevant that a complex, expensive suit may make a contingency agreement the only
way for a client who is not wealthy to bring this suit.⁷
Some criteria for evaluating fairness and reviewing a contingent fee are set out.⁸
Thirty to 40%
fee (depending on how far the suit progresses) is fair for high-risk suits like medical malpractice,
but not for ordinary motor-vehicle cases, especially if party-party costs are also to go to the
lawyer.⁹
Thirty-ﬁve percent was too high for a tobogganing accident settled early for $3 million.

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.8
25% is within the range usually negotiated in Alberta for contingency agreements.¹
The court gave
what it assessed at double the usual large hourly rate, but under half what the fee contracted for
would have been.²
Twenty-ﬁve percent was too high where a claim was already accepted and
the work was almost clear cut, and the risk minimal; $250 per hour was substituted.³
A judge
(reviewing the review ofﬁcer) cut the contracted 20% down to 5%, taking judicial notice that that is
what real estate agents charge to sell buildings.⁴
Such an agreement can provide for an escalating
percentage if the suit becomes longer and more contested.⁵
H. Miscellaneous
The client does not owe any implied duty of good faith to the lawyer, nor of settling the suit only
in a way which will protect the lawyer’s best interests.⁶
How to calculate a contingency fee where the recovery was not a lump sum, is an exercise in
mathematics and actuarial science, not arbitrary.⁷
Where the client is an infant, the court’s power to review comes from a number of sources.⁸
A client with experience of making land deals (a landman) has above-average capacity to
understand and negotiate legal agreements such as contingency fees.⁹
Only Alberta’s Class Actions Act requires advance approval of a contingency fee agreement.
It is proper to give notice to the Public Trustee if many or most of the people involved in the class
would not be sui juris. Notice ordinarily would not be given to the defendant.¹⁰
There is a lengthy
discussion of the criteria.¹¹
The law restricting champerty still exists, and so a solicitor cannot take an assignment of his
or her client’s cause of action, before judgment, even if the client has become insolvent well into
the lawsuit, nor can the solicitor ﬁnance the client’s lawsuit.¹²
Lawyer’s Non-compliance With Contingency Fee Agreement
10.8 If a lawyer does not comply with rule 10.7(1) to (4), (6) and (7) [Contingency
fee agreement requirements], the lawyer is, on successful accomplishment or
disposition of the subject-matter of the contingency fee agreement, entitled
only to lawyer’s charges determined in accordance with rule 10.2 [Payment for
lawyer’s services and contents of lawyer’s account] as if no contingency fee
agreement had been entered into.¹³

Related Provisions
10.18(4) (disallowance of contingency fee agreement)

See also R.10.2 and R.10.7and notes, about the results of a defect in a contingency agreement.
If the contingency fee contract is not enforceable, the court can still allow more than a simple
hourly rate, to reﬂect risks run by the lawyer.¹
An infant’s contingency fee contract violated the Rules, lacking a statement about taxable
costs or about a maximum, an afﬁdavit of execution, and also proof of service on the client. So
it was not enforceable under the new or former Rules and had to be ignored, and the default R.
10.2 applied.²
Subdivision 4
Right of Review
Reasonableness of Retainer Agreements and Charges Subject to Review
10.9 The reasonableness of a retainer agreement and the reasonableness of a
lawyer’s charges are subject to review by a review ofﬁcer in accordance with
these rules, despite any agreement to the contrary.³

Related Provisions
10.17(2) (no second review allowed) ; 10.19(2) (reasonableness determined at time of

signing); 10.24 (review in special circumstances)
On reviewing contingency agreements and who can get disclosure or participate in the review,
see R.10.7 supra.
For the history of court review of solicitors’ agreements, see the Steinke case.⁴
The power to review and adjust lawyers’ fees charged to their own clients may be inherent in
the superior courts. It is not conﬁned to litigation in King's Bench, and extends to services in other
courts (civil or criminal), such as Alberta Court of Justice, or the Federal Court, or before other
tribunals. It also covers all non-litigious services, such as drawing up wills or giving legal advice.
(There are special provisions for services to the estates of a deceased. )⁵
But in Alberta a review ofﬁcer reviews retainer agreements because of R.10.9, without resort
to the common law.⁶

10-23
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.9
A retainer agreement may not be applicable to legal work done outside of the scope set out
in the retainer. When there is no applicable written retainer, the Court will determine the scope of
the retainer governing the parties, and the terms of that agreement is largely a question of fact.¹
A client and lawyer can agree on almost any type of billing, both as to its basis and timing, and
if the client fully understands and there is no other pressure or misconduct, that contract binds
both sides.²
What the retainer agreement between client and solicitor says is only one factor to
consider when a review ofﬁcer judges whether the fee charged is proper.³
The client only need show that the agreement is unreasonable, not that it is clearly
unreasonable.⁴
Whether a contingency agreement is reasonable is assessed, based on fairness at the time
it was made,⁵
the risk of not being paid, the fairness of the agreement over time, the client’s
understanding, whether the parties acted reasonably, and the Rules of Court, such as nature,
importance and urgency, circumstances of the client, what fund it is to be paid out of, the
manner in which the services are to be performed, the skill, work and responsibility involved,
the client's circumstances and ﬁnancial circumstances,⁶
and any other relevant factor under R.
10.2.⁷
Reasonableness involves what the client knew when it contracted, including how quickly
funds from success would be available.⁸
But the agreement should not be simply displaced by
the factors in R.10.2.⁹
The court balances the desirability of upholding such agreements against
protecting the vulnerable.
Reasonableness of fee agreements generally and reasonableness of contingency agreements,
are not the same thing.¹⁰
A review ofﬁcer should hear and decide an application over the propriety and fairness and
amount of a lawyer’s bill, and not adjourn the matter until a negligence suit is decided, especially
if the law ﬁrm has little control over how quickly that suit moves, but the clients do. The review
ofﬁcer has expertise over fairness and reasonableness of fees, but not over negligence.¹¹
Here
the contingency fee agreement which contract was not disputed, and was clear, and the law ﬁrm
simply sought the percentages in the contract.
If the agreement is not enforceable, the agreement is not irrelevant, because it is part of
the client's reasonable expectations, and is a factor recited in R.10.2.¹²
That the client took an
unreasonable legal position contrary to the ﬁrm's advice, is also relevant.¹³

R.10.10(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Rule 10.9 only covers agreements between a lawyer and his or her client, not agreements
between two non-lawyers. The court can disallow an award based on the contract, e. g. for
unnecessary steps or extraordinary measures.¹
The steps claimed for must be necessary, in
the sense of increasing the likelihood of the lender's security being protected and bringing the
foreclosure action to an end.²
The review ofﬁcers can decide whether a retainer contract is reasonable, but they cannot rule
on its interpretation, nor any question about what are its terms?³
A party must put his or her best foot forward on a review by a review ofﬁcer of a lawyer’s bill.⁴
However, if a new issue arises or the retainer agreement is held unreasonable or improper, then
new evidence is needed and the Palmer tests for new evidence on appeal need not be met.⁵
Time Limitation on Reviewing Retainer Agreements and Charges
10.10(1) A retainer agreement may not be reviewed if 6 months has passed after
the date on which the retainer agreement terminated.
(2) A lawyer’s charges may not be reviewed, whether at the request of the lawyer
or the client, if one year has passed after the date on which the account was sent
to the client.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;140/13; 36/20]

Related Provisions
13.5 (extension of time)

Subrule 2 was amended in 2020 to extend the time limit from 6 months to one year.
This Rule sets ordinary time limits⁶
for a client (or other person liable to pay) seeking to review
the lawyer’s account which he or she owes. The time limits probably do not apply to interim

accounts, but what is an interim account is a matter of some debate in the many recent cases.¹

whether the statement of account was interim or ﬁnal,⁷

R.10.11 PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Extending time without notice is questionable.¹
The time limit applies only to review at the instance of the client, as otherwise a lawyer could
be time-barred without knowing that there is an issue.²
The time limit for review did not bar review
here because (at the clients' request) some of the services were rendered well after the invoice
for them! And R.10.24 is separate and more speciﬁc than R.10.10(2).³
The modern rules of interpretation of statutes apply also to the Rules of Court, and that
includes (though with less weight) their statement of their purpose, so long as speciﬁc operational
words are not distorted. Rules 1.2 and 1.7 were looked at, and applied to R.10.10 (time limit for
review of a legal account).⁴
What is subject to the deadline (a year plus 75 days if in 2020),⁵
despite some conﬂict in
authority, is to be interpreted purposively, contextually, and harmoniously with other Rules,
including R.10.13(4). So this deadline stops running when the Appointment is obtained, even if
it has not yet been served. Service within a reasonable time sufﬁces. If there is undue delay in
serving, that can be managed by the Review Ofﬁcer, who has many applicable powers.⁶
Like most time limits, breach of these ones must be somehow raised in a reasonable time, not
held silently until a late stage in the proceedings.⁷
Some objections to a lawyer’s account would be within the jurisdiction of a review ofﬁcer,
some for only a judge. If both kinds are present, an Applications Judge can order that the review
proceed ﬁrst (before a review ofﬁcer).⁸
See further on which topics a review ofﬁcer can and cannot
decide, notes to Rr.10.17 and 10.18.
This is a limitation period, even though in some circumstances it can be extended. So the
mandatory Ministerial Order made by the Minister of Justice applies and is mandatory. Because
of almost inaccessible courthouses, it extends time to commence proceedings by 75 days, from
March 17, 2020 to June 1, 2020, and those 75 days cannot be counted in calculating the time limit
for commencing (suing), and under this Rule the 75 days were not counted.⁹

Related Provisions
Definition of “client”

A lawyer can have the court review his or her own bill either to get execution (enforcement), or
to demonstrate that it is appropriate.²
A client is presumed liable to pay his or her lawyer (counsel or solicitor), even if someone else
(such as an insurer) has also agreed to do so. But the lay client is not the only one liable to pay.
A lawyer who retained another lawyer (as distant agent or as counsel) is also presumed liable to
pay.³
And often other persons contract to pay solicitor-client costs, e. g. mortgagors, or those who
agree to pay costs as part of a settlement. The expanded deﬁnition of “client” in the Appendix lets
them have the account reviewed in the same way that the client could, though thorny issues of
privilege may then arise.⁴
A bill to an executor by counsel hired by him was approved by the executor. A mere beneﬁciary
of the estate was unable to have the legal bill reviewed. Her right was to have the executor’s
accounts and fee taxed, not the counsel’s bill?⁵

R.10.12(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Because the injured plaintiff and his solicitor have a proprietary interest in the material which
they have generated and it costs money, the Minister of Hospitals with a subrogated claim should
reimburse them for a proportionate share of the out-of-pocket expenses paid to third parties and
incurred in obtaining material such as experts’ reports.¹
Review is a remedial order, and so it interrupts or restarts the limitation period for a lawyer to
sue his client for fees.²
Does the liberality of the review of fees depend on whether a non-client or a trust is to pay
them?³
The mere fact that there is usually privilege involved in communications between a lawyer and
his or her client does not bar the lawyer from having the account reviewed if the client does not
pay it. Nor does it bar such a review at the instance of the successor in title of the lawyer, such as
a legally appointed Custodian of his practice.⁴
In addition, reviews are usually held in the private
meeting room or ofﬁce of the reviewing ofﬁcer, and though the reviewing ofﬁcer may borrow the
ﬁle or papers of the lawyer, invariably the ofﬁcer returns them at the end, and never puts them on
the public court ﬁle.⁵
If a solicitor is an estate trustee and takes fees from it without prior court approval, a successor
trustee can have the court later review the fees and order part refunded.⁶
Location of Review
10.12(1) Where a lawyer carries on business in Alberta, the lawyer’s charges may
be reviewed by a review ofﬁcer at the judicial centre nearest to where the lawyer
carries on business.
(2) Where a lawyer does not carry on business in Alberta but the client resides in
Alberta, the lawyer’s charges may be reviewed by a review ofﬁcer at the judicial
centre nearest to where the client resides.
(3) Notwithstanding subrules (1) and (2), and in any other case, the Court may
designate a location for the review of the lawyer’s charges.⁷

both.

R.10.14(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Client-Obtained Appointment: Lawyer’s Responsibility
10.14(1) If a lawyer is served with notice of an appointment for a review of the
lawyer’s charges or retainer agreement, or both, the lawyer must ﬁle
(a) a copy of the account, appropriately signed, in respect of which the
client seeks a review,
(b) a copy of any time records upon which the account is based, and
(c) a copy of any retainer agreement between the lawyer and the client
whether or not the lawyer intends to rely on them.
(2) The documents must be ﬁled 5 days or more before the appointment date or
within any other period speciﬁed by a review ofﬁcer, and the review ofﬁcer may
vary the period before or after the time limit has passed.
(3) If the lawyer does not comply with this rule, the lawyer forfeits the right to
payment of the lawyer’s charges in any account that is the subject of a review
unless the review ofﬁcer otherwise directs.¹
[Alta. Reg.124/10;140/13;128/15;36/20]

Review Ofﬁcer’s Authority
10.17(1) For the purpose of conducting a review under this Division, a review
ofﬁcer may do all or any of the following:
(a) take evidence either by afﬁdavit or orally under oath, or both;
(b) direct the production of records;
(c) require notice of the appointment for the review to be served on
persons who may be affected by the review or who have an interest
in the trust, estate, fund or property from which the lawyer’s charges
are or may be paid or charged;
(d) give directions about how notice of the appointment for the review is
to be served;
(e) allow a party to be represented by a lawyer;
(f) require details of a retainer agreement, or of the services provided,
money collected and expended, disbursements or other charges
claimed or any other matter necessary to understand the agreement
or charges and decide whether the agreement or charges, or both,
are reasonable;
(g) validate service of the notice of the appointment or, if service is
impractical or impossible, dispense with service.²
(h) determine the applicability of a time period specified in these rules
in respect of a review conducted under this Division and extend or
shorten an applicable time period.

Paragraph 1(h) was added in 2020.
The review is based in part on the ofﬁcer’s expertise.²
On criteria to review a retainer agreement, see R.10.7 nn. E, G, and R.10.9 n.
A review ofﬁcer cannot hear any question about who is liable to pay fees, but he or she can ﬁx
the amount despite such a dispute.³
A review ofﬁcer should hear and decide an application over the propriety and fairness and
amount of a lawyer’s bill, and not adjourn the matter until a negligence suit is decided. The review
ofﬁcer has expertise over fairness and reasonableness of fees, but not over negligence. Here
there was a contingency fee agreement which contract was not disputed, and was clear, and the
law ﬁrm simply sought the percentages in the contract.⁴
Many aspects of the client’s aims, instructions, behavior, communications, and cooperation
with his or her lawyer, are relevant when assessing the proper size of the lawyer’s bill.⁵
An Applications Judge cannot assess the reasonableness of fees or the work done to earn
them, even on a foreclosure suit.⁶
Especially if there is a contract to pay the plaintiff’s legal bills.
The correct procedure is for the review ofﬁcer to review the fees.⁷
Evidence heard by the review ofﬁcer need not be sworn.⁸
The lawyer can give evidence of his
or her own experience, and need not call an independent expert witness.⁹
The review ofﬁcer should not make assumptions about a law ﬁrm’s ability or experience.¹⁰

Reference to Court
10.18(1) A review ofﬁcer
(a) must refer any question arising about the terms of a retainer
agreement to the Court for a decision or direction, and
(b) may refer any question arising about a lawyer’s charges to the Court
for a decision or direction.
(2) The review ofﬁcer may do all or any of the following:
(a) require one party to serve another party or other interested person
with notice of the reference;
(b) specify how a reference to the Court is to be prepared and by whom;
(c) prescribe time limits;
(d) specify any other matter for the effective and efﬁcient disposition of
the reference.
(3) On considering a question referred to it, the Court may make any order it
considers appropriate in the circumstances, including
(a) an order to enforce a direction given under rule 10.17 [Review
officer’s authority], or
(b) with respect to a review of the terms of a retainer agreement, an order
approving the agreement or varying or disallowing the agreement in
whole or in part.
(4) If a contingency fee agreement is disallowed, the amount payable to the
lawyer for the lawyer’s charges must be determined under rule 10.2 [Payment for
lawyer’s services and contents of a lawyer’s account].

10-34
R.10.19(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
The cases once were divided on whether the review ofﬁcer can decide whether there was a
retainer or liability, or whether there must be a suit or trial of an issue before a judge to decide
that.¹
But in Alberta now the latter view seems to prevail because of R.10.18.²
At one time reviews
were by Clerks without legal training, but now when legally-trained ofﬁcers perform them, whether
the lawyer who rendered the account was negligent is a proper topic for the review.³
If a judge
considers whether a solicitor was negligent, that is not a collateral attack on a review ofﬁcer’s
decision earlier, if the ofﬁcer could not and did not rule on the negligence question.⁴
The terms of the retainer agreement cannot be decided by the review ofﬁcer, and that includes
questions of interpretation, and includes implied retainer agreements or oral agreements.⁵
A Review Ofﬁcer hearing a dispute about a legal bill has power to take evidence, even unsworn
evidence. An allegation that the retainer (for an hourly charge) had been suspended and that
further work was free, is a dispute about amount, and does not need a reference to the court
under this Rule.⁶
Review Ofﬁcer’s Decision
10.19(1) A review of a lawyer’s charges must take into consideration the factors
described in rule 10.2 [Payment for lawyer’s services and contents of lawyer’s
account], except to the extent that a retainer agreement otherwise provides.
(2) A review of a retainer agreement must be based on the circumstances that
existed when the retainer agreement was entered into.
(3) The review ofﬁcer may, in a review of a lawyer’s charges, allow the charges, or
vary, reduce or disallow all or any of the charges and certify the amount payable
for and against each party, and may issue an interim certiﬁcate.
(4) A review ofﬁcer’s decision must be given by an interim or ﬁnal certiﬁcate,
which may be endorsed on a copy of the lawyer’s account, and the certiﬁcate
must
(a) certify the amount to be paid by each party or person,
(b) certify any special circumstance and the amount to be paid by each
party or person with respect to the special circumstance, and
(c) be dated and signed by the review ofﬁcer.
(5) An interim or ﬁnal certiﬁcate of a review ofﬁcer that meets the requirements
set out in subrule (4) is conclusive proof of the amount that a party or person
who had notice of the review must pay.

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.20(1)

Related Provisions
10.9 (contingency fee agreement reviewable for reasonableness).

See also R.10.9 n.
The appellant had argued for a decision on whether the Review Ofﬁcer found the 10 lawyers’
periodic bills being reviewed were interim and to be reviewed as a whole at the end, or each
ﬁnal and to be reviewed individually. The parties disagreed on that topic. The Review Ofﬁcer
never decided that basic question, and his view is unclear, which omission was a clear error of
law.¹
It is vital to determine that question for two reasons: to know how to assess each bill, and
to see whether any bills were being reviewed outside the one-year limitation (and most of these
bills were reviewed more than a year after their receipt under R.10.10(2) ).²
So the decision was
quashed and a new review by a different Review Ofﬁcer was ordered.³
If a review ofﬁcer ﬁnds the retainer agreement unfair, or if a King's Bench judge or the Court
of Appeal ﬁnds the decision under appeal unreasonable, then that higher ofﬁcer or judge must
analyze the fee afresh, and set the fee on any reasonable basis, not necessarily a percentage of
recovery.⁴
If a lawyer collects fees from a retainer and then his or her account is reduced on review, that is
res judicata and a court order. The same is true if the account is reduced on appeal from review.⁵
A duty to refund is implicit when a paid lawyer’s account is assessed down.⁶
One common-law spouse was in privity with the other and bound by adverse court rulings
against the other, though not named in the style of cause.⁷
On negligence by the lawyer, see R.10.1 and commentary, supra.
Because of Rr.10.2 and 10.19, even an unenforceable contingent-fee agreement has some
(limited) relevance.⁸
Enforcement of Review Ofﬁcer’s Decision
10.20(1) The Court, on application by a party with notice to the other party, may
direct a decision of a review ofﬁcer to be entered as a judgment or order.
(2) No direction may be made under subrule (1) before the time for an appeal of
the review ofﬁcer’s decision has expired or, if the decision is being appealed,
before a decision is made under rule 10.27 [Decision of the judge].
[Alta. Reg.124/10;36/20]

10-37
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.23
Information Note
This rule is an exception to
- the general rule that judgment may be entered against a defendant who does not file
a statement of defence or demand for notice (see rule 3.36 [Judgment in default of
defence and noting in default], and
- the general rule that a successful party is entitled to a costs award.
A lawyer who obtains a default judgment gets costs only if the Court so orders.

Related Provisions
10.20 (enforcement of review officer’s decision) ; 10.29 (costs follow event)

Public policy is so tender to the interests of clients, that this Rule bars default judgment or
costs of suit in a suit on legal fees (unless there is leave of the court), when the plaintiff is the
lawyer or a lawyer’s assignee. The court often orders review of the account,¹
but that makes little
sense if the amount of the account seems reasonable, especially as an evasive client will later
deny the retainer, or claim that he has paid, neither of which the review ofﬁcer can deal with. The
lawyer should note the defendant in default, and then apply in chambers as though for summary
judgment, with personal service again. Review should be ordered only if the defendant or the
material from the plaintiff shows some question as to quantum.
Costs of Review
10.23 A review ofﬁcer may allow or disallow the reasonable and proper costs of
a review and ﬁx the amount but may not make a costs award
(a) against the client as a result of a client–requested review unless the
client’s request was unreasonable or the client acts improperly or
unreasonably at the review, or
(b) against the client as a result of a lawyer–requested review unless the
client acts improperly or unreasonably at the review and the Court
approves the costs award.²

Where a lawyer got only a small fraction of the fees he claimed, he got no costs and had to
pay costs.³

10.24(1) If any of the following events occurs, a lawyer, the lawyer’s personal

(f) the client unreasonably discontinues or abandons any matter to
which a contingency fee agreement applies;
(g) any other event that creates uncertainty about a lawyer’s charges or
who is to pay them or to whom they are to be paid.¹
(2) When making a decision under this rule, the review ofﬁcer must take into
consideration the factors described in rule 10.2 [Payment for lawyer’s services
and contents of lawyers account], except to the extent that a retainer agreement
otherwise provides.

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.26(1)
There is a discussion of whether the client or the lawyer owns any given papers on the ﬁle of
that lawyer. That the paper in question was created by work for which that solicitor charged that
client is not conclusive. References to internal memos and communications should not be read
literally, and should be conﬁned to topics administrative or for the sole assistance of the law ﬁrm.
Many papers will have been created for more than one purpose, and then one should see which
was the dominant purpose. Ownership between principal and agent is not the test. There are few
bright lines.¹
Subdivision 5
Appeal from Review Ofﬁcer’s Decision
Appeal to Judge
10.26(1) A party to a review ofﬁcer’s decision under this Division may appeal the
decision to a judge.
(2) The appeal from a review ofﬁcer’s decision is an appeal on the record of
proceedings before the review ofﬁcer.
(3) The record of proceedings is
(a) Form 42 served under rule 10.13(2),
(b) the material the parties ﬁled to support or oppose, or that was
required for, the review,
(c) the transcript of the proceedings before the review ofﬁcer, unless
the judge waives this requirement, and
(d) the review ofﬁcer’s certiﬁcate.
(4) The appellant must ﬁle and serve on the respondent to the appeal, within one
month after the date of the review ofﬁcer’s decision,
(a) notice of the appeal in Form 43 including the date, time and place of
the hearing,
(b) the record of proceedings described in subrule (3) or, if the transcript
is not available at the time of ﬁling, conﬁrmation that the transcript
of the proceedings has been ordered, and
(c) any further written argument.
(5) The respondent to the appeal must, within 10 days after service of the notice
of appeal, ﬁle and serve on the appellant any written argument the respondent
wishes to make.

10-40
R.10.26(5) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
The time limits in R.10.26(4) should be watched carefully. They could be extended under R.
13.5, but one would have to explain the delay and maybe show merit to the appeal.¹
Extending
the time to review is discussed in the Samson Cree case.²
Would those criteria apply by analogy
here? Or would some of them?
Such appeals can be difﬁcult for several other reasons:
1. They are conﬁned to the evidence before the review ofﬁcer,³
but parties rarely think to ask
to have the evidence before that ofﬁcer recorded or kept.⁴
Some review ofﬁcers used not
to allow a court reporter to attend (which is a fundamental error and injustice). The ofﬁcer
probably records on the "FTR" system now, but often the paper record is deﬁcient, and
a lot of oral communications or even credibility are involved. Unless the chambers judge
wants to conduct a long hearing de novo,⁵
an appeal may be hopeless without a proper
record. If for the ﬁrst time, on appeal the judge or judges ﬁnds the retainer unreasonable,
then for the ﬁrst time more evidence becomes relevant and need not meet the Palmer
test for new evidence on appeal.⁶
What salaried lawyers or judges earn has little weight,
as they are paid promptly and without exception and so are risk free. Nor are contingent
real estate commissions relevant, nor income taxes.⁷
If the retainer agreement is held
unenforceable on appeal, the appeal court can ﬁx the fees on any reasonable basis, not
merely the one in the agreement.⁸
2. The courts will not change the result of a review without proof of some error in law
or principle affecting the result, or a result which is clearly outside the proper range.⁹
That is partly because the ofﬁcer has specialized knowledge and experience, and partly
because he or she gets the evidence ﬁrst hand.¹⁰
On deference by a judge on appeal, see

10-41
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.27(1)
further the Court of Appeal in the Betzer case.¹
Deciding a topic which he had no power
to decide, would be error in principle by the review ofﬁcer.²
Examples of reviewable error
by the ofﬁcer are given.³
Strictly speaking, the size of a proper fee is a question of weight,
not of discretion.⁴
But interpretation of a fee contract is a question of law decided on a
correctness standard, and there is no question then of deferring to the review ofﬁcer’s fact
ﬁndings.⁵
Leave to appeal from an assessment to a judge was refused, as the appeal had little chance
of success, given the standard of review.⁶
Where an assessment ofﬁcer feels unable to deal with the amount of fees, because of serious
issues of fact and law about the contract between the parties, or lack of a contract, no leave is
necessary to appeal to the Court of Appeal from a chambers judge’s appeal from the assessment
ofﬁcer.⁷
A local registrar or taxing ofﬁcer taxing a lawyer’s account to his or her client looks at what the
work done was worth and what agreements, arrangements, estimates, or legislation governed
such amounts. It is about quantum, not about whether there was any duty to pay fees at all.⁸
To try
to appeal on the grounds of no liability to pay at all is an attempted collateral attack on a decision
to refer to the local registrar or taxing ofﬁcer.⁹
An appeal about a dispute between a lawyer and his client over the lawyer’s fees is probably
not “as to costs only” requiring leave to appeal to the Court of Appeal. “Costs” normally refers
to payments between opposing parties. And review of a lawyer’s contingency fee agreement is
deﬁnitely not as to costs alone, as it raises other issues of principle, and no previous decision
holds that that is as to “costs alone”. So leave is not needed.¹⁰
A decision of a Review Ofﬁcer may be upset and a new review ordered, if the Ofﬁcer failed to
decide a basic topic, such as whether the legal accounts being reviewed were interim or ﬁnal.¹¹
Decision of Judge
10.27(1) After hearing an appeal from a review ofﬁcer’s decision, the judge may,
by order, do one or more of the following orders:
(a) conﬁrm, vary or revoke the decision;
(b) revoke the decision and substitute a decision;
(c) revoke all or part of the decision and refer the matter back to the
review ofﬁcer or to another review ofﬁcer;
(d) make any other order the judge considers appropriate.

R.10.27(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
(2) If the amount of lawyer’s charges payable pursuant to the decision of the

Related Provisions
10.44 (appeal from assessment officer)

Strong deference is owed by a judge to a review ofﬁcer’s assessment (taxation) of a solicitor’s
bill to the client.²
A solicitor’s account was reviewed, but the client disputed who was to pay, a question which
the review ofﬁcer could not decide. The chambers judge should not have upset the review. The
certiﬁcate of review was restored, and the lawyer could move for judgment, and the client could
(with evidence) dispute that on any ground other than quantum or alleged negligence or worth
(which the taxation made res judicata).³
A judge who reversed the review ofﬁcer's decision on reasonableness of size of a 20%
contingency fee refused to send the matter back to the ofﬁcer to rehear, and substituted his own
view, including some judicial notice of business practices, and substituted 5%.⁴
If the Review Ofﬁcer failed to decide a basic factual topic, the decision will be upset on appeal
and a new hearing by a different ofﬁcer ordered.⁵
But the odd accusation of “appeal proofing” and
“pre-empting an appeal” (¶’s 10.26) may be questionable. Trial judges often decide one issue,
which ends the matter, but then decide another issue raised before them in case they are wrong
about the first issue. That obviates a need for a new hearing and so is often recommended, but it
in no way impairs an appeal.

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.29(1)
Information Note
Party is defined in the Appendix [Definitions] as a party to an action. There are other Court
proceedings that are not “actions” and so the definition of party is expanded to allow a costs
award against anyone participating in an application or proceeding that is not an action
started by statement of claim or originating application. `

Related Provisions
Appendix - “party”; 10.50 (costs payable by lawyer)

The court can make a costs order against a non-party who ﬁled a document or participated in
an application, or opposed an application made by the court on its own motion,¹
even if the court
invited certain persons thus to join in.²
An insurer was the real instigator of the litigation and the real one who would have beneﬁtted,
and had been told that it would have to pay costs. Costs were ordered against it when its side lost.³
Even where there are exceptional circumstances (no one to argue the other side), if a tribunal
argues its powers or the merits properly before the Court of Appeal, lack of misconduct does not
immunize it from paying costs if it loses.⁴
General Rule For Payment of Litigation Costs
10.29(1) A successful party to an application, a proceeding or an action is
entitled to a costs award against the unsuccessful party, and the unsuccessful
party must pay the costs forthwith, notwithstanding the ﬁnal determination of
the application, proceeding or action, subject to

award],
(b) the assessment ofﬁcer’s discretion under rule 10.41 [Assessment
officer’s decision],
(c) particular rules governing who is to pay costs in particular
circumstances,
(d) an enactment governing who is to pay costs in particular
circumstances, and
(e) subrule (2).
(2) If an application or proceeding is heard without notice to a party, the Court
may
(a) make a costs award with respect to the application or proceeding, or

A. Basic Approach¹
1. Little Restraint on Justices or Applications Judges
These 2010 Rules do not change the principles of costs, but give more detail about what
factors must be considered. The general costs principles remain and are much the same under
the 1968 and 2010 Rules.²
The general principles of costs apply in matrimonial property, support, and custody cases,
including the law on offers and misconduct.³
On costs in family litigation, see R.12.38n.⁴
Here is the key to understanding costs between the parties to a suit. The Rules of Court put
almost no restriction on the power of a justice or Applications Judge to decide who pays costs,
who gets costs, and how much the costs will be. Rule 4.29 is almost the only express Rule limiting
that.⁵
Rule 10.29(1) (a) makes express the general discretion over costs.⁶
There is more than one
way for a judge to reach a reasonable amount for costs.⁷
Rule 10.33’s factors also sometimes
apply to who will get costs and when.⁸
Many other Rules and tariffs at ﬁrst glance appear to set costs, but they do not bind justices
or Applications Judges; they only bind the assessment ofﬁcer.⁹
Those Rules or tariffs only apply
where the justice or Applications Judge setting costs has been silent on a topic. In particular,

(2d ed.2010) ; Alberta Justice, Court Services, Court of Queen’s Bench Costs Manual: Costs Between
Parties (2010) ; Orkin, The Law of Costs (2d ed. Can. L. B. looseleaf). Many recent authorities are summarized
in A. G. S v. R. N. S.2024 ABKB 280, JCC 4801 172541 (May 16) (its earlier parts).

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.29(2)
Schedule C sets no limit. The assessment ofﬁcer can sometimes award lower amounts,¹
and a
justice or Applications Judge need not use Schedule C at all, and can award higher amounts, or
award costs on a totally different basis.²
There is no presumption that a judge must use Schedule
C or any other Rule. It is not necessary to show exceptional circumstances to persuade a
justice not to use them, because they are addressed to the assessment ofﬁcer, not to justices or
Applications Judges.³
See further, R.10.31 n.
Using the court’s inherent jurisdiction is dangerous, and must be done sparingly, even in
reviewing bills of costs.⁴
Especially if the legislation or Rules cover the ﬁeld or have an inconsistent
scheme or policy.⁵
After a full trial, the trial judge is intimately familiar with the case, and the trial judge should set
the size or level of costs (such as which column of Sched. C), not an assessment ofﬁcer.⁶
On appeal, the Court of Appeal is slow to interfere with the discretion of the judge under appeal
as to costs.⁷
But it can and will change costs awards for failing to apply all the right principles.⁸
Costs are decided by the trial judge, not the jury.
2. Effect of Success
There are some limits on the discretion of a judge to award costs or not award them, but most⁹
are judge-made, and not expressed in the Rules of Court:
(a) The discretion must be exercised judicially, and in line with the factors in R.10.33.¹⁰

some of those in R.10.29.¹
Costs awards must be based on matters relevant in law, under
the Rules in Part 10, including R.10.33 not mere whim, dislike, sympathy, nor personal
characteristics,²
such as whether a party is rich or poor.³
Impecuniosity is irrelevant to liability for costs.⁴
Costs can produce hardship, at times severe.
But that has never been a reason to order civil costs or to limit costs to what the payor can afford.⁵
Ease of access to justice is not a ground to refuse to make the losing party pay costs. All the aims
of civil procedure law and the Rules of Court point in the opposite direction to that. Costs are
designed to promote settlement, deter frivolous actions and defences, deter unnecessary steps,
and sanction those who reject a reasonable settlement offer, as the Supreme Court of Canada
has held. That applies equally to plaintiffs and to defendants.⁶

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.29(2)
As a general rule, a successful party cannot be denied costs, much less be ordered to pay
costs, unless there are exceptional circumstances.¹
And novelty of the failing point is no ground to
excuse the losing party from paying costs.²
(b) A party who has been guilty of no misconduct of any kind in either causing the suit or in
running it, and has ﬁnally³
won on the merits, cannot be made to pay costs to the losing
side. He or she should be awarded costs, unless there are special relevant circumstances.⁴
That the losing party acted reasonably is no ground to deprive the winners of their costs.
That is the situation in most lawsuits which are contested.⁵
3. Aims of Costs
Costs exist for indemnity, discouraging frivolous positions, to sanction bad behavior, and to
encourage settlement.⁶
The principal aim is ordinarily indemnity.⁷
Especially at or near the end of
the suit.⁸
See further R.10.31n. B.

10-48
R.10.29(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
One purpose of costs is indemniﬁcation, which is more important at the end of litigation than
at interlocutory stages.40-50% indemniﬁcation is reasonable, but it can be higher or lower in light
of how the litigation was conducted, reasonableness of the fees incurred, and other things.¹
Many reported cases on costs levels and Schedule C, are about litigants with little experience
with litigation, and limited funds. Where the litigants are sophisticated and well funded, the stakes
in the suit are high, and the parties expect large legal bills, they are capable of assessing the
risks and potential net recoveries, and preventing unnecessary legal bills. Access to justice then
is not a factor to weigh. Industry needs courts which are responsive, effective, and as efﬁcient, as
non-judicial dispute resolution. In such cases costs awarded should be meaningful to the winning
party, and commensurate with the value of the claims litigated.²
4. Miscellaneous
The ordinary costs principles apply in family law matters.³
And the ordinary principles apply to
applications for interlocutory injunctions.⁴
At common law, the Crown neither paid nor recovered costs, which is still the rule in indictable
criminal matters, though it is different if the Crown is appealing over costs.⁵
But in civil cases,
legislation says to treat the Crown like any other party for costs.⁶
Counsel for a child appointed by Legal Aid pursuant to a judge’s order is not a respondent to
an appeal, even if the order said she was to be treated by other parties (as would any other party).
On appeal, the child was an interested party, much like an intervener. The order said the child’s
counsel could seek costs, but that did not mean she was entitled to receive costs. And usually the
child does not pay for the child’s counsel. Arrangements to share such expenses should not be
lightly interfered with. No costs were awarded to child’s counsel here.⁷
Costs may be authorized by a statute, or by a Regulation.⁸
On whether costs are payable in
statutory proceedings in Alberta Court of Justice and on appeal to the Court of King's Bench, see
two child welfare cases.⁹
A trial award of punitive damages is relevant when awarding costs, but the damage award
need not bar higher costs or even full indemnity costs, and the issue is largely proportionality.¹⁰
The Schedule C costs for applications can be increased by using items 19 and 20 of Schedule
C (for Court of Appeal costs), but generally only when the application is unusually lengthy, or the
underlying record or legal issues are unusually complex or costly.¹¹
