10-49
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.29(2)
Rule 10.29 is only about entitlement to costs, not about their scale.¹
B. Interlocutory Costs
1. Pay as You Go
(a) General Rule
A respondent should not be subjected to continuous litigation with no prospect of recovering
costs. Costs are an important control on quantity and intensity of litigation. A judgment-proof
litigant is free from such controls. Giving a litigant a chance for his day in court is important, but
less so if he has had repeated chances to pursue his claim, and all failed.²
That the losing party ﬁled an appeal is no reason to defer deciding on costs.³
Rule 10.29 provides that a successful party to an application is entitled to costs payable
forthwith.⁴
This Rule sets the default mode for interlocutory costs;⁵
if nothing is said by the justice
or Applications Judge, they now go to the winner of the application.⁶
Success in getting an
interlocutory injunction is a real victory and not merely a way station, and entitles the plaintiff to
costs.⁷
Ordinarily, it is more equitable to award costs to the winner of an interlocutory motion; this
is a healthy discipline for the party who loses a motion (or may lose a pending motion).⁸
But this is
subject to subpart B.4 below. If the result at trial cannot change the result of the application, costs
in any event are proper.⁹
There is no reason to depart from the default mode in this Rule, nor to
delay granting costs of a decided application, where most of the issues decided on the motion will
not be revisited.¹⁰
A winning party should get paid costs forthwith; the onus is on the losing party to show why
not.¹¹
It takes some solid reason to depart from payment forthwith; costs do not require frivolity

10-50
R.10.29(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
or weakness of the application.¹
Pay-as-you-go has more advantages than disadvantages, and a
committee studied the topic before the recent predecessor Rule was revised. Any overpayment
can always be redressed at the end of the suit.²
“Pay as you go” is now a much more common
practice.³
The Limitations Act does not set a deadline for seeking costs. Costs are indemniﬁcation, not a
requirement to comply with a duty or to pay damages for violation of a right (which is the deﬁnition
of a “remedial order” under that Act).⁴
(b) Decisions Which Will Not Change
It is customary to award costs in any event to the winner of an application brought because
a party improperly refused to answer a question on discovery or to produce a document. The
application would never have arisen without the refusal, and on that topic, one party must be right
and the other wrong.⁵
The later result of the suit will not change that. Procedural defaults before
trial are a proper ground for costs in any event and payable forthwith. The fact that the party guilty
of the procedural defaults later wins the trial and is absolved of any misconduct in the underlying
facts giving rise to the litigation is irrelevant, and not a ground to upset the earlier costs order.⁶
Where a party refuses a reasonable request or application, causing an unnecessary hearing, it
may well have to pay costs in any event.⁷
Costs should be awarded to the winner of an appeal of
an interlocutory application where the issues in it are independent and separate from the ultimate
question of the lawsuit.⁸
Even in family litigation, ultimate success does not mean that that winning
party had an excuse for resisting early interim motions by the opponent.⁹
(c) Costs in the Cause
Costs in the cause are often a very bad idea.¹⁰
“Costs in the cause” means that costs of the
motion automatically go to the winner of the suit, and there is a 50% chance that that will be

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.29(2)
the party who was wrong in the interlocutory ﬁght. If the real ﬁght in the main suit is quantum,
not liability, then the plaintiff is almost certain to get costs of the suit. That would be most unfair
treatment for interlocutory costs. The fact that the party whose procedure was wrong later wins
the suit, does not usually change that.¹
Costs in the cause might be fair where no one was guilty of
misconduct, and the interlocutory step was really necessitated by the mere existence of the suit.²
But they are unfair where one party was clearly wrong in the interlocutory step.³
(d) Payment Only Deferred
Occasionally interlocutory costs are awarded in any event, but payable at the end of the suit.⁴
The court can postpone the date to pay costs even if they are unconditionally awarded, and often
does in interlocutory motions.⁵
(e) Miscellaneous
Any presumption that the winner of an application should get costs no matter who later wins
the suit, would be awkward where the application is ex parte,⁶
or by consent.⁷
Where leave to appeal is granted despite active opposition by the respondent, immediate
costs to the appellant of the application may be appropriate. A number of the tests for leave have
nothing to do with the merits, and so will not come up on the main appeal. A respondent is not
obliged to oppose the leave application.⁸
Schedule C is most useful in interlocutory stages where a quick costs award is needed.⁹
2. Postponing Costs Decision
See R.10.30. If neither the parties nor the court addressed the issue of costs when deciding
an important interlocutory order, the Court has jurisdiction to give a later direction about those
costs.¹⁰
But waiting to ask for costs until 11 years of litigation had elapsed in a family law suit is too
long and suggests no costs would be sought, not that they would be long deferred.¹¹

10-52
R.10.29(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Occasionally one cannot tell who was right or wrong in the application without trying some
fact question which will be tried at trial.¹
Then the proper remedy is not “costs in the cause”, for
reasons given above in subpart 1. The remedy is to order that the trial judge will decide who will
get costs of the application.
Where an application for adjournment of an application is unsuccessfully opposed by the
plaintiff, at the end of the suit it is doubtful that the court has any power to give the plaintiff costs
of the adjournment application, given R.10.29(1).²
To determine who should get costs of interlocutory applications which had been decided
pending the main hearing, at the end of the suit the court should be able to rely upon the face of
the orders and written applications, and if that does not make it apparent who won the application,
the party seeking costs of that application may not get them. Rigorous conditions put on the
order which was sought may indicate that results of the application were mixed, and so each side
should bear its own costs of the application.³
Or where applications are housekeeping leading
onto hearing the case, if the party with carriage wins the case, he may get a modest fee for them.⁴
3. Mixed Issues
On a single application, if one party succeeds it should ordinarily get costs; the court should
not split that by different issues on the application, nor try to apportion costs.⁵
Whether in the end
result the application should or should not have been brought is the key question, not reasoning
at intermediate steps.
Where success on an interlocutory application is divided, it is very common to give one party
reduced costs.⁶
Or if each side's costs would be similar, to make no award and order that each
party bear its own costs.⁷
Where success is divided and the application was unnecessary and
victory for either side would have helped it little and loss would have harmed it little, neither side
recovered costs.⁸
But success on appeal is usually calculated globally, and not issue by issue; a
winner often does not get quite all that it claims.⁹
It takes very unusual circumstances to waive costs just because the issues were novel.¹⁰
If some issues are of public interest, and others were personal interests, the winner’s costs
may be limited to steps directly related to the precise personal interest.¹¹
4. Indulgences and Neglect
If a party succeeds on an application, but the application is necessary because of his or her
earlier carelessness, often it is proper to make that party pay costs of the application, not receive
them.¹²
Examples are found throughout this book.

Heavy costs were granted for 20 post-judgment repetitive applications and attendances before
the court because of the solvent defendant’s attempts to avoid paying the judgment (despite
the plaintiff’s offer to hold the funds pending appeal).¹
Two applications instead of one, and two
successive afﬁdavits, necessitating two reply afﬁdavits, led to extra costs.²
Causing an adjournment may be grounds for liability to pay costs of the adjournment,³
but
not grounds for higher costs of the motion when it is later decided on the merits with no (more)

5. Miscellaneous
Where what the two sides demanded was more or less compatible, to award costs at the end
the chambers justice looked at whose offer had been more reasonable.⁵
Usually costs of an application and a cross-application heard at the same time are dealt with
as a single appearance and a single assessable set or item of costs.⁶
“Costs of the application” in an order may in context well refer not merely to arguing, but also
to ancillary steps such as cross-examining on afﬁdavits.⁷
Settlement does not (without express mention) affect the right to costs of past decided
applications.⁸
If a settlement is silent as to costs and deemed effective before some interlocutory
steps, the court can award no costs for those steps unless the parties agree on those costs.⁹
The mere fact that a party abandoned his application should not make his costs payable larger
than had he lost it.¹⁰
On what are thrown-away costs, see R.10.31n.¹¹
On how to calculate the column for interlocutory costs in any event, see Schedule C.¹²
Even
if strictly speaking the chambers proceedings in question dealt with non-monetary relief, the
chambers judge may base the costs on the amount in issue in the underlying arbitration.¹³

R.10.30(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
10-54
Where a party insists on carrying on litigation which does not have any evident foundation, but
he or she is short of funds, the appropriate interlocutory costs award against her may be a charge
on another estate in which she has an interest.¹
The winner of a motion for summary judgment got
no costs, because his afﬁdavit was ﬁled late.²
An urgent application for an injunction drew costs of $2, 000.³
The Court has discretion to award costs of case management meetings, even if no notice of
motion or afﬁdavit preceded them.⁴
But there are to be no costs for alternate dispute resolution or
judicial dispute resolution.⁵
At the end of a trial, the winning party’s disbursements for experts may not all be recoverable
as costs if part of that work was for interlocutory applications lost by that party.⁶
At the end of the
suit, the host of applications which the losing party had brought, and the fact that he had been a
moving target, were relevant to the scale or level of ﬁnal costs.⁷
A court is unlikely to refuse to accept a written brief of law ﬁled by a party, but if the judge does
not seek it and the Rules and practice of the court do not call for it, the author of it is unlikely to
get costs. There are other ways of warning the other side of what law will be relied on, without a
full brief.⁸
A decision about costs made before they are sought, cannot preclude an objection to the costs
later sought.⁹
When Costs Award May be Made
10.30(1) Unless the Court otherwise orders or these rules otherwise provide, a
costs award may be made
(a) in respect of an application or proceeding of which a party had
notice, after the application has been decided,
(b) in respect of a settlement of an action, application or proceeding, or
any part of any of them, in which it is agreed that one party will pay
costs without determining the amount, and
(c) in respect of trials and all other matters in an action, after judgment
or a ﬁnal order has been entered.¹⁰
(2) If the Court does not make a costs award or an order for an assessment ofﬁcer
to assess the costs payable when an application or proceeding is decided or
when judgment is pronounced or a ﬁnal order is made, either party may request
from an assessment ofﬁcer an appointment date for an assessment of costs
under rule 10.37 [Appointment for assessment].

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.30(2)

Related Provisions
9.14 (further orders after judgment) ; 13.1 (any judge can act)

A. No Time Limit
Many directions respecting costs, such as increasing a fee for an expert, must be made by
the trial judge, not the assessment ofﬁcer. At one time there were awkward time limits for such
directions, especially entry of the formal judgment. Those were long ago repealed in Alberta,¹
with
the result that old precedents mentioning a time limit are likely no longer valid. There used to be
some time limits on when a judge could give directions as to costs (notably entry of judgment), but
R.10.30(1) (c) allows late directions.²
Rule 10.30(1) (c) is not a bar or presumption against awarding costs before the judgment or
ﬁnal order has been entered. It is on a different topic: creating an exception to the functus officio
doctrine, letting the court award costs after judgment or a ﬁnal order has been entered on the
substantive issues. It allows a costs award then, but does not require it then.³
However, where after a trial a separate hearing about costs is held and decided and an order
is given, months later the trial judge can decline to hear or decide yet another application for
higher expert fees at trial, where still more evidence would be necessary.⁴
Rule 10.30(1) lets costs be awarded or set after entry of judgment, thus modifying the functus
officio rule. Because of the presumption in R.14.88, often the Court of Appeal makes no speciﬁc
direction about costs, but a request for a more speciﬁc direction should be made to the court
within two months, according to the Information Note associated with R.14.88. Case law says that
that period can be extended.⁵
If a costs award is made without the judge's knowing that there had
been a formal offer to settle, until the formal judgment or order has been entered, the judge is not
functus officio. And R.10.30(1) (c) makes an exception to functus officio for costs, until entry.⁶
If parts of a single costs award are enforceable and parts are not (e. g.2 topics), later the court
can use R.9.14 to apportion the award among the 2 topics. It is better that the original judge make
the apportionment, but if that judge has moved to another court in the meantime, and transcripts
etc. are available, another judge can apportion.⁷

10-56
R.10.30(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
A decision which speaks to costs before costs are sought cannot preclude an objection to the
costs later sought.¹
The Limitations Act does not set a deadline for seeking costs. Costs are indemniﬁcation, not a
requirement to comply with a duty or to pay damages for violation of a right (which is the deﬁnition
of a “remedial order” under that Act).²
B. Advance Costs
On an advance or interim costs award, at the outset of a suit, see R. v. Caron,³
and Anderson
v. R.⁴
Ordinarily, the courts do not order advance funding by the government of litigation costs in
private litigation.⁵
However, in exceptional cases involving infants or the public interest, if there is
no other way to let the suit proceed, the courts may order one litigant to pay the other interim costs
at an early stage of the suit.⁶
Advance costs orders can only be against adverse parties.⁷
Advance
costs are discretionary and not necessary even if the three tests for such costs are met.⁸
However,
the applicant must satisfy three absolute requirements: impecuniosity, a prima facie meritorious
case, and issues of public importance.⁹
Advance costs are ordered as a last resource.¹⁰
Advance costs are still governed by the Okanagan case and require inability to pay for the
suit, a prima facie meritorious case, and issues transcending a particular litigant’s individual
interests. All three are needed, but even if they are met, the court can still decline to order that.
The Anderson Supreme Court of Canada decision ampliﬁes the requirements. Access to justice
is not the paramount consideration, and advance costs are not a parallel legal aid system. The
courts’ constitutional role limits such costs to rare and exceptional cases. The tests are rigorous.¹¹

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.30(2)
The mere fact that the opponent can more easily pay than can the applicant, is not enough.¹
And an unlimited costs-plus approach to such ﬁndings is improper.²
A party seeking such advance
costs from the opponent must demonstrate impecuniosity, and that includes a duty to show that
other funding has been sought and has all failed, which in turn requires also showing that no
counsel can be found who will bring the suit purely on a contingency basis.³
The applicant must
give evidence of necessity, and at times that evidence must be detailed.⁴
The applicant plaintiff
must show that the suit has apparent merit,⁵
but previous certiﬁcation of it as a class action
sufﬁces?⁶
But going outside what has been certiﬁed does not have prima facie merit.⁷
To get
advance funding (costs) for a suit from the opponent, public importance beyond private interests,
on issues not yet decided, must be shown.⁸
It is important for the public to know that the rights of
those for whom the Crown is trustee, are protected.⁹
That a public body is a defendant is not enough to show important public interest, which must
be exceptional and have real impact and not have been resolved yet. The private interests and
torts claims cannot be ignored here.¹⁰
Vulnerability of minors, ﬁduciary duties to them, and the importance of holding such ofﬁcials
accountable, make advance funding especially suitable.¹¹
Family law cases are not apart from
general law on advance costs; they are indeed that law's exemplar. The usual 3-part test for
advance costs applies to family law cases.¹²
The 3-part test also applies to appeals, but merit may
be harder to show there.¹³
See further R.12.36 and notes.
Advance costs can be awarded in big chunks, not waiting for each step?¹⁴
C. Previous Costs Decisions
One King's Bench justice cannot make a costs award which contradicts an earlier costs order
by another judge in the same suit.¹⁵
But where the question is simply clarifying the intent of the

10-58
R.10.30(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
court in a costs order, it is not functus officio and can later clarify the costs award.¹
The whole
functus officio doctrine may not apply to amending judgments or orders in respect of costs, and
there is certainly no bar if the judgment or order is not yet entered.²
A hearing about costs of a previous substantive decision is not the time or place to rehear the
correctness of the previous decision, unless the judge agrees there is some obvious error in it.
Especially without notice to the other side of an intent to rehear.³
D. Settlement
If the parties settle part or all of a suit for a lump sum all-inclusive, R.10.30(1) (b) does not
apply, and the court cannot award one party costs for the part settled.⁴
A settlement contract ending a suit for a lump sum “plus costs” means that any dispute as to
how much the costs should be, will be decided by the assessment ofﬁcer (subject to any previous
directions by the court).⁵
A consent judgment calling for taxation (assessment) of costs if they are
not speciﬁcally agreed by the parties, implies that the judge exercises his or her usual discretions.⁶
Settlement of some issues during trial does not bar a costs award about them, at least to date
of that settlement.⁷
E. Miscellaneous
Costs are only payable when awarded, not before, and so they can be awarded after a party has
gone into bankruptcy and been discharged, even if the steps in question were before bankruptcy.⁸
Since an appeal does not create an automatic stay of execution, and no actual application
for a stay was made, that steps were being pursued in the Supreme Court was not a reason to
postpone any decision about costs.⁹
Where a contract does not provide for payment by one party of the other party’s legal fees,
then legal fees are generally not recoverable as damages, but they can be addressed in the
litigation through the costs regime.¹⁰

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(1)
Court-ordered Costs Award
10.31(1) After considering the matters described in rule 10.33 [Court
considerations in making a costs award], the Court may order one party to pay
to another party, as a costs award, one or a combination of the following:
(a) the reasonable and proper costs that a party incurred to ﬁle an
application, to take proceedings or to carry on an action, or that a
party incurred to participate in an application, proceeding or action,
or¹
(b) any amount that the Court considers to be appropriate in the
circumstances, including, without limitation,
(i) an indemnity to a party for that party’s lawyer’s charges, or
(ii) a lump sum instead of or in addition to assessed costs.
(2) Reasonable and proper costs under subrule (1) (a)
(a) include the reasonable and proper costs that a party incurred to
bring an action;
(b) unless the Court otherwise orders, include costs incurred by a party
(i) in an assessment of costs before the Court, or
(ii) in an assessment of costs before an assessment ofﬁcer;
(c) do not include costs related to a dispute resolution process described
in rule 4.16 [Dispute resolution processes] or a judicial dispute
resolution process under an arrangement described in rule 4.18
[Judicial dispute resolution arrangement] unless a party engages in
serious misconduct in the course of the dispute resolution process
or judicial dispute resolution process;
(d) do not include, unless the Court otherwise orders, the fees and
other charges of an expert for an investigation or inquiry or the
fees and other charges of an expert for assisting in the conduct of a
streamlined trial or a trial. [Alta. Reg.126/23]
(3) In making a costs award under subrule (1) (a), the Court may order any one or
more of the following:
(a) one party to pay to another all or part of the reasonable and proper
costs with or without reference to Schedule C [Tariff of Recoverable
Fees];
(b) one party to pay to another an amount equal to a multiple, proportion
or fraction of an amount set out in any column of the tariff in Division

Footnote
2 of Schedule C [Tariff of Recoverable Fees] or an amount based on
one column of the tariff, and to pay to another party or parties an
amount based on amounts set out in the same or another column;
(c) one party to pay to another party all or part of the reasonable
and proper costs with respect to a particular issue, application or
proceeding or part of an action;
1 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.600(1) (a). It came from 1914 R.729, via 1944 C. R.727.

10-60
R.10.31(4) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
(d) one party to pay to another a percentage of assessed costs, or
assessed costs up to or from a particular point in an action.
(4) The Court may adjust the amount payable by way of deduction or set–off if
the party that is liable to pay a costs award is also entitled to receive an amount
under a costs award.
(5) In appropriate circumstances, the Court may order, in a costs award, payment
to a self-represented litigant of an amount or part of an amount equivalent to the
fees speciﬁed in Schedule C [Tariff of Recoverable Fees].
(6) The Court’s discretion under this rule is subject to any speciﬁc requirement
of these rules about who is to pay costs and what costs are to be paid.

A. Scope of Costs
About the only thing which costs awarded by a court cannot cover is the expenses of the
court or tribunal (beyond fees under R.13.32), or of applications by the parties to other tribunals
or regulatory agencies.¹
Costs may include steps necessary but taken before the order, e. g.
cross-examination on afﬁdavits used to get the order.²
Or expenses necessary before suing, e. g.
interpreters' fees.³
The court can allow as costs any other lawyer’s fee or any other disbursement
made or owing and connected with the litigation which it ﬁnds reasonable.⁴

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
In R.10.31(1), the word "participates" is construed broadly. Defendants are usually involuntary
parties, and if they win, no particular level of activity is needed to receive costs, whether as fees
or as disbursements. Trying to have the suit dismissed, or incurring disbursements reasonably
expected to help, sufﬁces.¹
There were two separate suits. In one suit, the plaintiffs got court leave to use a lot of discovery
information got in the second suit, to save money. When the other suit failed, the defendant costs
were held to include costs for its efforts about the discovery in the ﬁrst suit.²
Of course it is important to avoid double counting, and compensating the same work twice,
under different Rules.³
Though courts have inherent power to order costs in an indictable prosecution, that should
be done only in rare and exceptional circumstances, and costs should not be given (under that
power) of a costs application which failed.⁴
On G. S. T. , see R.10.48n.
The usual Rules and procedures about costs apply to recusal applications,⁵
and appeals to
the Court of King's Bench from Alberta Court of Justice.⁶
And even a failed criminal prosecution?⁷
A reference to “compensation” in an Act does not let a tribunal award costs. This was general
law not close to the tribunal’s expertise and home statute; no deference is owed to such a decision.⁸
Ordinarily there would be no costs in a judicial dispute resolution because of R.10.31(2) (c),⁹
but they were awarded where a contract called for them.¹⁰
But there is no bar to costs for later
motions to settle the terms of a compromise, even if it came from a judicial dispute resolution and
to settle other costs issues.¹¹

10-62
R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Costs have ﬁve aims: indemnity, aiding access to justice (including access by those with
little money), discouraging frivolous claims and defences, sanctioning inappropriate behavior,
and encouraging settlement.¹
The principal aim is indemnity.²
That a costs award would “unduly
penalize” the losing party is inaccurate, as costs are primarily designed for indemnity, not
punishment, penalty or beneﬁt.³
But litigants who are experienced and sophisticated and well
funded, pursuing a high-stakes lawsuit, involve somewhat different aims, and access to justice is
of slight importance then. See R.10.29 n. A.3.
Party-party costs are always discretionary,⁴
not an absolute entitlement, and Schedule C is
not automatic. Costs can be used to encourage settlement and discourage frivolous suits and
unnecessary steps, as well as to indemnify, though indemnity is the most important.⁵
Costs are desirable and intended to reimburse a party who should not have to pay his or her
(full) expenses; they are not a penalty and are not payable to the court or government.⁶
C. Terminology
The terminology⁷
of costs orders or agreements is sometimes confusing or inconsistent, so
it is safer to spell out in a formal order exactly what one means. But the following seem to be the
proper meanings of some common phrases:
(a) costs in the cause: the later winner of the lawsuit will receive from the loser the costs of
the present step;⁸
(b) costs reserved to the trial judge (or to the panel hearing the appeal) : the later trial judge
(or later panel) will decide at the end of the suit who (if anyone) gets costs of the present
step;
(c) costs in any event (of the cause) : the party named receives costs of the present step,
irrespective of who later wins or loses the suit (and they are payable at the end) ;⁹
(d) costs to X in the cause: if X later wins the suit, X receives costs of the present step; if X
later loses the suit, no one gets costs of the present step;
(e) costs to X in the cause in any event: contradictory and so meaningless.
(f) no costs: each party pays its own lawyer and disbursements, with no indemnity from any
other party;

(g) thrown-away costs: wasted expenses, not motions won by the defaulting party.¹
(h) “costs of the application” in an order: in context may well refer not merely to arguing, but
also to ancillary steps such as cross-examining on afﬁdavits.²

“Thrown-away costs” do not cover steps which could be useful later in the lawsuit or ultimate
resolution of the dispute. So limited thrown-away costs were awarded.³
They are especially

D. Evidence
Inadmissible material cannot be looked at by the court, even when deciding on costs.⁵
Usually costs of a suit require no (more) evidence, because they come after a trial and the
judge knows the facts. But if they are assessed after one party concedes a suit partway through
it, there should be a written application disclosing what afﬁdavits will be relied on, with a chance
to cross-examine, and there may even have to be live evidence.⁶
The Bank of Canada maintains an inﬂation calculator on the internet, whose cite is given in
Verwoerd v. Verwoerd.⁷
Rule 10.35 requires a bill of costs from the party seeking costs, and absence of that harms
the party asked to pay, especially if the actual legal bills incurred by the party seeking costs are
argued as some basis for calculating these party-party costs now sought. It would be an appendix
or enclosure to the costs argument, not to have its pages counted in the judge’s limit for the
length of written argument on costs. Without such a bill, the actual expenses incurred by the party
seeking costs, cannot be considered.⁸

10-64
R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
2. Lump Sum Partial Indemnity
The fee part of party-party costs at the end of the suit is really but one lump sum, however it
is calculated, whether that is on a tariff, or an adjusted tariff, as a lump sum, or as some fraction
of solicitor-client costs. The basic question is what is reasonable, and these methods merely test
that.¹
Schedule C does not set what services are inherently “worth”.²
Schedule C is designed to
cover a fraction of the winner’s bill from its lawyer.³
Forty to ﬁfty percent of actual expenses is the
aim.⁴
That aims to balance indemnity of the winner with not too strongly discouraging access to
the courts.⁵
The court can go up or down from that percentage but using the relevant factors in
Rr.10.33 and 10.2(1).⁶
Enhanced costs can take any of a number of forms.⁷
If it is proposed to
award a fraction of actual reasonable lawyers’ bills, not use Schedule C, actual computations and
analysis of both items should be given to the court, not mere totals of either or both.⁸
Though actual legal bills of either side are one relevant factor to consider, they should not be
used blindly; a fraction of an unreasonable or not proportional legal bill (vis-à-vis either side in the
suit) is not a proper starting point for calculations, and should be reasonable in the circumstances,
even if a fraction of it is being calculated.⁹
The sum awarded as costs must not be nominal, as incentives and disincentives are needed,
but it must not be so large as to deter most litigation.¹⁰
Length of the trial, the number of exhibits, or the number of witnesses, is not the vital factor in
assessing costs, especially if counsel worked hard to shorten the trial and arrange and agreed on
things.¹¹
The same is true of the costs schedule’s (tariff’s) item for preparation for trial.¹²

10-65
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
There is a review of somewhat older case law on awarding a multiple of a column of Schedule
C as fees.¹
3. Tariff (Schedule C)
(a) General
Rule 10.31(1) (a) gives a menu of possible types of costs order, which includes a lump sum
or percentage. Schedule C limits the party-party fees which the assessment officer can award
only if the court has been silent on the subject. But Schedule C has no application whatever to a
justice or applications judge, unless the justice or applications judge chooses to apply it.²
There
is no longer a presumption that the tariff in Schedule C applies to a judge by default.³
It is merely
one of a number of possible tools.⁴
Schedule C is simple, quick, efﬁcient, and inexpensive to apply
or compute.⁵
And predictable?⁶
But the list is not exclusive, and any other reasonable and proper basis to reimburse expenses
incurred is allowed.⁷
An odd suggestion that Schedule C should be used if one party has less money than the
other,⁸
seems contrary to a large number of precedents, old and new, especially as it says 40% to
50% of full indemnity is then an improper aim.
(b) Inﬂation
Until early 2020, Schedule C was grossly out-of-date and badly eroded by inﬂation. The 2020
amendment was some years old because the government had previously refused to enact it,⁹

R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
long after the joint statutory Rules Committee adopted it. In recent years the rate of inﬂation has
again increased dramatically as well, and the current Schedule C yet again does not reﬂect that.
Because of the passage of time, judges often allow for the effect of inﬂation by various means,¹
including by an increase over Schedule C.²
As Schedule C was over 20 years old, it was proper for
the Court to multiply the amounts in the column which would otherwise apply.³
It is about 23 years
since the 1997 Report calculating the Schedule C which was in force until early 2020.⁴
Since Schedule C was so far removed from reality until early 2020, it may have been easier
to achieve the aim of Schedule C (40-50% of actual legal bills)⁵
by awarding as party-party costs
some fraction of actual legal fees.⁶
That is still a permissible method (or cross check).⁷
But now
again Schedule C with only a slight increase is not adequate in protracted litigation.⁸
(c) Not Using Schedule C
Judges in Alberta frequently do not use Schedule C⁹
(sometimes by using a lump sum¹⁰
).
Examples are found in several circumstances:¹¹

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
(iv) where the loser has some partial duty to indemnify, or the winner is litigating a public
right, not merely his own;¹
(v) where the steps taken are duplicative, unnecessary, or overcautious;²
(vi) where the amount in issue is much above that for the top column, and the case is
complex;³
(vii) where the indirect worth or importance of the issues is large.⁴
(viii) where the lawsuit is unusually complex or hard fought.⁵
(ix) where the losing party’s case had no merit at all.⁶
But those examples (i) -(ix) above are in no sense exclusive. A justice or applications judge is
in no sense obliged to use Schedule C, nor even to start with it and merely adjust it.⁷
The court has a broad discretion to set costs other ways, regardless of Schedule C.⁸
This is
discussed more fully in R.10.33 n. A.4.
All the circumstances in R.10.33 are relevant to adopting a multiple of Schedule C.⁹
If a fraction of full indemnity is awarded, that is based on a reasonable legal bill to the winner;
the actual legal bill might be too high (or too low).¹⁰

10-70
R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
A big issue is proportionality, especially whether or not the winner litigated disproportionately,
or whether there is double counting or undue punishment.¹
(d) Interpreting Schedule C
Costs are not higher for personal injury litigation.²
Where costs must be partial indemnity, not
full, and there are no other factors justifying enhanced costs, use of counsel from another province
is not a ground for enhanced costs, even if the winning party resides in that other province.³
On appeal from Alberta Court of Justice to King's Bench, the items in Schedule C for appeals
should not generally be used (items 13, 14, 15). The Alberta Court of Justice’s tariff is not suitable
either.⁴
In weighing the adequacy of tariff (Schedule C) items, one must recall that many (even most)
services are not listed, and so are often provided for (if at all) by the size of the amounts given
for the other items which are listed.⁵
Unfortunately, judges rarely apply subrule 10.41(3) (c), which
seems to be little known. Some cases ignore it by implying that every step must necessarily be
part of one of the items in Schedule C. That is plainly not correct, given both the wording of the
individual items, and this subrule.⁶
Time for certain steps such as cross-examining on an afﬁdavit goes by minimum steps of a
half day, with no deduction for time spent arguing over the steps or questions or undertakings.⁷
When Schedule C gives a fee for a contested application, the Schedule does not intend to
have added to that the fee for ﬁling an afﬁdavit, even if that afﬁdavit was used to support the
position of that party on that application.⁸
4. Amount in Issue
As a general principle, the amount of costs should be proportionate to the amounts in issue
in the proceeding, which may put limits on when full indemnity is awarded. All the factors in R.
10.33 are relevant.⁹
The different columns of Schedule C reﬂect different degrees of preparation usually appropriate
to different amounts in issue, though the justice or applications judge is not bound by them.¹⁰
If money is the ultimate object (relief) of the suit, Column 1 is not appropriate.¹¹
But some older
decisions ignore the aim of the suit, or what is clearly but indirectly in issue.

10-71
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
The fact that an application ultimately results in a consent order, does not mean that it ﬁts
within Schedule C's words "uncontested application".¹
If the amount awarded is a large multiple of the amount of Schedule C, Column 5, a lump sum
far above Schedule C, Column 5 may be awarded.²
Where no amount of money is directly in issue, the court can nevertheless ﬁx an amount of
costs, or adopt a column of Schedule C, with an eye to the amounts likely ultimately involved.³
In a suit including a matrimonial property claim over property said to be worth almost $3 million,
Column One of Schedule C is not appropriate. A monetary amount is involved in a matrimonial
property suit, even if equal division is sought.⁴
For example, a 45% interest in land worth $2-3
million.⁵
In a matrimonial property case, if the difference between the worth of property (or division)
claimed and that determined by the court is large, a higher column may be used.⁶
To select a column of Schedule C, or otherwise calculate the fee part of costs, the Court usually
uses the amount claimed in the pleadings, not the amount supposedly “really” in dispute, nor the
amount in dispute in the relevant interlocutory application.⁷
The proper column of Schedule C is
set by the amount initially claimed in the statement of claim, even if the parties agreed on a lower
assessment of damages shortly before trial.⁸
Someone making an unrealistically-large claim for
damages is stuck with that amount when a column for costs payable by him must be noted and
used.⁹
If there was a ﬁnal judgment, usually that sets the column of Schedule C, or the basis for costs,
even on appeal. But costs are in the court’s discretion, and if the appeal requires going through
very bulky evidence and where the amounts claimed in the losing party’s factum are large and
unsubstantiated, the court may go higher.¹⁰
If the amount awarded (or claimed) at trial is far above the amounts for the columns in Schedule
C, for that and other reasons, multiplying Schedule C fees is not very suitable, especially as big,
complex suits often involve work not mentioned in Schedule C.¹¹

R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
If relief is not monetary, and no amount is claimed, whether to go over column 1 depends on
(inter alia) importance of issues to the parties, complexity of the issues (in all its facets), and
conduct of a party shortening or lengthening the suit or trial, including changes of course, and
the amounts indirectly in issue.¹
That should not be a mechanical or superﬁcial exercise. Setting a
precedent may justify higher costs.²
One cannot escape columns of Schedule C higher than column 1, by saying the suit was
declaratory where the suit also sought signiﬁcant damages, or where the relief sought was
otherwise coercive, e. g. the right to certain income streams. If given, such relief would yield
immediate property or money.³
If there is a big counterclaim, then the column of Schedule C to be used against the defendant
(plaintiff by counterclaim) is that apt for the size of the counterclaim, i. e. the defendant is treated
as a plaintiff.⁴
If contributory negligence reduces the plaintiff’s award, the trial judge can use the lower column
appropriate to the net award, or can reduce costs for split result, but should not do both, for that
would be double deduction.⁵
The costs in the case of a counterclaim on two competing contracts
were based on the net result of dismissing the claim on one and allowing the other.⁶
The Alberta Court of Justice cannot hear a suit in conversion, so the fact that the amount
recovered for that is within Alberta Court of Justice dollar limits, is irrelevant to costs.⁷
If the Court of Appeal reduces the damages or debt awarded, then if a lower column of
Schedule C would apply to the reduced award, the costs should be adjusted down accordingly.⁸
The "Framework of Schedule C" at the opening of that Schedule, says that up to the date of
an offer (not bettered), cost to one party are based on the judgment amount, and after that date,
to the other party based on the amount claimed.⁹
5. Second Counsel
Tests for granting a fee for second counsel are reviewed. Complexity and importance of the
case are relevant, but are not conditions precedent. Monetary value of the case is weighty.¹⁰
The
second counsel need not have attended every appearance, but must have helped in preparation.

In real doubt, the court should err on the generous side, as costs are only partial indemnity.¹
And the fact one side used only one counsel does not mean that the winner’s use of two was
unrealistic.²
A second counsel fee will not be approved for a straightforward appeal,³
or a fairly simple
trial.⁴
But generally, the test for this is fairly lax.⁵
Second counsel fee may be allowed on a complex
motion,⁶
or in an important complex suit where neither side can agree on anything.⁷
Or on a
complex appeal in the Court of King's Bench reopening dense and voluminous facts entailing
hours of detailed labor.⁸
Or if the trial is complex and the issues wide and the record bulky, even if
second counsel did not speak much at trial.⁹
If one of several plaintiffs retains separate counsel,
even if a separate set of costs is not proper, a second counsel fee may be.¹⁰

documents. That was done instead of boosting the costs of the action.¹¹
An articling student helped at trial, so one-half of second counsel fee was given.¹²

involve a lot of work by support lawyers behind the scenes, so a special award of second counsel

6. Risk of Nonpayment
Though English and Ontario courts have increased the winner’s costs where the winner was
impecunious, to give his lawyer a bonus to compensate for risk, the Supreme Court has barred
such a premium in Canada, whether or not the lawyer worked on a contingency.⁴
The court may increase costs to the winners on the ground that they are members of the
losing party and so indirectly will be paying a signiﬁcant part of their own costs.⁵
7. Miscellaneous
The ground for ﬁnding liability in favor of one party generally does not determine the size of
the party-party fees to be awarded.⁶
An appellant who ﬁled a long and very compressed factum in a very complex case, got 1-1/3
times item 14.⁷
If the court sits until 6: 30 p. m. , an extra ½ day may be allowed under Schedule C.⁸
See also subpart F.7.
F. Disbursements
1. Wording of Rule
The seemingly circular reference to “costs” in R.10.31(2) (a) is likely intended to refer to or
include “disbursements”.
2. What Disbursements Are Recoverable⁹
These types of disbursements which are recoverable as costs are not listed, prescribed, nor
limited.

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
A judge can allow things such as courier charges,¹
service,²
the fee for a letter of credit as
security,³
long-distance telephone or fax tolls,⁴
or the like.⁵
And maybe internet charges for sending
or receiving bulky data packages? An investigator to locate witnesses might be taxable, but only
on proof that he was necessary.⁶
Translation costs may be recoverable, even if incurred before
commencement.⁷
Also recoverable may be medical illustrations to help explain expert evidence,⁸
or a videographer who ﬁlmed the opposing party’s medical examination.⁹
Preparation of Appeal Books (Record) by counsel, at the rate which government transcript
services charge, can be allowed.¹⁰
Daily transcripts are sometimes denied as a disbursement.¹¹
Some electronic transcripts and copying other ones were not fully reimbursed, partly for violating
Rules.¹²
If evidence is taken outside Alberta on commission for use in an Alberta trial, the fee for a
transcript of the commission hearing is recoverable.¹³

disbursements for expedited transcripts were allowed; in Bowman v. Ralph’s, supra. This disbursement
was allowed in a very technical long trial, in Crooked Post v. Masterfeeds, supra. But ordering transcripts
after the end of argument to help prepare written argument sought by the judge was proper and recoverable:
Katish v. Mergaert (#2) 2006 ABQB 794. Transcripts were allowed, in Parrish & Heimbecker v. All Peace
Auctions (#2) 2002 ABQB 602, 326 AR 383.
12 Singh v. Kaler 2017 ABCA 275, 56 Alta LR(6th) 256 (¶’s 73 ff. ).
13 Crowe Mackay & Co. v. Ferry (#2) 2020 ABQB 280, JCE 1203 15920 (Apr 23) (¶’s 18-27).

R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Disbursements do not include ordinary ofﬁce expenses or overhead, such as stationery
supplies, laser printing,¹
postage, or equipment expenses.²
But surely postage or courier fees
for items larger than an ordinary letter should be recoverable. Binders for exhibits are a proper
disbursement.³
So are old medical textbooks which were current at the time of the allegedly
negligent act.⁴
If evidence is taken on commission outside Alberta for an Alberta trial, the commissioner's fee
is recoverable.⁵
Costs may include travel of counsel out of town⁶
where there are no capable local counsel
available. Or where counsel have to travel to interview potential witnesses,⁷
or to inspect the
records, which are in another city.⁸
Or where evidence is taken on commission outside Alberta,
especially because the reason that legal fees cannot be recovered as disbursements, is that it
is presumed that counsel does everything.⁹
Or where bilingualism and knowledge of another
culture and legal system were important in a huge claim.¹⁰
Or where familiarity with the client
would produce big enough economies?¹¹
Economy airfare is good enough for counsel.¹²
Air travel
to consult counsel was allowed, but cut back from a private charter to regular fares and regular
hotel rooms, not "club ﬂoor" rooms.¹³

10-77
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
3. Liability, Not Payment
The disbursements need not have been actually paid out to be recoverable; it is enough that
they are a legally-binding debt payable by the party seeking costs.¹
If previous costs have been hard to review, the reviewing ofﬁcer may dispense with vouchers
or proof of any disbursements which are comparatively small, or appear reasonable in light of
common practices or experience.²
But a court awarding costs should usually not look at material
unless it is properly on the record.³
4. Not Legal Fees
One rationale for disallowing legal fees as a disbursement is discussed.⁴
One limit on disbursements is whether they are tantamount to legal fees.⁵
Computer searching
of law is not usually allowed as a disbursement between parties, on the theory that it is a substitute
for lawyers’ fees.⁶
Whether or not hiring outside counsel is reasonable, it is not a disbursement.⁷
In an urgent situation, hiring agents abroad to get scattered documents may be a reasonable
disbursement.⁸
What is a disbursement depends on context. Between lawyer and his or her client, the lawyer’s
fees for work would not be, but they could be between the client creditor and its debtor.⁹
Party-party costs should not include interest paid to a company which ﬁnances litigation,
especially if the plaintiff is not impecunious.¹⁰
5. Reasonableness
The other important limit on disbursements (whether before a justice or applications judge or
before the assessment ofﬁcer) is that they should have been reasonable.
Reasonableness refers to the facts which the party claiming them knew or should have known
when it incurred the obligation to pay the disbursement. Hindsight is not the test,¹¹
and still less is

R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
it whether the disbursement later actually contributed to victory.¹
Payment to an adviser or expert
later not used may be reasonable and reimbursable.²
An unreasonable and so unrecoverable disbursement may be one which was negligent, or
overcautious or extravagant, bearing in mind all the facts then known, including the amounts
in issue in the suit and whether other matters such as people’s reputations were at stake.³
It is
relevant that the number of experts who can testify is limited by law.⁴
The onus of proving reasonableness is on the party claiming reimbursement.⁵
For the expense of having a nominee attend one’s medical examination, necessity need not
be proved, but a satisfactory explanation is called for.⁶
The mere fact that someone did not object to an expert opinion on receiving notice of it, does
not bar questioning its reasonableness in costs later.⁷
Schedule C’s item for “pleadings” should not be reduced just because there was no dispute
about the pleadings.⁸
Reproducing vast amounts of evidence for an appeal (some twice), most quite unnecessary,
led to disallowance of two-thirds of this disbursement.⁹
Disbursements were reduced for copying
some authorities, as there was ample time before they were due, to notice that the other side had
already ﬁled copies of some of the same authorities.¹⁰
The amounts claimed for scanning and printing ordinary papers seemed high and were
reduced.¹¹
Wasted disbursements should not be recoverable where (in the light of the ultimate Court of
Appeal decision), they were spent to execute to get more money than was properly awarded.¹²
A disbursement may not be reasonable as part of party-party costs, where it was occasioned
by the (winning) party’s having moved away during the lawsuit.¹³

10-79
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
6. Experts
(a) Who Pays
On time to object, and reasonableness, also see subpart 5 above.
Today often the biggest item on a party-party bill of costs is the fees of experts. With no special
order from the judge, the expert would get $100 per day for testifying.¹
The cost of an expert disbursement that helped resolve a custody issue was ordered to be
shared.²
(b) Amount
So long as expert's bills are reasonable, they are very often allowed in full.³
In principle there
seems to be no reason why those services should be conﬁned to testifying or preparing to testify;⁴
advising the lawyer or assisting in cross-examination should not be rigidly excluded, so long as it
was reasonable.⁵
A disbursement for an expert witness at trial is not unreasonable just because it is signiﬁcantly
higher than the bill of the similar expert paid by the other side.⁶
The higher bill reﬂected more time
spent. This witness had to testify in rebuttal on very short notice because the opposing expert
raised a new issue. This fee was not that much higher.
(c) Expert Not Testifying
If a witness is subpoenaed and briefed and attends, but is not put on the stand, often those
disbursements are assessed as having been reasonable at the time the subpoena was issued;
hindsight is not the test.⁷
But where a defendant got the case dismissed on a legal point, he was

10-80
R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
not able to get experts’ fees.¹
Where an expert was needed and used, but his preparation was
slow and inefﬁcient, especially as the plaintiff’s counsel fell ill and had to be replaced, not all the
expert’s fee was recoverable.²
If the opponent refuses to concede that various witnesses are
unnecessary, that may be ground to give costs for calling them.³
Retaining an accountant to do an
asset tracing report was proper, even if not needed ultimately at trial, because the opposing party
had clearly said on initial “questioning” that she had no other bank accounts, but later disclosed
two others in her name only, so independent asset tracing seemed needed at the time of retainer
and was a proper disbursement for costs.⁴
These general principles of experts as disbursements in a party-party bill of costs are not
displaced by the court's having ordered an expert assessment, nor by its having ordered which
party would pay in the ﬁrst instance.⁵
Nor displaced by using an expert from another province.⁶
Though ordinarily costs of attendance of an expert witness at trial depend on which party
demanded the expert attend, R.4.29 governs (not general costs Rules) after an offer is not
bettered, and the attendance was after the offer was served.⁷
(d) Reasonableness
Generally a party seeking reimbursement of a disbursement has the onus of showing that it
was reasonable.⁸
But the trial judge can decide whether the expert witness had sufﬁcient expertise and whether
his evidence was helpful.⁹
An interpreter for a trial witness was reasonable even though the
witness understood English, if the witness’ spoken English was only reasonably good.¹⁰
If retaining
the expert was unreasonable (e. g. to attend a R.5.41 medical examination), the disbursement will
not be reimbursed.¹¹
An attack on a disbursement for an expert alleging lack of ethics would require
proper evidence of unethical conduct.¹²
For criteria to review disbursements for medical experts,
see the Lee case, which also holds that reasonableness at the time is the test, and there should
be some reduction where there was overlap in work.¹³
A reasonable existing offer from the other
side is not a bar to recovering such disbursements.¹⁴

The court should be vigilant not to reimburse unreasonable expert fees.¹
For example,
an opinion on a factual scenario not proved at trial (or at least to be paid only by the party
responsible for the confusion as to the facts).²
Or a report merely summarizing existing evidence
and predicting the future.³
Where an actuarial bill of over $200 thousand was not really explained,
and far exceeded the competing expert's bill, only $40 thousand was reimbursed.⁴
The mere fact that the trial judge did not accept an expert’s opinion is not enough to deny his
bill entirely as a disbursement; it was likely still prudent to have hired and called him.⁵
7. Copying and Record Management
On transcripts, see subpart F.2.
Copying transcripts was denied as a disbursement because of noncompliance with the Rules
on transcripts.⁶
On reasonableness, see subpart 5 above.
Making copies of documents has always been regarded as a proper disbursement.⁷
The main difﬁculty is that the true cost of photocopying is hard to compute, and most law ﬁrms
charge a rate per page which is higher than pure disbursements, and includes something for the
labour of copying. As documents do not copy themselves, that seems proper in principle.⁸
Costs
are not conﬁned to disbursements. Of course the amount must be reasonable.⁹

Sometimes excessive numbers of sheets copied can lead to only partial indemnity,¹
and
reproducing all the evidence from a long trial in the appeal record, much of it in colored copies,
though much of it was unnecessary (some even on issues no longer disputed), and much of it
duplicated (in the record twice), led to only one third of those disbursements being recoverable
as costs.²
Similarly some “fax” charges are partly or wholly a fee, not a true disbursement.³
The assessment ofﬁcers are currently allowing 15¢ a page for scanned documents, as a
disbursement. But some evidence must be given as to why the documents were scanned, if it is
a party-party bill of costs.⁴
Though almost all the records produced were in a foreign language, the expenses of translating
those not yet translated were not initially passed on.⁵
Some authority allows as disbursements, fees to a company providing services for managing
a computer scanning or other database of documents or of electronic records.⁶
8. Miscellaneous
That money was saved by taking evidence of some witnesses by video, does not mean that
those disbursements should be shared.⁷
But expenses of a psychological report about custody
were shared equally.⁸
A fee for a letter of credit posted to get a stay of execution may be a proper disbursement.⁹
Apparently one’s wages lost by attendance at the other side’s independent medical examination
of one are recoverable in costs.¹⁰
Having a nominee attend every medical examination for the
opposite party was not shown to be reasonable.¹¹
If timing requires 2 separate appeals (on merits and on costs), separate disbursements to the
winning appellant for separate notices of appeal and separate appeal books are proper.¹²
An agreement between the sheriff’s ofﬁce and various civil enforcement agencies (bailiffs)
in Alberta sets the maximum tariff of fees which civil enforcement agencies can charge. An
assessment ofﬁcer cannot tax the account of a civil enforcement agency, even if it is brought in
with a bill of costs, and there would be no point to doing so anyway.¹³

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
If disbursements in U. S. funds should be covered by party-party costs, the currency conversion
should be at the date of actual payment, and prima facie at the rate actually paid by the party.¹
9. Ordinary Witnesses
Witnesses are entitled to the prescribed daily fee for their time, plus their expenses for travel²
and meals. The daily fee is calculated including travel time. For ordinary witnesses it is $100 per
day or part of a day. The complete expenses of having a potential witness attend trial and be
briefed may well be reasonably incurred and so recoverable as a disbursement in costs, even if
he does not testify, or has moved out of Alberta shortly before.³
Expert fees, or any other direction about costs, may be awarded after formal judgment, given
R.10.30. See the notes to that Rule, supra. In theory conduct money payable before is just an
estimate and advance, and the amount taxable should be the actual amount.⁴
See R.13.32 and
the notes to it.
Often a party gets a witness fee for himself or herself testifying.⁵
G. Indemnity as Costs Ceiling
Costs are ordinarily capped at full indemnity for expenses, and should not exceed that.⁶
In
other words, at best the winner should break even; he or she should not make a proﬁt out of
getting costs. If the party had a lawyer and did not do the work personally, ordinarily costs are
limited to what the party's lawyer charges.⁷
While self-represented parties incur no legal fees, and though R.1.1(2) says that the Rules
apply equally to the self-represented, R.10.31(5) exceptionally allows the court to award them
costs.⁸
This should rarely be done. The aim of party-party costs is indemnity for expenses, not

R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
payment for time, effort, and inconvenience.¹
A prospect of making a proﬁt off costs is undesirable,
especially for a self-represented litigant.²
A self-represented couple got one-half of Schedule C,
as the husband did most of the work which a lawyer would have done.³
Despite earlier cases,
now it seems settled that such an award of costs is not limited to disbursements, and may include
some fees even if the party getting costs did not hire a lawyer, and represented himself or herself,
whether or not he or she is trained in law or belongs to the Bar.⁴
That can be based on a number
of factors, including lost salary or opportunity costs,⁵
degree of complexity, quality of work, degree
of assistance and skill, lost time, undue consumption of other’s time,⁶
and reasonableness of
conduct.⁷
Schedule C is inappropriate, even as a starting point.⁸
A lay litigant got $10, 000, as
he had to do much work, and hired and paid a lawyer for certain services.⁹
Time spent by the
self-represented party is only a minor factor.¹⁰
A self-represented party was awarded over $11
thousand as costs where he won and the opponent abused court process by duplicating a claim
previously dismissed.¹¹
The winning party is presumed to be liable to pay her lawyers, witnesses, etc. their proper
fees, and so ordinarily she need not prove that liability, to collect her party-party costs.¹²
Still less
need the party seeking party-party costs already have paid them.¹³
Being on legal aid does not bar
recovering the usual party-party costs, as the Legal Aid fund has notoriously limited resources,
and its clients are supposed to reimburse it, even if only in part.¹⁴

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
It is no bar to party-party costs that the winner used a salaried in-house lawyer.¹
At one
time it was thought that house lawyers paid a salary could not produce taxable costs, and some
legislation partly amended that. The distinction seems illogical, and modern cases usually give
full fees for them.²
A statutory body appearing in court by its house lawyer is not self-represented,
and that fact does not disentitle it to costs.³
Costs of an appeal are no less payable because the party seeking them has the right to
be indemniﬁed from them by some other person. For one thing, the right to the costs may be
subrogated to that other person.⁴
It is no bar to getting party-party fees, that the lawyer agrees to be paid only out of costs
recovered from the other side. Similarly, if an insurer or protective association provides the lawyer,
that does not impede full costs.⁵
The winner is entitled to costs even though statute calls on his
employer to reimburse his legal bills.⁶
A somewhat brief and ambiguous dollar limit in a guarantee was interpreted as not capping
ordinary costs. To do that would be unusual, and require clearer words.⁷
H. Representative Parties’ Costs
1. Various Costs Issues
Costs questions can be complex where one party represents an estate or is a legal personal
representative. For example, an executor, administrator, trustee of a trust, trustee of a dependent
adult, guardian, litigation representative under R.2.11, receiver, liquidator, or trustee in bankruptcy.
Different topics may get mixed together when they should not be. Anyone involved in an estate
should look up the law ahead of time before incurring legal or litigation expenses which may not
be reimbursable.
One must carefully distinguish separate questions from each other, such as
* is the representative liable to pay costs to the other side?⁸
* if so, is that liability limited to assets in the estate?⁹
* is the representative entitled to indemnity from the estate, or from beneﬁciaries of it?¹⁰

In an estate ﬁght, court costs and legal expenses are not the same. Before a costs award, an
estate administrator can seek reimbursement from the estate for expenses he has incurred, e. g.
to defend estate assets. An administrator can be, and (usually is) a beneﬁciary, and that does not
bar such reimbursement.⁴
2. Does the Estate Pay?
Affected parties usually get costs out of the estate if the application in question is part of giving
the trustee advice which will beneﬁt the whole estate.⁵
But one losing litigation is not shielded from
paying costs by good faith or the fact that the questions litigated were important and unresolved
legal questions,⁶
nor for having led evidence arguments which also "contributed" to deciding the
issues.⁷
The cases on estates and representatives’ costs are not always easy to reconcile.⁸
The
usual rules of costs between parties apply.⁹
There is no longer any presumption that all parties

to the litigation will get costs out of the estate.¹
Many cases say that now the party who wins
should receive costs from the losing party, and that giving all parties costs "out of the estate"
means that the residual beneﬁciaries fund both sides of the lawsuit, even if they are blameless,
or won, or took no part in the ﬁght.²
Obviously that is usually unjust. Generally speaking, a
party substantially successful and guilty of no bad conduct in the lawsuit, should receive costs.³
A body representing a minority of the beneﬁciaries of a fund or trust who are not consulted
about litigation, should not expect to get the body’s costs out of the fund, for a failed risky and
novel intervention to suggest some beneﬁt for the beneﬁciaries.⁴
A solicitor ﬁled suit for a small
group, most of whom did not authorize the suit, so the solicitor and no one else was ordered to
pay costs of striking out the non-authorizing parties.⁵

R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Some cases ask whether the deceased was “at fault” for the litigation. However, that “fault”
factor must be applied with caution. The testator never starts the lawsuit; the plaintiff or claimant
does, and doubt could be resolved by a compromise bargain, so fault could be greater at the
bargaining stage after death, especially if the result was fairly predictable.¹
Even if the deceased
started the suit, died, and his executor was then substituted for him, the executor chose to be
substituted, and could have dropped the suit. Such testator’s “fault” does not exist where the
deceased had given instructions to prepare a will and then failed to execute (sign) it.²
If a Family Relief Act suit succeeds, normally the plaintiff gets full indemnity costs?³
That the party seeking indemnity for his costs from the estate is the deceased’s legal personal
representative is not conclusive. If he litigated to advance his position as a potential beneﬁciary of
the estate, especially if he could instead have sought advice and direction from the court, then he
litigates personally and not as executor or administrator.⁴
Full indemnity costs to one of the parties to the estate litigation are reserved for misconduct,
bad faith, or unreasonable conduct. As in other litigation, one party should get full-indemnity costs
from an opposing litigant for bad conduct during the time of the litigation.⁵
Even if there is such
conduct, if the party seeking costs also lengthened the litigation somewhat, the amount for the full
indemnity basis should be reduced somewhat.⁶
I. Full Indemnity Costs
1. Introduction
Counsel often ask for costs from the other side “on a solicitor-client basis, ” which means full
indemnity for legal bills of the party requesting such costs.
Why is that request or topic now more common? That is partly because there may be more
sharp or abusive litigation practice now than there used to be, and partly because inﬂation means
that Schedule C falls further and further behind real legal bills.⁷
Now inﬂation is fairly high again.
If the latter is the prime motivation, it would be better to ask the judge to award something more
than Schedule C, such as a multiple of it, or some fraction of solicitor-client fees. There is a fair
amount of recent authority for that.⁸

2. Grounds for Full Indemnity
(a) When Full Indemnity is Proper
“Solicitor-client” costs between opposing parties are authorized by R.10.31(1) (b) (i), but will
normally be awarded in one of 7 situations only:¹
(i) There was unusual serious intentional²
or neglectful or careless³
misconduct⁴

the suit;⁶

a contract must be worded clearly enough, in order to the court is to award solicitor-client
costs to the opposing side.¹
So it will not cover what is not enforcing the debt or mortgage
against the debtor or the transferee from the debtor.²
And such a covenant to pay such
costs must be pleaded.³
There is no public policy restricting a covenant to pay solicitor-
client costs. Nor is there any reason to construe such commercial contracts narrowly, in a
picky fashion.⁴
They should be enforced.⁵
Where there is a covenant to pay solicitor-client
costs, it was doubtful that a judge has a “discretion” not to follow that covenant, especially
where the fees charged reﬂect no churning, no unnecessary steps, no sharp practice,
nor pursuit of trivia to an uneconomical length.⁶
But now such a discretion has been
introduced.⁷
There is less discretion where the covenant in the particular case was for

The burden of those costs on different opponents may be

Close to full-indemnity costs may be awarded against a non-proﬁt organization which

(b) When Full Indemnity is Denied
Solicitor-client costs will not be awarded to an opponent in other circumstances, such as mere
slowness in performing, nor a genuine legal dispute,¹⁰
nor a failed but arguable case in court.¹¹

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
Nor because the indigent winner established important new precedent.¹
Nor on a failed motion to
disqualify opposing counsel.²
Nor fault on both sides for lengthening the proceedings.³
The fact
that the losing party had sought solicitor-client costs himself is a very doubtful ground, especially
as so many statements of claim now seek them.⁴
Little merit, and stubborn silliness is usually not
enough for full indemnity costs between parties.⁵
Full indemnity costs between parties must be rare and based on established categories, e. g.
on intentional misconduct or a contract awarding them.⁶
Distaste for the litigant who lost, or for its
pre-litigation conduct, or for its cause of action, is not enough.⁷
Nor is there any exception to that
general rule where full indemnity is thought needed for “justice”. Such vague basis is conclusory
“and would overtake the rule”.⁸
Some cases express reluctance to award solicitor-client (full indemnity) costs against tribunals
or other statutory bodies.⁹
Conversely, there is no reason to give a condominium corporation
solicitor-client costs just because it won against a member, absent a provision in its by-laws.¹⁰
A contract about costs between one of the parties and a non-party, is irrelevant.¹¹
Dicta in one case¹²
suggest full-indemnity costs for public interest litigation, sed quaere. There
is no special rule that indemnity costs will be awarded in constitutional cases. There was no
lengthy trial in these proceedings, and it had not been shown it was impossible to pursue the
litigation with private funding. Determining whether legislation is ultra vires is “fairly routine”.¹³
There is no default mode or presumption for full-indemnity costs in Charter litigation.¹⁴

R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
(c) Miscellaneous
But if solicitor-client costs were given in the Court of King's Bench, they may be given in the
Court of Appeal without special request, because of the Rules.¹
If the strict tests for solicitor-client costs are not met, the lesser misconduct is often punished
by higher party-party costs under R.10.33, which has laxer tests.²
Similarly higher party-party
costs may be granted where full indemnity is not given for vigorous and lengthy complex and bulky
litigation, and those may be a fraction of full indemnity.³
3. Quantum
On the terminology, see the J. S. case.⁴
There is no set tariff for solicitor-client fees; they are whatever is reasonable in the
circumstances.⁵
They exclude anything which was extravagant, not reasonably necessary,
careless, or overcautious.⁶
If the winning client instructed its lawyer to do such things, the lawyer
can get paid for all that by that instructing client, which is called solicitor-and own-client costs.⁷
But an award of costs from the opponent on a solicitor-client basis would not give the expense of
such excessive steps or charges.⁸
A court’s express award of solicitor-and-own-client-costs⁹
might
cover that. Where a contract calls for legal fees or indemnity, they should be solicitor-client, not
solicitor-and-own-client, especially when the contract says “reasonable”.¹⁰
What both sides actually paid is relevant, but full-indemnity costs are based on what is
reasonable, not necessarily on what legal bills the winning party actually received: see Part E.2,
supra.

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
So assessing full-indemnity costs needs some kind of bill of costs to show just what work was
done and what disbursements were incurred.¹
But courts sometimes are rather stingy with some aspects of a lawyer's bill to his or her client,
e. g. travel or copying expenses, or fees paid to access or search legal websites. Could solicitor-
and-own-client costs be appropriate for such topics?
A number of ways of calculating full-indemnity costs are discussed, and to save labor and
lengthy disputes, a lump sum was awarded approximating the winning party’s reasonable legal bill
and approximating a 3- or 4-fold multiplication of Schedule C costs, was awarded.²
4. Miscellaneous
Where there is bad misconduct and full indemnity would be proper for it. if the party seeking
the costs also delayed the litigation, the full-indemnity ﬁgure may be reduced somewhat.³
It may be proper to extend the solicitor-client costs to a hearing on what type of costs to award.⁴
Punitive damages and solicitor-client (full indemnity) costs should not both be given for the
same misconduct.⁵
There is no reason that a default judgment cannot give solicitor-client costs. The effect of the
contractual covenant for that does not disappear when judgment is signed. Default judgments are
given by the Clerk of the court, not by the taxing ofﬁcer, and any limit on the power of the taxing
ofﬁcer does not extend to the Clerk giving a default judgment. And if a judge gives costs, any
limit on the power of the taxing ofﬁcer or the Clerk is irrelevant where the Clerk merely settles
the minutes of the order by the judge.⁶
Furthermore, facts recited in a statement of claim are
deemed admitted when default judgment is signed. Much high authority to that effect is quoted.
Though there are some narrow exceptions not relevant here, that means that contrary evidence is
inadmissible. That extends to recitals in the statement of claim of covenants to pay solicitor-client
costs.⁷
Lack of a prayer for such costs can be overlooked where the main reason for it was in conduct
at the trial.⁸
If a ﬁduciary formally admits a breach of duty, in court he can still contest the quantum of the
reimbursement sought from him.⁹
J. Multiple Parties’ Costs
1. Nonparty Participating
Someone who gets herself added as a party and supports the position of one of the existing
parties may have to pay costs, if her side loses.¹⁰

a condominium project, the association had standing to apply for costs at a later stage. It had
reasonably incurred costs after an order in its favour had been made but not obeyed.¹
Similarly, an intervener fully participating in a suit as though it were a party may have to pay
costs. Or it may get costs.²
There seems to be no ﬁrm general rule about costs of interveners, but usually they neither pay
nor receive costs.³
It is very unusual to make an intervener Attorney General applying to defend
the constitutionality of legislation, pay costs.⁴
Occasionally the Crown is ordered to pay the costs
of someone mounting or defending a constitutional challenge.⁵
The court has jurisdiction to award costs against the true promoter of litigation, looking at
who would beneﬁt and who took active steps in the litigation. That extends also to bankrupts and
their trustees, the trustee paying out of its own assets, including suits by or against a trustee in
bankruptcy in the ordinary civil courts.⁶
In England, those funding the litigation may become liable for costs.⁷
2. Among Parties to the Suit
If there are cross-suits about the same matter, the winner may get one set of costs as a
plaintiff, and another as a defendant.⁸
One defendant should not have to pay costs of proving the plaintiff’s case against another
defendant.⁹
But where a plaintiff sues two or more defendants and at trial some defendants win
and some defendants lose, ordinarily the loser will pay costs to the plaintiff and the winner will
receive costs from the plaintiff.
In such suits, sometimes it was reasonable, even necessary, for the plaintiff to sue both
defendants.¹⁰

in the costs bill against the losing party, simply depends on whether it was reasonable in all
the circumstances to include those people in the suit. It is not always necessary that the losing

The records had information
gaps, so it was reasonable at ﬁrst to sue all the treating and consulting physicians or surgeons for
the disorder in question.²
For example, if a plaintiff had a house built which promptly collapsed, it
is almost certain that the builder or the architect (or both) is at fault, but it may be very hard to tell
which; so the plaintiff has to sue both.
In the right circumstances, if one defendant wins and the other loses, the court may make a
Bullock order, letting the plaintiff recover from the losing defendant the costs which the plaintiff
has to pay out to the winning defendant.³
Or a Sanderson order, letting the winning defendant get
his costs directly from the losing defendant, e. g. where the plaintiff may be insolvent.⁴
But such costs indemnity should end once it becomes apparent that there is no longer any
point pursuing one defendant, e. g. , on appeal.⁵
And a plaintiff who delays in accepting an offer
by one defendant should alone pay costs incurred during the delays without contribution from a
co-defendant.⁶

(¶’s 29-58). It should be for partial costs if the plaintiff was not wholly successful against anyone and may

being made to pay is not wholly responsible for the other defendant being sued? Spartek Sys. v. Brown

R.10.31(6) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
A defendant who wins can get costs against the plaintiff even if a statute required the plaintiff
to name that person as a defendant.¹
Where one wins, the court may consider which other parties
kept that defendant in the suit.²
Where a party ﬁles authorities on 3 related appeals with distinct appellants ordered to pay
costs, no one opposing party should be solely liable to pay those disbursements.³
3. Separate Costs to Co-Parties
(a) Parties on the Same Side⁴
Where each of several parties with the same interest appears by separate counsel, and all
argue,⁵
the court may award no costs, or give only a single set of costs.⁶
But parties on the same
side (e. g. co-defendants) are not obliged to take a common approach to liability or damages,
or to retain a common expert or common lawyers. Parties separately represented and ﬁling
full separate arguments making different points, may each get separate costs.⁷
As interests of
co-parties diverge, separate costs (one set each) become appropriate.⁸
If co-defendants have
common counsel but ﬁle separate but overlapping statements of defence, they may not get
two full sets of costs for pleading.⁹
If two parties for a time use the same counsel, they may
each get only half the usual costs for that period.¹⁰
But where the plaintiff’s arguments in the
