10-101
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.31(6)
King's Bench and Court of Appeal treated different defendants differently (with separate
allegations) it was proper for each defendant to use a separate lawyer and get separate costs.¹
As
interests of co-parties diverge, separate costs (one set each) becomes appropriate.²
The question
is what it had appeared might come up or need arguing, not how the suit in fact later ended.³
The
court is slow to second guess whether separate parties need separate counsel.⁴
Where there were multiple parties and cross-examination on an afﬁdavit, costs were given to
each party whose counsel attended that questioning.⁵
Where disbursements were shared by the two counsel acting for a party who was a plaintiff
in one suit and the defendant in a cross-suit, and he got costs in one action only, that included
the full disbursements which would have had to be incurred even if there had been no cross-suit.⁶
A party moving unsuccessfully to disqualify its opponent's counsel should anticipate that fresh
counsel will be hired to oppose that motion, leading to more costs than would otherwise be
incurred.⁷
(b) Inactive Parties
Where a party has no real interest in the issue tried, and takes no active part, she should not
have to pay costs.⁸
Conversely, where the party with carriage of the action beneﬁts a number of
other parties with similar interests by its work, they may have to contribute to the expense.⁹
And
a party added partway through, who takes an active part, may have to pay costs.¹⁰
There may be
split costs where a party has to attend a hearing only part of which concerns her.¹¹
The insurance
company which was the instigator and real party had to pay the costs when its suit failed, the
company wound up being insolvent, though an Ontario court running the winding-up had allowed
the insurer to bring the suit.¹²
(c) Joint and Several Liability
Unless there is good reason to order otherwise, parties on the same side who sue together
are jointly and severally liable for costs if they lose.¹³
If that was reasonable, often they can each

get a set of costs if they win.¹
And each pay costs if they lose.²
The members of an unincorporated
association can be ordered to pay costs if the association does not.³
But the subrogated hospitals authority should not be jointly liable with the main plaintiff for all

(d) Miscellaneous
If both the main claim and the counterclaim in a lawsuit have some success, for party-party
costs of the suit, one should recognize that the counterclaim is an “independent action” under R.
3.58, but the case law conﬂicts and shows three different ways to handle costs where both claims
have some success.⁵
The judge then lists 8 topics to weigh in deciding what to do. In this case,
the two parties each got party-party costs for the claim on which they succeeded (main claim and
counterclaim respectively), the two costs awards to be set off.⁶
If someone says that he should be immune from costs because he was really representing
the interests of another group, at the least he should show that that other group cannot reimburse
him for the costs he has incurred, and he would have to show that it was a suitable case to litigate
with sufﬁcient evidence.⁷
If a newly-added third party wins, it may get costs from the beginning of the suit from the party
who had it added.⁸
4. Costs Not Usually Divided
If there is no reason to order otherwise, parties’ liability to pay costs is joint and several. A
creditor should not have to chase dozens of people, each for a small sum,⁹
and plaintiffs bringing
a class action have to organize and claim contribution from each other.¹⁰
Where a party ﬁles
authorities on 3 related appeals with distinct appellants ordered to pay costs, no one opposing
party should be solely liable to pay those disbursements.¹¹

Costs in Class Proceeding
10.32 In a proceeding under the Class Proceedings Act or in a representative
action, the Court, in determining whether a costs award should be made against
the unsuccessful representative party, may take into account one or more
of the following factors, in addition to any other factors the Court considers
appropriate:
(a) the public interest;
(b) whether the action involved a novel point of law;
(c) whether the proceeding or action was a test case;
(d) access to justice considerations.

Related Provisions
2.61 (representative actions)

Costs are discretionary in all suits, even class actions.²
Decisions from other provinces can be
misleading, as often their legislation on costs in class actions is different, even opposite.³
The factors in Rr.10.32 and 10.33 are not naturally exclusive; the reference in R.10.32 includes
R.10.33’s factors. And R.10.33 on reducing costs can be inﬂuenced by R.10.32 factors, on “any
other matter related”.⁴
Which factor in Rr.10.32 and 10.33 to use in an individual case, is up to the
trial judge, even in a failed class action.⁵
For costs, access to justice is relevant and disabled elderly people can be a “disadvantaged
group”.⁶
In a class actions costs, what relation the elderly disabled had with their counsel and how
it was funded, are relevant.⁷
Behavior modiﬁcation is a legitimate consideration for party-party costs, especially in a class
action.⁸
That the suit is a class action is itself also relevant.⁹
Deep pockets are generally irrelevant,
but have some weight in a class action.¹⁰

10-104
R.10.33(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
It took years of litigation, in ﬁve provinces, with appeals and objections by large commercial
companies advancing their own common interests, not any public interest, to get approval to
settle class actions, for over $45 million. So three times Column 5 was awarded as costs for that
approval process.¹
Court Considerations in Making Costs Award
10.33(1) In making a costs award, the Court may consider all or any of the
following:
(a) the result of the action and the degree of success of each party;
(b) the amount claimed and the amount recovered;
(c) the importance of the issues;
(d) the complexity of the action;
(e) the apportionment of liability;
(f) the conduct of a party that tended to shorten the action;
(g) any other matter related to the question of reasonable and proper
costs that the Court considers appropriate.²
(2) In deciding whether to impose, deny or vary an amount in a costs award, the
Court may consider all or any of the following:
(a) the conduct of a party that was unnecessary or that unnecessarily
lengthened or delayed the action or any stage or step of the action;
(b) a party’s denial of or refusal to admit anything that should have been
admitted;
(c) whether a party started separate actions for claims that should have
been ﬁled in one action or whether a party unnecessarily separated
that party’s defence from that of another party;
(d) whether any application, proceeding or step in an action was
unnecessary, improper or a mistake;
(e) an irregularity in a commencement document, pleading, afﬁdavit,
notice, prescribed form or document;
(f) a contravention of or non-compliance with these rules or an order;
(g) whether a party has engaged in misconduct.
(h) any offer of settlement made, regardless of whether or not the offer
of settlement complies with Part 4 [Managing Litigation], Division 5
[Settlement Using Court Process]. [Alta. Reg.124/10;36/20]
Information Note
The Court has complete discretion over what to order in a costs award unless a specific rule
limits that discretion.

10-105
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.33(2)
The typical starting point will be to decide what are the reasonable and proper costs incurred
in carrying on litigation: see rule 10.31 [Court-ordered costs award]. Rule 10.33(1) [Court
considerations in making a costs award] sets out a list of matters the Court may consider
related to the litigation (degree of success, amount involved, complexity and so on) and under
subrule (2) the Court may consider matters related to the conduct of the parties, including
unnecessary steps or delay, misconduct, and contravention of the rules or contravention of
Court orders. If the conduct of a party is found to be inappropriate, the Court can impose,
deny or vary an amount that otherwise would have been allowed in the costs award.
Note that some rules have immediate costs consequences; for example, rule 5.12 [Penalty
for not serving affidavit of records] contains a specific sanction for not serving an affidavit of
records in accordance with the rules.

A. Costs to Successful Party
1. Limits on Judge's Discretion
See R.10.29 n. A.2
2. Who is Treated as Winner
Usually costs at the end of a suit are awarded and presumed to be properly awarded,²
on the basis of who won the suit on balance, i. e. the nature of the ﬁnal judgment.³
On
misconduct, see Part B below. And costs of an appeal usually go to the winner of the

10-106
R.10.33(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
appeal.¹
Being less successful on appeal than one had been at trial is not a ground to alter the
costs in the trial court.²
Good faith or raising unsettled issues does not shield the loser from costs.³
A plaintiff who succeeds in getting an award of a signiﬁcant amount of damages or debt in her
favor rarely gets as much as she asked for, and often she made some arguments which did not
succeed. That is not ordinarily looked on as divided success, particularly when the unsuccessful
claims or arguments did not much lengthen the suit, and are evidentiary or procedural.⁴
Success
or partial success refers to results, not just to winning one argument; the court does not generally
parse issues for costs purposes.⁵
And several issues with overlapping evidence are not usually
segregated for costs purposes.⁶
It may be different if the ultimate winner litigated issues which
all failed, and raised the winning point only at a late stage, or if the court found the winning point
without counsel’s help.⁷
What is success on an issue is measured objectively.⁸
If there were a number of interlocutory motions, a party may get costs only for the motions
won. If the person losing the suit won one interlocutory motion, whether that decreases the costs
he or she pays one opposite party may depend upon whether various parties were involved and
whether that motion had much to do with this party’s position.⁹

10-107
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.33(2)
What seems to be a minor issue (e. g. a small counterclaim) which fails, is often ignored for
costs purposes. If the losing party alleges that the minor issue much prolonged the trial, he may
have to prove that through clerks' notes or transcripts.¹
If the main claim and the counterclaim
have essentially the same issues, often no costs are given for the counterclaim, and there is
apportionment of costs, not costs for the counterclaim.²
If the plaintiff wins the main claim and on
the counterclaim, usually the costs are calculated on the amount of the judgment.³
If the costs awarded at trial were too low, the recipient should raise that as a matter of appeal,
not wait and ask for a higher costs level on appeal than the trial level.⁴
Matrimonial litigation follows the usual principles for costs.⁵
It is not true that no one wins a
matrimonial property suit; ordinary principles of costs apply.⁶
But not in insolvency matters?⁷
Where someone has to go to court to get some relief, and the other side shows up but elects
to make no submissions, nor to argue against the application, so that relief is granted, it is proper
to give costs against that respondent to the applicant who got the order.⁸
A respondent to an
application should be ordered to pay costs if the order prayed for is later granted, unless the
respondent plainly states beforehand that he consents, and does not present arguments against
any part of the order later granted.⁹
Half the normal costs were granted where an application
was abandoned, but where it had to be brought and was abandoned simply because after it
was brought the other side gave the relief which had been prayed for.¹⁰
Where an application
was abandoned but it produced some result, namely the concession that a lawyer need not be
called as a witness at trial, and an acknowledgement by one defendant that he had no limitations
defence, cross-costs presumably one-half the normal fee, were allowed.¹¹
If the Court of Appeal reverses the result at trial, the basis for the Trial Judge’s trial costs
decision disappears, and the Court of Appeal can thus revisit trial costs, even if the Trial Judge’s
costs part of his or her decision contained no error.¹²
A contested interlocutory motion for an injunction to obey a contract was granted after a very
late consent, the enjoined party not admitting that he breached the contract, but the applicant
gaining a substantial beneﬁt. The applicant was found partly successful, so it got Schedule C
costs (on column 4).¹³

R.10.33(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
3. Truly Divided Success
However, where a suit really has several separate parts, or where an unsuccessful argument
has consumed a good deal of the time and money spent in the suit, then the trial judge has the
power to apportion costs because of the divided success.¹
Success is determined objectively from who had the greater success, looking at the outcome,
degree of quantitative and qualitative success, and success on issues or claims. Overall, a
balanced assessment of the outcome and the nature of the ﬁnal judgment is sufﬁcient.²
If each party has some large success, often a trial judge will properly not enter into detailed
calculations, and avoid calculations and set-off, by awarding no costs to either side.³
In true cases of divided success, the judge can either award each side partial costs against
the other (and usually order that they be set off),⁴
or the judge can calculate a rough set-off, and
award reduced costs to one side approximating what the set-off would yield.⁵
Or simply award
no costs, if the offsetting costs would be approximately equal.⁶
or if the exact balance cannot be
exactly calculated.⁷
A party abandoned a key trial issue only after all the evidence at trial had been
heard. Not doing so sooner lengthened the trial. In setting costs, ultimate success is not the only
relevant topic, and success in the other direction on some issues can be looked at. So the plaintiff
should only pay costs for a 4-day trial, not for all 6 days.⁸
There cannot be double counting (double
discount) for lack of full success, e. g. both lower column and fractional costs.⁹
In Alberta, it is traditional to give the plaintiff costs of a size proportional to the net damages
which she recovered, and not to split up costs according to the degrees of blame found at trial.¹⁰
On costs where both the main claim and the counterclaim succeed, see R.3.58 and a review
of the 3 possible results and the 8 topics to weigh.¹¹

(City) (#2) 2005 ABQB 791, [2006] 4 WWR 164; Lavallee v. Siksika Nation (#2) 2011 ABQB 282, [2011]

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.33(2)
4. Excuses to Escape Costs
(a) Irrelevant Poor Excuses
Lack of wrongdoing by the losing party is not a reason to shield him or her from costs.¹
Costs
give a healthy disincentive to bring doubtful claims.²
After all, a lawsuit consumes large resources
owned by both parties and by society. They are gone forever. To award no costs would be a gift to
the losing party and a burden on the winning party.³
A consolation prize is not appropriate.
Costs usually go to the winner, even if the litigation was caused by the tribunal below, not
by the losing party.⁴
But it would be very rare that the tribunal has to pay costs, even on judicial
review.⁵
If judicial review fails because another type of suit is the proper avenue, the government
agency whose decision was not overturned is entitled to costs.⁶
Ease of access to justice is not a ground to refuse to make the losing party pay costs. All
the aims of civil procedure law and the Rules of Court point in the opposite direction. Costs are
designed to promote settlement, deter frivolous actions and defences, deter unnecessary steps,
and sanction those who reject a reasonable settlement offer, as the Supreme Court of Canada
has held. That applies equally to plaintiffs and to defendants.⁷
That one side lacked merit does not prove that the other side had merit.⁸
A limitations statute is not a mere technicality, and serves a beneﬁcial purpose, so failing by
suing too late does not mean that the plaintiff should pay no costs, or only small costs.⁹
The Local Authorities Election Act lets a successful candidate disclaim the seat and be free of
costs. A candidate who supported her election win by opposing a court challenge can be made to
pay costs. The municipality is only liable for costs if the problem was its fault.¹⁰
If a civil suit challenging a Alberta Court of Justice conviction fails, the plaintiff must pay civil
costs, especially if his or her motives are oblique.¹¹
Not seeking costs at stages when a party was unsuccessful is probably not a relevant factor
when the party wins, even as defendant in a class action.¹²
That the case is novel and important to the public, and the losing party stood to make no
money, is not a bar to, nor maybe even a signiﬁcant ground for, exercising to the judge’s discretion
to make him pay costs.¹³

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.33(2)
(b) Possible Excuses
When and to what level a public authority litigating is subjected to any different costs
considerations is discussed (with analysis of some case law).¹
A party who unnecessarily caused litigation may be denied costs.²
But in a class action, “public interest” includes issues of broad public importance or persons
historically disadvantaged in society, such as those elderly and disabled.³
Other irrelevant excuses which do not shield a litigant from paying costs include impecuniosity,⁴
having followed legal advice and being ignorant of the laws,⁵
that no money was sought in this
lawsuit,⁶
or what the winner will or will not spend the costs on.⁷

1. Introduction
On full-indemnity (solicitor-client) costs for litigation misconduct, see R.10.31 n. I.

R.10.33(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
defending the suit, is a frequently-used ground for denying costs to that party though it won,¹
or
for increasing the costs which that party would otherwise pay.²
Indeed misconduct and difﬁcult
behavior may demand enhanced costs, and allow the Court of Appeal to intervene if they are not
given.³
Increased costs for misconduct are especially ﬁt if the resulting expense to the opponent
was predictable, and the volume of materials before the court was large.⁴
This use of costs is now
more important, given R.1.2 and the emphasis on proportionality and economy, the need for court
sanctions for misconduct short of contempt, and the need for court supervision.⁵
Sometimes the higher costs for misconduct are to punish the wrongdoer, but often they are
proper compensation for the innocent party.⁶
Of course punishing includes deterring misconduct.
"Punitive costs" are not a third category separate from party-party and solicitor-client costs.
Indemnity is the primary principle underpinning cost.⁷
That there is no contempt of court is no bar to assessing extra costs for misconduct.⁸
Indeed
if a motion for contempt fails, the party accused of contempt may have to pay elevated costs for
his misconduct.⁹
Litigation misconduct can take many forms. But mere lack of merit in the suit, even misconceived
litigation, is not misconduct,¹⁰
unless the position was patently indefensible, having almost no
chance of succeeding, frivolous or vexatious.¹¹
Usually the costs penalty for misconduct is conﬁned to the expense or inconvenience of that
particular misconduct, or of correcting it; changing the cost of the whole suit requires systemic and
persistent misconduct.¹²
If one party is guilty of bad enough misconduct for full-indemnity costs but
the other side needlessly lengthened the litigation somewhat, the otherwise-suitable full indemnity
ﬁgure may be reduced somewhat.¹³

10-113
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.33(2)
These principles of higher costs for misconduct apply also in child-custody cases,¹
and in
estate litigation.²
One must not conﬂate punitive damages and punitive costs, which differ fundamentally; usually
the former is for pre-suit misconduct and the latter for post-suit.³
Punitive damage awards are not
an automatic bar to higher (or full-indemnity) costs for litigation misconduct.⁴
But clear evidence and advance notice of the allegation and the costs issue are needed.⁵
Sometimes, misconduct on both sides may cancel out.⁶
One element supporting increased costs payable for litigation misconduct can be the fact that
the innocent party’s counsel charged an unsually low hourly rate.⁷
A party faced with an accusation of misconduct being used to seek special costs, should
either give his explanation to the ﬁrst judicial ofﬁcer (e. g. an Applications Judge ruling on costs), or
should at least say that there was a matter before the Law Society, and that he was not at liberty
to disclose the facts then. He cannot remain silent and then later raise this defence.⁸
2. Examples of Occasions for Higher Costs⁹
Sometimes misconduct removes the right to get costs, e. g. where the guilty party wins. Those
examples are included in this Part B.2.
Examples¹⁰
of such misconduct allowing higher costs or extra costs, are dishonesty; perjur y or fraud;¹¹

R.10.33(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
interfering with opposing counsel’s cross-examination on afﬁdavit, by interrupting, answering for
the witness, coaching the witness, or reframing the question, though carefully warned, with case
law;¹
continuing until a very late stage (after all cross-examination and argument) to insist on
sloppy incorrect information on an important point, despite early explanation and demonstration,
under oath, by the opponent of the inaccuracy;²
refusing to participate in dispute resolution;³
notarizing pseudolegal papers;⁴
disclosing very bulky ﬁnancial information long outstanding only
a week before the scheduled hearing.⁵
Even if discovery or disclosure is not refused or is not falsely claimed complete, very lengthy
delay in giving or completing discovery or disclosure, with no good explanation, is also litigation
conduct founding costs penalties or enhancement or denial.⁶
For years costs for failure to admit what a notice to admit called for was dealt with in a separate
Rule, but since 2010, R.10.33(2) (b) has given the costs penalty, which courts should not hesitate
to apply, without ﬁnding excuse for the non-admission.⁷
A new allegation of fraudulent concealment to bypass a limitations defence ﬁrst raised on
appeal, seems to have been a ground to raise costs from the 1½ x column 5 at trial, to double
column 5 on appeal.⁸
Where each side claimed that the other was guilty of litigation misconduct (largely in the trial
court) and both parties were very aggressive and made strong accusations and thus made the
litigation more complex, that was an additional reason for trebling column C, but not for any further
costs adjustment.⁹
One reason for no costs was failure to ﬁle a quantiﬁcation of the costs ordered by the trial
judge, as the Court asked before hearing of the application in question.¹⁰
Having no lawyer is no excuse for litigation misconduct, especially repeated misconduct.¹¹
Whether to increase costs for misconduct is owed deference on appeal.¹²
One of the principal aims of the Rules of Court is to prevent multiplicity of proceedings.
Therefore, where two proceedings are conducted where one would do, or some steps are
unnecessary,¹³
costs may be used as a remedy.

10-119
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.33(2)
The Surrogate Rules give additional grounds for extra costs for misconduct.¹
Demanding
formal proof of a will in a trial is misconduct where if the will were invalid, that would have no
effect, as the will gave exactly the same result as would an intestacy.²
3. Examples of Usual Standard of Costs
But changing a company’s constitution and so beneﬁtting oneself is not such misconduct as
warrants higher costs, if the change was made long before suit was brought, and ﬁling with the
government had been simply neglected.³
The fact that a trial judge preferred the evidence of one party over the other’s, does not
necessarily mean that the latter gave false evidence, nor tried to deceive the court.⁴
Conversely,
no rule bars enhanced costs for litigation misconduct based on credibility ﬁndings, especially if it
is of an unusual and high degree, beyond the trial judge’s simply accepting the evidence of one
witness over others.⁵
Accusations of misconduct will affect the costs only if the misconduct is in the litigation, and
the accusations are proved or readily ascertainable.⁶
And a mere passing reference in evidence to
fraud, not pursued or argued, is not a ground to increase (or alter) costs.⁷
Allegations of fraud or
the like, not scandalous in wording, and whose substance the court had not yet ruled on, did not
justify full indemnity costs.⁸
Mild slackness or poor drafting was not bad enough to increase costs.⁹
Merely losing a lawsuit does not constitute misconduct,¹⁰
but later in the lawsuit the strong
probability of losing one or more issues, especially in a case not very complex, may be evidence
of substantive unreasonableness, and doubly so if very large sums are claimed, especially in light
of R.10.33(2) on denial or refusal to admit.¹¹

R.10.33(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
When setting costs in a family or divorce matter, after a trial, the court should not look into
whether there was misconduct when an earlier Emergency Protection Order was obtained, as
no “chill” on such proceedings should be imposed, especially if it was some years before and
memories are no longer fresh.¹
Here are a number of examples of behavior which is not misconduct. Asserting a wrong
interpretation of a contract.²
An unproven plea of conspiracy if conduct of the defendant at ﬁrst
made conspiracy seem likely.³
Refusal to agree to judicial dispute resolution.⁴
Not giving ongoing
oral discovery, and not warning before trial that one’s evidence would change.⁵
Not forcing the
other side to prove a fact not originally in issue, not prolonging the trial, nor creating other delays,
nor concealing material evidence, nor failing to produce things in a timely manner, without deceit,
intimidation, nor misrepresentation.⁶
Litigating over whether a plan of arrangement extinguished
a claim.⁷
Misguided motions and irrelevant cross-examination and irrelevant submissions.⁸
A late
failed request for a third party notice.⁹
That a motion should have gone to an afternoon special,
even if the judge also had concerns about that, where neither party suggested that, and instead
both were willing to argue the application on the merits then.¹⁰
Beating the opponent's offer by only a slight amount does not affect costs, except in exceptional
circumstances.¹¹
4. Amount of Costs for Misconduct
Such higher costs should at least be enough to indemnify fully the innocent party’s expenses
directly caused by the wrongdoing; and they may be more, intended to denounce or punish the
wrongdoer. In one case it was the full lawyer’s bill for time in court, and half his bill for preparation.¹²
Since the misconduct does not typically operate throughout the lawsuit, often it leads to a lump-
sum increase in the costs otherwise payable, but not to full indemnity costs for the whole suit.¹³
There should not be a reward of costs plus something else.¹⁴
Even if it is proper to order full indemnity costs, some adjustments to the legal bill incurred by
the innocent and winning party may be needed, and how to verify the bills’ overall reasonableness
may involve more time and effort than it is worth. Where the bills were very lengthy and detailed
and stretched over about 6 1/2 years, and the losing party did not question those accounts, and

5. Failure to Admit
An important but often overlooked Rule is R.10.33(2) (b). Defendants very often deny
everything, and make the plaintiff prove what the defendant knows is clearly true. The Rule
authorizes a special costs award in such cases.¹¹
Even if the pleadings are ﬁne on their face, and
even if there was no notice to admit under R.6.37, at the end of trial a losing party can ask for
costs of part of the trial when the opposing party has denied things which turn out to have been
plainly correct and should have been admitted.¹²

10-122
R.10.33(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
For example, often a plaintiff sues for goods or services sold and delivered, but the defendant
proves at trial that they were faulty and so the amount owing is greatly reduced or even eliminated.
Usually the defendant has denied the contract and delivery of the goods in his pleadings, and
made the plaintiff prove that. The plaintiff should cite R.10.33(2) (b) and get costs of proving the
contract and delivery.¹
It is proper to penalize a defendant’s refusal to admit a very plain case,²
even if the defendant is represented by an insurer.³
A liability insurer will often maintain a denial
of liability up to trial, when liability is very clear, and only quantum is in issue, and that should be
more frequently penalized in costs.
6. Expenses Caused Without Fault
A party may have to pay costs of adjournments which it caused, even if no one was at fault.⁴
Despite R.10.33(2) (c), two sets of costs were appropriate, though both proceedings had the
same aim (spare an afﬁant cross-examination), because they were by two separate law ﬁrms and
on somewhat different issues.⁵
7. Who Pays Costs Penalty
Rule 10.49 expressly lets the court order one of the lawyers to pay costs personally.⁶
And the
common law gives the court a broad power to order anyone to pay costs, even though he or she
is not a party to the suit.⁷
That may be done:
(a) where that non-party is the person ultimately liable⁸
(e. g. on a chain of contracts), or
(b) where he or she was the real instigator of the litigation and the one which would have
received the fruits,⁹
(c) where the named parties were ﬁctitious;¹⁰
or
(d) where the non-party was guilty of serious misconduct relating to the suit.¹¹

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.34(1)
Courts have approved items (a) to (c), but point out that they are not an exhaustive list.¹
Costs against a lawyer personally, at the end of a suit, are unusual, and not a topic with
wide discretion. Serious misconduct or seriously undermining the court’s authority or serious
interference with the administration of justice, is needed. Unfounded frivolous or vexatious
proceedings have to denote a serious abuse. A mere error is not enough, unless there is gross
neglect or inaccuracy, or a marked and unacceptable departure from the standard or reasonable
conduct for a lawyer.²
Before such a ﬁnding and award, there must of course be notice to the non-party, and a
chance to argue against his or her liability.³
Seventy-ﬁve percent of the costs awarded to one winning party and 25% of the costs to
another winning party were to be jointly and severally payable by the losing party’s counsel. That
is exceptional, but there was serious misconduct. Counsel had lots of notice, by correspondence
and then a written brief for costs, almost 2 years before. And these lawyers had seen that the
misconduct complained of was theirs.⁴
A non-proﬁt body which voluntarily took over litigation which a private person had abandoned,
the body wishing the court to set some rule of law, but lost, had to pay almost full indemnity costs.⁵
Court-ordered Assessment of Costs
10.34(1) The Court may order an assessment of costs by an assessment ofﬁcer
and may give directions to the assessment ofﬁcer about the assessment.
(2) The Court must keep a record on the court ﬁle of a direction
(a) given to an assessment ofﬁcer,
(b) requested by a party and refused by the Court, or
(c) requested by a party that the Court declines to make but leaves to an
assessment ofﬁcer’s discretion.⁶

Subdivision 2
Assessment of Costs

Preparation of Bill of Costs
10.35(1) A party entitled to payment of costs must prepare a bill of costs in Form⁴⁴
(a) if that party wishes or is required to have the costs assessed by an
assessment ofﬁcer, or
(b) on request of a party who is required to pay the costs.
(2) The bill of costs must
(a) itemize all the costs sought to be recovered, distinguishing between
fees, disbursements and other charges, and
(b) be signed by the person responsible for its preparation.

Related Provisions
9.35 (costs in foreclosures) ; 10.2 (contents of solicitor-client account)

See also R.10.37 and notes. On timing, see also R.10.30 and notes.
If a party seeks costs, especially full-indemnity costs, at the beginning of the review by a
review ofﬁcer it must provide a bill of costs, a statement of account, or other evidence of the
amounts claimed.²
Absence of a bill harms the party asked to pay, especially if the actual legal
bills incurred by the party seeking costs are argued as some basis for calculating these party-
party costs now sought.³
Without such a bill, actual expenses incurred cannot be considered.⁴
Computation of Schedule C and full indemnity should be provided.⁵
A bill of costs between parties must follow the prescribed form. However, if fees are to be on a
solicitor-client basis, the services should be itemized, but the fee may be a single lump-sum fee,
and then the form of account lawyers use to bill clients is probably satisfactory.
For party-party costs, if the court set no other basis for costs and said nothing to the contrary,
the bill of costs should itemize the services according to the items in Schedule C, giving the item
numbers from there. The bill should have room in the margins and at the end, for the assessment
ofﬁcer to change numbers and recompute totals.⁶

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.36(1)
This bill of costs would be an appendix or enclosure to the costs argument, not to have its
pages counted in the judge’s limit for the length of written argument on costs.¹
Post-judgment interest runs on costs awarded in a judgment, but it is not itself costs, and such
interest should not be included in the bill of costs.²
This Rule no longer requires proof of disbursements by an afﬁdavit or certiﬁcate by the lawyer.
Items which are mingled fee and disbursements, such as photocopying at 30 cents a page, are
difﬁcult. It is traditional to allow such mingled items, especially as the precise cost per page is
hard to determine and may ﬂuctuate. But they violate the requirement to distinguish fees and
disbursements; it is necessary to avoid misrepresentation, and to disclose that they include a fee
element, and not pass them off as a pure disbursement. See further R.10.31n.
The need to sign a bill has been interpreted laxly.³
The winning party need not prepare a bill of costs if the only costs sought are the presumptive
costs applicable to the steps recognized under Schedule C, and the only objection is that there
should be no costs.⁴
Assessment of Bill of Costs
10.36(1) After a judgment or order has been entered, an assessment ofﬁcer, in
accordance with any Court direction or order, may make an assessment of costs
payable in accordance with rule 10.41 [Assessment officer’s decision] in any of

default];

under rule 10.41 [Assessment officer’s decision] as payable by one party to
another must be determined by way of an appointment with an assessment
ofﬁcer unless the Court or the assessment ofﬁcer otherwise permits.
(3) Despite subrules (1) and (2), if one party approves a bill of costs prepared by
another party adverse in interest, an assessment ofﬁcer must certify the bill of
costs under rule 10.43 [Certification of costs payable], without change.⁵

A tariff of fees and an agreement with the sheriffs’ ofﬁce regulate the fees and expenses which

executor or trustee. Some expenses may be found legitimate without a voucher, and others may
be found not justiﬁed, and the fees of the executor or trustee may be reduced.⁴
Appointment for Assessment
10.37(1) A party entitled to payment of costs may obtain from an assessment
ofﬁcer an appointment date for an assessment of costs.⁵
(2) If a party entitled to payment of costs makes the appointment, that party
must, 10 days or more before the appointment date,
(a) ﬁle a proposed bill of costs, and
(b) serve every party affected by the appointment with notice of the
appointment date in form 45 and the proposed bill of costs.⁶
(3) If any other party obtains an appointment date, that party must
(a) 20 days or more before the appointment date, serve notice of the
appointment date in Form 45 on every party affected, and
(b) serve on the party entitled to payment of costs a request that the
entitled party prepare a proposed bill of costs.

Related Provisions
13.5 (extending time)

When the court awards costs between parties but does not specify their exact amount, the
procedure is as follows. The lawyer seeking costs draws up a bill of costs.¹
It is traditional to send
the draft bill of costs to the lawyer for the payor and ask him or her to agree as to the amount.
Often negotiation results. It is not necessary to wait a long time or reach agreement under R.
10.36(3). The payee can get a date from the assessment ofﬁcer for a hearing to approve or vary
the bill of costs, print up a formal appointment ﬁxing that day, have the Clerk sign and seal it, and
serve it on the payor’s lawyer. The other party has no right to object to large swathes of dates for
that hearing and so postpone the hearing unduly.²
Before an assessment of party-party costs, the payor need not give advance notice that he will
object that the proposed costs would exceed full indemnity.³
Assessment Ofﬁcer’s Authority
10.38(1) For the purpose of assessing costs payable, an assessment ofﬁcer may
do all or any of the following:
(a) take evidence either by afﬁdavit or orally under oath, or both;
(b) direct the production of records;
(c) require notice of the appointment for the assessment to be served
on persons who may be affected by the assessment or who have an
interest in the trust, estate, fund or property from which the costs are
or may be paid or charged;
(d) give directions about how a notice of the appointment for the
assessment is to be served;
(e) allow a party to be independently represented by a lawyer;
(f) require details of the services provided and disbursements or other
charges claimed or require information about any other matter
necessary to understand the reason for an item in the bill of costs
and to decide whether the item and charge is reasonable and proper;
(g) validate service of the notice of the appointment or, if service is
impractical or impossible, dispense with service.⁴

On the general principles of regulating party-party costs, see Alta. Treas. case.¹
A Review Ofﬁcer hearing a dispute about a legal bill has power to take evidence, even unsworn
evidence. An allegation that the retainer (for an hourly charge) had been suspended and that
further work was free, is a dispute about amount, and does not need a reference to the court
under R.10.18(1) (a).²
Under Rr.10.38 and 10.39 the assessment ofﬁcer can make a reference to the court for a
decision or direction on the reasonableness of costs or level of costs, and this was done and
commented on by the referee (a Master).³
At one time a certiﬁcate of disbursements was needed, but even then its absence was not fatal,
and was readily curable if receipts are available, as they are better evidence and the assessment
ofﬁcer can (and often does) look at them.⁴
After 2010, a Master (as referee) held that invoices or
other proof of disbursements should automatically be provided with the proposed bill of costs.⁵
The review ofﬁcer cannot decide the terms of a retainer agreement, including how to interprete
it, nor decide on implied retainer agreements; they must go to a judge to decide, if contested.⁶

the reference.

Related Provisions
10.31(4) (set off of costs)

A. References
For the general principles of assessment, see R.10.29 n. A, and R.10.31 n. E. On inﬂation and
the obsolescence of the amounts in Schedule C, see R.10.31n. E.3.
B. Reasonableness
The word “reasonable” can refer to different things. Before a taxing officer it means whether
the expense seemed desirable at the time and not too large, given the litigation existing and all
its issues and implications. Whether the costs are payable under a Rule or because a justice or
Applications Judge ordered costs, is not subject to a reasonableness test, even usually before the
justice or Applications Judge. Costs usually go to the winner, even if the loser’s case was arguable
and his or her conduct reasonable.³
Subrule (b) (iii) about unnecessary work or expenditures also applies to full-indemnity, for only
reasonable work is usually indemniﬁed.⁴
C. Picking a Column or Level
For column of costs, only two numbers are material: the amount of money which the plaintiff’s
pleadings claim, and the amount which the plaintiff actually recovers. Costs need to be based
on something certain, not “representations throughout the litigation process”, especially as that
would waste post-trial time hunting “through transcripts and submissions for materially inconsistent
damages characterizations”.⁵
This Rule lets the assessment ofﬁcer double costs because of an offer bettered, under R.
4.29.⁶

R.10.41(5) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
It is customary to multiply the top column (5) in complex actions about very large amounts, and
in one case it was tripled. At one time, some percentage of the winning party’s actual legal bills
used to be thought an unreliable benchmark for party-party costs, though a useful cross check,
but no longer.¹
A long hard complex suit important to both sides likely has a big indirect amount in
issue, for costs purposes.²
See also R.10.33 and commentary above on that Rule.
If contributory negligence reduces the plaintiff’s award, the trial judge can use the lower
column appropriate to the net award, or can reduce costs for split result; but not both, which would
be double deduction.³
The costs in the case of a counterclaim on two competing contracts were
based on the net result of dismissing the claim on one and allowing the other.⁴
A contract about costs between one of the parties to this suit, and a non-party, is not relevant.⁵
A judge awarded solicitor-client costs for moving to set aside Anton Piller and injunction
orders, but that did not extend to the level of costs of a motion to quantify or particularize costs as
that is usually a stand-alone step?⁶
D. Miscellaneous
There will be no double costs for a step at trial taken but once, even if two unconsolidated suits
were then tried together.⁷
If a case management conference is much more like a special chambers application, then the
$250 in Schedule C is not enough and a larger fee is proper.⁸
Schedule C names $5, 000 for written ﬁnal argument, but if it was useful, that may well be too
low, and $15, 000 was awarded here.⁹
A default judgment for debt “and costs to be taxed” means ordinary party-party costs, and the
clerk (who gave default judgment) had no jurisdiction to order more.¹⁰
Agreeing with some of the opponent’s submissions is evidence of reasonableness, not
misfeasance.¹¹
If there are multiple parties, then set-off may involve apportioning costs.¹²
Whether two parties
on one side had overlapping argument or needed separate argument is not central, as the analysis
apparently starts with what is a proportionate amount to award to the winning party, and then

Actions Within the Court of Justice Jurisdiction
10.42(1) This rule applies only to actions the subject matter of which is within the

(2) Despite anything in this Division or Schedule C [Tariff of Recoverable Fees],

R.10.43(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.

Related Provisions
Schedule C (Tariff of Fees)

The ceiling for civil suits in the Alberta Court of Justice went up to $50, 000 in August 2014, but
the change was not retroactive.¹
Rule 10.42(2) speaks of the Alberta Court of Justice monetary
ceiling when the King's Bench suit began, not when it ended or was tried.²
But the Regulation
setting the new jurisdiction of the Provincial Court puts it up to $100 thousand only.³
The 75% ceiling in R.10.42(2) (a) is not a ﬁxed amount; the court can award a sum less than
75% of Division 2 of Schedule C. Proportionality is relevant under R.1.2(4).⁴
$7, 500 costs, not $45, 000, were awarded to the successful respondent on a probably-hopeless
appeal eventually abandoned after some procedural acrimony and difﬁculties.⁵
On appeal from Alberta Court of Justice to King’s Bench, the items in Schedule C for appeals
(items 13, 14, 15) should not generally be used. Alberta Court of Justice’s tariff is not suitable
either.⁶
It is irrelevant that the amount recovered is less than the ceiling amount for Alberta Court of
Justice, if the subject matter is outside that court’s jurisdiction.⁷
Certiﬁcation of Costs Payable
10.43(1) An assessment ofﬁcer’s decision must be given by an interim or ﬁnal
certiﬁcate, which may be endorsed on a bill of costs, and which must
(a) certify the amount to be paid by each party or person,
(b) certify any special circumstance and the amount to be paid by each
party or person with respect to the special circumstance, and
(c) be dated and signed by the assessment ofﬁcer.⁸
(2) A certiﬁcate that meets the requirements stated in subrule (1) is conclusive
proof of the amount that a party or person who had notice of the assessment
must pay.⁹

Appeal from Assessment Ofﬁcer’s Decision
Appeal to Judge
10.44(1) A party to an assessment ofﬁcer’s decision under this Division or a

assessment of costs and corrections] may appeal the decision to a judge.
(2) The appeal from an assessment ofﬁcer’s decision is an appeal on the record

(3) The record of proceedings is
(a) Form 45 served under rule 10.37(2),
(b) the material the parties ﬁled to support or oppose, or that was
required for, the assessment,
(c) the transcript of the proceedings before the assessment ofﬁcer,
unless the judge waives this requirement, and
(d) the assessment ofﬁcer’s certiﬁcate.
(4) The appellant must ﬁle and serve on the respondent to the appeal, within one
month after the date of the assessment ofﬁcer’s decision,
(a) notice of the appeal in Form 46,
(b) the record of proceedings described in subrule (3) or, if the transcript
is not available at the time of ﬁling, conﬁrmation that the transcript
of the proceedings has been ordered, and
(c) any further written argument.
(5) The respondent to the appeal must, within 10 days after service of the notice
of appeal, ﬁle and serve on the appellant any written argument the respondent
wishes to make.

10-136
R.10.45(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Extending time to appeal from an Assessment Ofﬁcer is governed by Cairns v. Cairns (1931),
and the time limit is very short. But the onus on the appellant is heavy. No timely intent to appeal
was shown here, though the appeal was arguable, and lack of prejudice was not enough to extend
time. Five unexplained months elapsed, and a self-represented party cannot just ignore time
limits.¹
On an appeal from assessment of a party-party bill, the judge appealed to can look at the
assessed version of the bill of costs in question, if it is on the Court ﬁle.²
On appeal to a King’s Bench justice, a review ofﬁcer gets much deference because of his
experience and expertise, but some appeals do succeed: wrong principle, or amount far out of
line.³
Semble one Justice of Appeal can hear an appeal from an assessment ofﬁcer’s assessment
of costs awarded by a panel on an appeal.⁴
Decision of the Judge
10.45(1) After hearing an appeal from an assessment ofﬁcer’s decision, the
judge may, by order, do one or more of the following:
(a) conﬁrm, vary or revoke the decision;
(b) revoke the decision and substitute a decision;
(c) revoke all or part of the decision and refer the matter back to the
same or another assessment ofﬁcer;
(d) make any other order the judge considers appropriate.
(2) If the amount of costs payable as originally assessed by the assessment
ofﬁcer has been paid and, after payment, is reduced on appeal, the judge hearing
the appeal may order the return of the excess by the party who has received it
and the order may be enforced as an order of the Court.⁵
[Alta. Reg.124/10;163/10]

An appeal from an Assessment Ofﬁcer is on the record and gets deference because of the

Review and Assessment Under Enactments
10.46(1) If an enactment requires or authorizes an amount to be considered,
taxed, assessed or reviewed under these rules, a review ofﬁcer or an assessment
ofﬁcer, as the circumstances require, must consider, tax, assess or review the
amount
(a) in accordance with the enactment, and
(b) in accordance with any of these rules that apply or that can be
applied or should be applied by analogy.
(2) If an enactment requires or authorizes both lawyers’ charges and other costs
of proceedings to be considered, taxed, assessed or reviewed under these rules,
a review ofﬁcer must perform the function
(a) in accordance with the enactment, and
(b) in accordance with any of these rules that apply or that can be
applied or should be applied by analogy.
(3) A review ofﬁcer or assessment ofﬁcer acting under this rule has all the powers
that the ofﬁcer has in carrying out a review or an assessment of costs under this
Part in addition to any powers that the ofﬁcer has under the enactment.
(4) A decision of a review ofﬁcer or an assessment ofﬁcer may be appealed
under rule 10.26 [Appeal to judge] or 10.44 [Appeal to judge], as circumstances
permit, and rule 10.27 [Decision of the judge] or 10.45 [Decision of the judge]
applies as the case requires.

Liability of Litigation Representative for Costs
10.47(1) A litigation representative for a plaintiff is liable to pay a costs award
against the plaintiff.
(2) A litigation representative for a defendant is not liable to pay a costs award
against the defendant unless
(a) the litigation representative has engaged in serious misconduct,
and
(b) the Court so orders.
Information Note
For rules about litigation representatives see Part 2 [The Parties to Litigation] Division 2
[Litigation Representatives].

R.10.48(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.

Related Provisions
2.11 (litigation representatives) ; 2.14(2) (f) (acknowledgment of liability for costs).

In complex receivership, where the court had to supervise, the court refused to allocate costs
based on the proportion of debt owed to each creditor to the total debt owed by the debtor. That
proposal would have made the creditor who would get the least from the option bear the largest
proportion of costs. The two secured creditors should not bear more than their pro rata share of
allocated costs.¹
The litigation representative of a minor plaintiff, the named plaintiff in a class action by minors,
got exemption from paying costs of the upcoming certiﬁcation motion.²
Recovery of Goods and Services Tax
10.48(1) Unless the Court otherwise orders, a party entitled to a costs award is
entitled to an additional amount on account of goods and services tax³
(a) on the fees portion of the costs award, and
(b) on those disbursements, if any, that are taxable supplies under the
Excise Tax Act (Canada).
(2) Notwithstanding subrule (1), no additional amount on account of goods and
services tax is recoverable where the tax is refundable or rebateable pursuant to
the Excise Tax Act (Canada).⁴
[Alta. Reg.124/10;140/13]

Related Provisions
10.31 (costs awards)

What is popularly called the goods and services tax, or "GST", is a tax levied under the federal
Excise Tax Act. (In many provinces, it is called the “harmonized sales tax” or “HST”. )
Rule 10.48 used to require an afﬁdavit saying that the GST is actually payable by the person
beneﬁtting from a costs award,⁵
but the court could waive that.⁶
That requirement has now been
taken out of the Rule, but a form replaces that. Form 44 (Writ of Enforcement) contains a warranty
that the recipient of party-party costs is not entitled to reimbursement of GST from anyone else.
That warranty has to be present and signed, to add GST to the costs.⁷
Most businesses are
entitled to that reimbursement from Revenue Canada, and so cannot add GST to party-party
costs.

Penalty for Contravening Rules
10.49(1) The Court may order a party, lawyer or other person to pay to the court

(a) a settlement of the action, or
(b) an agreement to the contrary by the parties.⁶

R.10.49(2) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.

Related Provisions
1.5 (non-compliance) ; 2.25 (duties of lawyer) ; 3.35(1) (consent orders) ; 3.68(3) (striking out

pleadings); 5.12 (penalty for failure to serve affidavit of records) ; 10.33 (costs for misconduct) ;
10.50 (costs imposed on lawyer) ; 10.52(contempt) ; 14.90 (sanctions on appeals)
This Rule is designed to provide a penalty for misconduct where the opposing party is
complicit, or is not harmed.¹
Mere failure to follow Rules etc. is not enough; interference with
the administration of justice is needed.²
Are procedural safeguards akin to those for contempt,
needed?³
An example would be an agreement to call a long trial a short one, to get an early trial date,
with resulting harm to other litigants when the trial runs over time. An adjournment of a special
chambers hearing because of late ﬁling of an afﬁdavit led to costs jointly against the counsel and
his client.⁴
Rule 3.3 requires commencement in the judicial centre speciﬁed, without waiting for the
defendant to complain. The court can transfer the suit to the right judicial centre on its own motion,
with costs under R.10.49.⁵
This Rule was used to make each counsel pay $250.00 to the Registrar for failure to transcribe
recorded reasons of the judge appealed from, in two appeals.⁶
Costs were assessed for not
speaking to the appeal list in a case involving children.⁷
And for causing a judicial dispute resolution
session to be cancelled at the last minute.⁸
And for not disclosing when getting an order nisi of
foreclosure, commencement in the wrong judicial district.⁹
This Rule was used for repeatedly
disobeying court orders and ﬁling forbidden documents,¹⁰
and for three times letting a criminal
appeal be dismissed and getting three restorations.¹¹
There is a review of the previous cases using
this Rule.¹²
This Rule does not apply to one side’s failure to pay costs previously awarded to the other
party.¹³
This Rule cannot be used to order a payment to another party.¹⁴
This Rule might allow a daily penalty.¹⁵
Misconduct by a solicitor led to a $1, 000 penalty under
R.10.49, plus costs of 4 times column 1 of Schedule C to the opposing party.¹⁶

10-141
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.50
Rule 10.49 was used (plus costs to the innocent party) in light of R.1.2, where court directions
were disobeyed but contempt could not be found for evidentiary and technical reasons.¹
One issue raised had already been decided against this would-be appellant in this litigation,
which was a collateral attack. This self-represented litigant was previously warned about
relitigating the same issues and was doing it again. A penalty of $2500 was ordered paid to the
Clerk of the Court under R.10.49(1). If this litigant persisted in that, the penalties would probably
grow.²
A litigant kept expressly rejecting advice from court ofﬁcials about how and where to appeal
a decision of a chambers judge, and used insults and threats and did not comply with even the
simplest requirements. Court resources are not inﬁnite. So $2000 penalty for contravening rules
under R.10.49(1) was levied.³
Lawyers “notarizing” odd pseudolegal documents for self-represented litigants have had to
pay costs under this Rule.⁴
If the offending party is the government, ordering costs to the Clerk or Registrar is pointless,
and the court may order costs to the opposing party instead.⁵
“Costs” or “security for costs” usually refer to moneys payable by one party to another (for
litigation expenses, not to a sum imposed under R.10.49 and payable to the Clerk of the Court),
and so payment of R.10.49 awards has no priority in payment out of an existing fund held as
security for costs.⁶
Costs Imposed on Lawyer
10.50 If a lawyer for a party engages in serious misconduct, the Court may order
the lawyer to pay a costs award with respect to a person named in the order.

Related Provisions
10.28 (”party”) ; 10.29 (costs awards)

A. Introduction
There are 3 different ways in which a solicitor might become personally liable for costs in a suit
without being a party:
(i) By acting for a party which does not exist, or is not competent (because of infancy or
mental defect), or has not given proper authority e. g. because of bankruptcy.⁷

Footnote
1 C. J. D. v. R. I. J.2018 ABQB 287, JCE 4803 127885 (Apr 12).
2 C. I. B. C. v. Hayden (#_) 2021 ABQB 985, JCC 2101 08260 (Dec 10) (¶’s 17-25) ; Royal Bank of Can. v.
Anderson (#_) 2023 ABKB 24, JCC 2101 07946 (Jan 13) (repealed pseudolegal doctrines).
3 McMunn v. Hok (#_) 2021 ABQB 601, JCGP 2104 00343 (Jul 29). For similar examples, see Royal Bank v.
Anderson, supra; Royal Bank v. Anderson (#_) 2022 ABKB 838, JCC 2101 07946 (Dec 16) (¶’s 5-9).
4 R. v. Ayyazi 2022 ABKB 836, JCC 2000 85314 Q1 (Dec 16) (¶’s 63-64) ; Royal Bank v. Anderson (#_) 2022
ABKB 838, JCC 2101 07946 (Dec 16) (¶’s 10-18).
5 Brick Prot’n. Corp. v. R.2011 ABCA 214, 510 AR 336 (¶ 65).
6 Rana v. Rana (#_) 2024 ABKB 326, JCC 1501 023369 (Jun 3) (¶’s 12-18).
7 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 4, Part K; dicta in Papaschase F. N. v. McLeod (#2) 2021 ABQB 698, JCE 1903
21547 (Aug 31) (¶’s 12-16). But taking merely oral instructions from clients abroad is not enough to give
liability: Re Nakonechna 2004 ABCA 54, 354 AR 4 (one JA).

10-142
R.10.50 PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
(ii) By being the real instigator of the suit and person for whose beneﬁt it is run.¹
(iii) By serious misconduct in prosecuting or defending a suit for a client.²
This remedy comes
from the court's inherent power over solicitors as ofﬁcers of the court.³
B. General Principle
An order that a solicitor pay costs personally is rarely given, and only infrequently does the
question whether to impose it arise. Only serious misconduct can justify personal costs, especially
as a lawyer has a duty to keep his instructions conﬁdential and advance even unpopular causes
with courage, and the court should not make fear of costs conﬂict with those fundamental duties
of an advocate.⁴
The courts should be cautious in making a costs order personally against counsel.⁵
But
contempt is not necessary for personal liability.⁶
Unfounded accusations of serious misconduct

proceedings, failure to turn up in court, or to return phone calls, and (probably) for telling his client to disobey
a court order: McNamara v. McNamara 2007 ABQB 51. $1, 000 for absenting self from long-scheduled
appeal and going incommunicado: Trang v. Dir. of Edm. Remand Centre, supra; Adams v. Adams (#2)

1103 14112 (Jul 12) (¶’s 67 ff. ). But then the Court of Appeal reiterated that it is very difﬁcult to get costs
against a lawyer personally if the lawyer is not a party to the lawsuit, and the misconduct by the lawyer must
be very bad, and a mere mistake or error short of gross neglect or inaccuracy is not enough, though the
range of circumstances where it might be ordered is wide: 1985 Sawridge Tr. v. Pub. T’ee.2019 ABCA 243,
[2019] 10 WWR 604, 35 CPC(8th) 225, 91 Alta LR(6th) 111, revg 2017 ABQB 530. Though the decision
2017 ABQB 530 was reversed, it does contain a considerable citation of previous case law.

been careless but not dishonest, the lawyer for the careless party was not ordered to pay costs personally:
Time Logistics v. Haworth (#2) (M) 2007 ABQB 633. Costs personally against a solicitor are a parellel

10-143
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.50
by the opposing party or her lawyer, especially both of them and their witnesses, and continual
intimidation, are a real problem in litigation. Though the aim is to compensate the injured party, not
to punish the solicitor, misconduct by the solicitor is necessary.¹
It is not enough that it is unlikely
that the solicitor's client would ever pay the costs.²
The court can impose personal costs against a solicitor for abuse of process, frivolous
proceedings, misconduct, dishonesty, or actions taken for ulterior motives whose effect is seriously
to undermine court authority or seriously to interfere with the administration of justice.³
Persistent,
unfounded, vexatious litigation instigated by the solicitor deliberately or by gross negligence or
with grossly disproportionate claims, may sufﬁce, especially if coupled with other misconduct.⁴
That high threshold is met if counsel conducted an unfounded, frivolous, dilatory or vexatious
proceeding denoting a serious abuse of the legal system, or dishonest or malicious conduct by
him that is deliberate. He cannot knowingly use judicial resources for a purely dilatory purpose in
order solely and in a calculated manner to obstruct the orderly conduct of the judicial process.⁵
A lawyer had to pay personally a lump sum said to replace full indemnity costs, for getting the
unrepresented opponent's approval of any draft order dispensed with, and then drafting a formal
order omitting the judge's conditions limiting the order to a certain time period.⁶
Personal liability
was not imposed where counsel's undoubted misconduct was thought probably carelessness,
without intent to deceive.⁷
Litigation misconduct can be bad enough for full-indemnity costs, yet not bad enough for
personal liability of counsel for those costs, e. g. if the conduct was careless but not intended to
mislead.⁸
Personal penalties against counsel in criminal cases are different from civil, as costs in
criminal cases are purely punitive, not at all compensatory, and a defence counsel has no duty to
encourage compromise or agreement, nor to help the Crown conduct its case.⁹
That the solicitor had a contingency fee agreement is not enough to show that the solicitor was
motivated by his or her ﬁnancial stake in the suit.¹⁰
[Footnote 6 continued from prior page]
remedy with contempt (and Law Society penalties) and it is possible that both (or all 3) could be imposed for
the same misconduct; Dir. v. Jodoin, supra at ¶ 20. The procedure for personal costs is less strict, which is
a relevant consideration: id. at ¶’s 22-23. And usually personal costs are slighter penalty: id. at ¶ 24.

10-144
R.10.50 PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
The Alberta Court of Justice’s statutory power to give costs is not conﬁned to costs against
named parties, and includes costs against solicitors personally.¹
Mistakes by the impugned
solicitor in drafting formal orders which were either reasonable, pardonable, slight negligence, or
even non-existent, were not enough for personal costs.²
Even when acting for herself, a lawyer owes a higher duty to observe procedures, on penalty
of higher costs.³
C. Procedure
Before costs are awarded against a solicitor personally, though the process can be ﬂexible,
there should be notice of the allegations and possible consequences, including information about
alleged facts and evidence, and enough time to respond and lead contrary evidence.⁴
The standard of proof for personal costs against a solicitor is the balance of probabilities, the
solicitor is not a "person charged with an offence", and ss.7, 11 of the Charter do not apply.⁵
A
proceeding to impose personal costs on a solicitor must not put on trial the lawyer's disciplinary
record, still less his career. Generally the only relevant facts are those of the suit or prosecution
before the court. But facts from other cases can be used to determine the solicitor's intention in
this case (such as good or bad faith), and to test the knowledge of the lawyer, e. g. as to what
is forbidden or unfounded, semble if such evidence is of high probative value and has strong
similarity to the facts alleged in this case.⁶
Mere inferences, and hearsay upon hearsay had no
effect, in the Washburn case.⁷
However, if the offending lawyer's client is solvent, whether the costs liability is personal on
the solicitor may matter little in the end result. If the misconduct was not the client's fault and
was the solicitor's neglect (or worse), then the client will seek reimbursement of any costs for
the bad actions which the client had to pay to the opponent, and the courts or the Law Society
will likely make the solicitor reimburse his ex-client.⁸
If the counsel's client wants counsel to pay
them, such indemnity should be in another forum; in the main lawsuit, it would involve privileged
communications.⁹
Once the question arises of ordering costs personally against counsel, the client should be
allowed an adjournment so that counsel may consider his position and whether anyone should
get independent legal counsel. A lawyer should not have to represent simultaneously his client
and himself.¹⁰

10-145
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.51
Subdivision 2
Civil Contempt of Court
Order to Appear
10.51 The Court may grant an order in Form 47 that requires a person to appear
before it, or may order a peace ofﬁcer to take a person into custody and to bring
the person before the Court, to show cause why that person should not be
declared to be in civil contempt of Court.¹

Related Provisions
10.53 (inherent jurisdiction)

These Rules speak of civil contempt, and so do the comments below. There is a closely allied
topic of criminal contempt which in theory is purely a common-law matter.²
On contempt generally,
see the textbooks.³
The Court of King's Bench and the Court of Appeal are superior courts, and therefore have
inherent power to punish contempt of court occurring before or in connection with them or any
other court in Alberta.⁴
There is a discussion of the common-law powers of inferior or administrative
tribunals over contempt.⁵
A statutory grant of the powers of superior courts extends to contempt
too.⁶
A Registrar in Bankruptcy has power, given by the Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act, to ﬁnd and
punish contempt outside the presence of the Court, so long as he or she does not order prison.
The word "commit" refers only to imprisonment.⁷
Canadian courts should be careful about enforcing foreign court orders by contempt orders for
disobeying them. Finality of the order, comity considerations, equitable discretion, and all the bars
to enforcing a domestic order, can all enter into it.⁸

R.10.52(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
The show-cause application under this Rule was reafﬁrmed as proper commencement
procedure.¹
An application for someone to show cause why he should not be held in contempt of
court sets up a two-stage process (which Rr.10.51 and 10.52 also set). So an order was issued
that the respondents appear and show cause why they should not be held in contempt.²
Though
the court has the power to force someone alleged to be in contempt to turn up in court, and can
arrest him if need be, arrest is rarely ordered for civil contempt.³
On freedom of speech and its impact on contempt, see the A. U. P. E. case.⁴
Declaration of Civil Contempt
10.52(1) Except when a person is before the Court as described in subrule (3) (a)
(ii) or (v), before an order declaring a person in civil contempt of Court is made,
notice of the application in Form 27 for a declaration of civil contempt must be
served on the person in the same manner as a commencement document.
(2) If a lawyer accepts service of a notice of an application seeking an order
declaring the lawyer’s client to be in civil contempt of Court, the lawyer must
notify the client of the notice as soon as practicable after being served.⁵
(3) A judge may declare a person to be in civil contempt of Court if
(a) the person, without reasonable excuse,
(i) does not comply with an order, other than an order to pay
money, that has been served in accordance with the rules
for service of commencement documents or of which the
person has actual knowledge,
(ii) is before the Court and engages in conduct that warrants a
declaration of civil contempt of Court,
(iii) does not comply with an order served on the person, or an
order of which the person has actual knowledge, to appear
before the Court to show cause why the person should not
be declared to be in civil contempt of Court,
(iv) does not comply with an order served on the person, or an
order of which the person has actual knowledge, to attend
for questioning under these rules or to answer questions the
person is ordered by a Court to answer,
(v) is a witness in an application or at trial and refuses to be
sworn or refuses to answer proper questions, or
(vi) does not perform or observe the terms of an undertaking
given to the Court,
or

A. Procedure and Object²
A vital step to maintain peace order and good government is the right and power of courts
to enforce their rulings, and maintain and protect the dignity and respect of the courts and their
proceedings. The power to do that by contempt proceedings is inherent. Compliance (coercion) is
one aim; so is punishment.³
The topic is important, because in many cases where any order has
been disobeyed, the real intention of the innocent party (and even of the court) is not to punish,
but to use contempt as the only real means of enforcing compliance with the order.⁴
Duties under a court order are owed to the court, not to the opponent.⁵
Rule 10.52(1), (2) deals with the procedure applicable on applications for contempt.⁶
There is
not much difference between the procedure on a contempt application and the procedure on any
other application.⁷
An application for someone to show cause why he or she should not be held in contempt of
court sets up a two-stage process (which Rr.10.51 and 10.52 also set. )⁸
Contempt applications should ordinarily be heard promptly.⁹
But usually great urgency is not
present, and then the Attorney-General or a party should start proceedings; the court itself should
not.¹⁰
In an urgent case such as a witness’ refusing to answer questions in open court, it will be

B. Notice
See also R.11.17. Alberta courts have held that care must be taken with notice in two respects:
1. Just as under administrative law or criminal procedure, the person accused of contempt
or who is possibly to be punished for it²
must have clear and precise advance notice of
exactly what wrong-doing is alleged against him or her;³
and a chance to respond with
excuses, argument or evidence;⁴
and
2. There must be personal notice of the application to that person. Of course it is sufﬁcient
that the party alleged to be in contempt get personal notice in some manner; it does not
have to be personal service to effect that.⁵
Service of contempt applications, other than
those in the face of the court, may occur:
a. as agreed in a contract;
b. by personal service or recorded mail addressed to the individual;
c. by service on an individual’s lawyer, if they are acting for the individual and
accept service in writing; or
d. by leaving a copy with the individual’s lawyer of record, ofﬁce or other designated
address, or by recorded mail to the lawyer at the lawyer’s ofﬁce (¶ 13) ;
A determination of whether notice was sufﬁcient for a contempt application is for the
judge hearing the application to decide.⁶
If counsel is served and shows up on the return
of the contempt motion, what he or she says about notice is admissible evidence, e. g.
that the notice was sent on to the client. The court may then infer that the client had
actual notice.⁷
Indeed, proof of service on the solicitor of record shifts the onus to the

C. Evidence
It is highly probable that civil contempt is considered a ﬁnal matter, so that the afﬁdavits
relied on to have the court ﬁnd contempt cannot contain hearsay.⁵
Double hearsay is likely to be
rejected.⁶
But certiﬁed copies of ofﬁcial documents, or proper secondary evidence of documents,
are permissible.⁷
The phrase “show cause” is misleading; the onus of proof is not on the person accused of
contempt.⁸
The party alleging contempt has the persuasive burden of proof, and the duty to give
or cite evidence, before the topic of reasonable excuse comes up.⁹
If contempt is shown, the onus lies on the offender to show that he has purged his contempt.¹⁰
Once the basic elements of contempt are proved, the evidential burden shifts to the accused
person to raise a reasonable doubt about some valid excuse for not obeying?¹¹
The precise nature
of the burden of proof about an adequate excuse for one's non-obedience is unclear.¹²
Even civil contempt must be proved beyond a reasonable doubt,¹³
as to both the actus reus and
mens rea.¹⁴
Proof that one knew of an order which one disobeyed, must be beyond a reasonable

R.10.52(3) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
doubt.¹
The party seeking contempt must prove disobedience of a clear unequivocal duty, known
to the person accused, and (ordinarily) intentional failure to comply.²
Contempt cannot be proved
by afﬁdavits which conﬂict on material questions.³
Reversal of onus may not be fatal if proof is
clear.⁴
The person accused of contempt cannot rely on mere factual assertions, and needs sworn
evidence.⁵
Like the party applying, he or she cannot rely on hearsay.⁶
The applicant should not plan or try to attack the respondent's credibility, yet have the
application to ﬁnd contempt heard in chambers without live evidence.⁷
But where the question is contempt in court, the Court of Appeal can act on its own knowledge
of that event.⁸
Admitting evidence potentially more prejudicial than probative, on a contempt hearing, is not
correct, but is not fatal, if the prejudicial parts were not relied on by the judge trying contempt, so
there was no effect on the result.⁹
Reasonable excuse for the accused person’s behavior is still a consideration and goes to guilt,
not just to liability.¹⁰
The reference in subrule (3) to “reasonable excuse” does remove either burden
of proof from the party alleging contempt. The court can presume that the accused person acted
knowing the facts, unless the latter gives some evidence, for only he or she knew what belief or
grounds for it he or she had.¹¹
D. Slips and Technicalities
A number of older cases suggested that the procedure for contempt is very strict, and that
small defects may derail the application. It is true that the matter is important, and that a court
should not put someone in prison where there may have been a serious procedural defect in the
process which got him or her there. But to ignore all the normal rules of civil procedure and the
normal slip Rules, and to rely on a technicality which in fact worked no real injustice, is wrong in
principle and ﬂouts many important Rules of Court. Why one would want to do that in favour of a
party who was (for example) disobeying a court order, and not do it for an honest litigant, is very
unclear.¹²
For example, notice how some curtailment of the right to cross-examine was not fatal in
the United Nurses case.¹³

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.52(3)
E. Discretion and Procedure
See also Parts L and M below.
Now in Alberta contempt of court is a legislated topic, not an equitable one, and its origins are
common law, even though R.10.52(3) uses the word “declaration”. It is a quasi-criminal offence.
Equitable principles do not apply.¹
A judge has a discretion not to proceed with a complaint of contempt of court.²
So a technical
contempt may lead to no ﬁnding of contempt if the disobedient person was in good faith and took
reasonable steps to comply.³
A chambers judge can refuse to proceed with a contempt motion
involving a receiver, where it is not the right way to decide the real dispute, which is whether the
receiver is entitled to do what he proposes to do.⁴
The courts are critical of contempt motions
brought on slight or technical grounds.⁵
Where misconduct is shown but falls short of proving
contempt, the court can make other orders necessary to achieve justice.⁶
The reference in R.10.52(3) to absence of “reasonable excuse” makes the contempt power
highly discretionary, and it should not be used routinely to get compliance with court orders, and
is a last resort, especially in family law cases, even more so if custody is involved?⁷
Going into bankruptcy does not absolve one from a duty to obey a court order, nor bar or
satisfy contempt proceedings.⁸
Nor is being arrested on the scene and jailed over the weekend
until release, punishment for contempt, nor a purging of contempt, nor double punishment.⁹
The court can rehear a contempt ﬁnding and set it aside.¹⁰
Much harm is done by trying to sever parts of a contempt application and hear them on
another date or by another procedure.¹¹
For one thing, delay is bad where orders are being ﬂouted,
even if some issues are complex, and doubly so if the contempts are ongoing or threatened with
repetition, not historical.¹²
Usually contempt hearings are split into liability and penalty phases, and once the ﬁrst is
ﬁnished, during the second it is usually too late to reopen the liability issue.¹³
Compliance with

F. Intent and Efforts
Only general mens rea is necessary for contempt;²
and for many types of contempt,
carelessness sufﬁces. For example, one ordered by a court to do something has a duty of diligence
and care to observe the order.³
Where a clear undertaking is given on questioning (examination
for discovery) to produce a certain document, best efforts exerted are not enough excuse for non-
compliance to avoid a ﬁnding of contempt.⁴
Intent to disobey is unnecessary.⁵
So it is enough to prove an intentional act or omission which
in fact breaches a clear order or court process of which the accused had notice.⁶
Direct knowledge
of the order is not needed if wilful blindness is proved.⁷
Error of law or acting on legal advice is not a defence.⁸
Nor is delegation to subordinates.⁹
It is
not an excuse that one’s lawyer was tied up or busy.¹⁰
A person ordered to do something can take
a little time to consider how, but cannot weigh competing considerations as to whether to obey.¹¹
