Nor is it a defence, that the parties had agreed to stop obeying an order,¹
nor are prior conﬂicting
commitments.²
Though serious illness might be a defence, weak or vague evidence about illness
is not enough excuse.³
On what is a "reasonable excuse" (defence), see Imp. Finishing.⁴
On the other hand, reasonable excuse is relevant to more than just penalty. If the person
accused took reasonable steps to obey and acted in good faith, the judge has a discretion not to
convict, in order to avoid an injustice.⁵
Negligence is of course usually less blameworthy than deliberate disobedience.⁶
G. Jurisdiction and Error
Superior courts have full jurisdiction over allegations of contempt of court, even for acts before
a judge of the Alberta Court of Justice. “Competent jurisdiction” refers to what existed before
the Charter, and is not conﬁned to Charter issues. If one judge of the Alberta Court of Justice
is involved in the matters, she may and should refer the contempt matter to another judge. If the
Crown takes over the prosecution of contempt, it should then go to a superior court judge.⁷
It is well settled that an order of a superior court is never a nullity, and it is no defence to
contempt that the order disobeyed had been given as a result of some error or unfairness in
procedure or substance.⁸
Even if it was later upset on appeal.⁹
Violation of a known clear order
is contempt of court. It is no answer to contempt that the order itself was improper or should not
have been granted. It is valid and enforceable unless and until set aside.¹⁰
Nor is it an excuse that
the court order created a safety hazard, nor that the person disobeying is aboriginal.¹¹
Semble if a court has jurisdiction over a suit (e. g. by the respondent’s attornment), it has
jurisdiction to hear an application for contempt by disobeying the court’s directions.¹²

Nor is default or disobedience by one’s opponent an excuse.¹
Bad conduct of the opposing
party is irrelevant to contempt by this party, unless the opposing party actually impeded this
party’s performance of his or her duties and so led to the contemptuous act or omission.²
But it is not contempt not to obey an order which has already expired; and doubt whether it
has, can bar contempt.³
Our courts can order disclosure of foreign assets, so lack of jurisdiction
to make the order does not arise on a motion for contempt by nondisclosure.⁴
A party might get the court’s permission to withdraw an ill-advised undertaking, but only if the
party seeks that by a prompt application, not as an attempted defence to a much later contempt
motion.⁵
A bankruptcy Registrar can ﬁnd and punish contempt, but not imprison.⁶
Parliament purported to give youth courts exclusive jurisdiction over ex facie contempt by
youths. The grant does not violate s.96 of the Constitution, but the exclusivity does: the grant is
not exclusive.⁷
H. Disobeying Court Orders⁸
1. Introduction
The most common type of contempt of court is disobeying, or interfering with observance of,
a court order⁹
(except failure to pay money).¹⁰
On disobeying an erroneous court order, see R.10.52n. G, and the C. P. E. , Chapter 56, Part
M.3 and Chapter 62, Part P.3.
This is especially bad when done by a lawyer.¹¹

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.52(3)
Respect for the court’s dignity, authority, and processes, is vital. Overlooking intentional
breaches of court orders erodes respect for the proper administration of justice. Especially where
the disobedience was not a momentary lapse of judgment amidst proper conduct.¹
2. Intent and Knowledge
Bad intent, or special mens rea, or intent to disobey or to harm, is not necessary for contempt.
It is enough to know the act and know²
of the contrary court injunction.³
There must be proof
beyond a reasonable doubt that the person accused had actual knowledge of the order which was
not obeyed.⁴
That the act done might not ultimately gain a beneﬁt is no defence to contempt, if it is
done with knowledge of an order to the contrary: trying to obstruct justice by interference with an
order (not yet in effect) is enough.⁵
Even in child custody matters, there is no room for self-help.⁶
Disobeying an arbitration award is not, without more, contempt.⁷
Even if before the arbitration the
court said that any later award would be deemed an order of the court, and then it approved this
award?⁸
Contempt by disobedience includes an inferior tribunal’s order which by statute has been
ﬁled in King's Bench and is enforceable as a King's Bench order would be.⁹
Claiming to have no key to the residence she owned, and so being unable to give one to
the realtor, despite an order to cooperate in selling the home, was either simple refusal, or was
engineering key custody so she could state she had no key, and was held to be in contempt of
court. She had a legal right to a key.¹⁰
People are to obey the spirit of court orders, but ambiguity in the relevant part of the order may
be a defence to a contempt charge.¹¹

or application cannot be given by consent or concession: D. S. v. V. W. [1996] 2 SCR 108, 196 NR 241; but cf.
1199918 Alta. v. TRL Hldg.2011 ABQB 506, 523 AR 274.
7 Martineau v. Henry Espina 2023 ABKB 664, JCL 4806 02213 (Nov 23).
8 Martineau v. Henry Espina, supra. Quaere; odd brief reference to strictness, on which see Part D above.
9 Amalg. Transit U. , Local 569 v. Edmonton, supra, Part C. See the C. P. E. , Chapter 62, Part P.5. Where
statute allows a tribunal’s decision to be ﬁled in King's Bench and enforced as though a judgment, for
contempt, the court can have regard to some facts occurring before ﬁling, though there cannot be contempt
before ﬁling (¶’s 69-73) : Continuing Care Employers’ Bargaining Assn. v. A. U. P. E. & Maclennan 2002
ABCA 148, 303 AR 137.

14123 (Sep 6) (¶ 84). But indirect or circumstantial proof of such actual knowledge will sufﬁce: Koch v. Koch
2017 ABCA 310, Calg 1701 0091 AC (Sep 27) (¶’s 18 ff. ). If the parties agree to an order requiring one of
them to do some work, but the order proves not clear enough to found contempt, it may be too late for the

10-156
R.10.52(3) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Being over-busy is no excuse.¹
That the person who got the order had poor motives is
irrelevant.²
To be guilty, one has to know of the order or court process.³
3. Those Liable
Who can be liable for disobedience of an order is a little complicated. But the general rule is
that anyone who knowingly assists in interfering with the working of an order is guilty of contempt,
even if he or she is not a party to the suit and not named in the injunction.⁴
If one is named by
the court and the injunction is mandatory, one can be liable for contempt if one does nothing,
carelessly assuming that others will perform.⁵
Or tells one's opponent to do the work himself, and
hands him the source information and tells him to do the searching in it.⁶
It is contempt to disobey
directions of a pre-trial conference judge, and to refuse to cooperate at the last moment.⁷
Foreign
secrecy laws do not excuse disobeying a court order for better discovery.⁸
4. Miscellaneous
On reasonable excuse and burden of proof, see notes C and F above.
There is a discussion of repeatedly doing what the court had forbidden and failing to do what
it had ordered.⁹
The idea that the harm had already occurred, was a defence, was rejected.¹⁰
A continuing failure to obey a mandatory order (e. g. to produce items) is not a continuing
contempt allowing repeated convictions, nor allowing additions to an existing ﬁxed-length jail
sentence. A new conviction requires a new actus reus.¹¹
But if the injunction is negative, a new
act is a new actus reus.¹²
That the wrongful act has since become irrevocable, or that one has a conﬂicting duty, is
no excuse. There are better ways to deal with such a conﬂict.¹³
Claiming to have no key to the
residence which the respondent owned, and so being unable to give one to the realtor, despite a
court order to cooperate in selling the home, was either simple refusal, or was engineering key
custody so she could state she had no key, and was contempt. She had a legal right to get a key.¹⁴

10-157
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.52(3)
Failure to obey an order to pay money into court is not an "order to pay money" under R.
10.52(3) (a) (i).¹
Nor is failure to pay money into one’s solicitor’s trust account, or to another
stakeholder, nor an order to give an accounting and pay the balance.²
I. Non-Attendance
A lawyer’s failure to appear in court despite a direction to do so, can be contempt.³
Failure to show up as a witness⁴
at questioning (examination for discovery) or failure to ﬁle an
afﬁdavit of documents, under the Rules or an appointment, is no longer itself contempt; the Rule
now requires that an order be obtained and breached to constitute contempt.⁵
What remedy ﬂows
from that is more a matter of civil procedure than it is one of punishing contempt.
In many situations, the court is more concerned to aid the innocent party by securing
enforcement of the duty to attend or give discovery, and to indemnify the innocent party’s
expenses.⁶
The Court of Appeal afﬁrmed an order that a plaintiff who would not reveal how
an accident happened, nor come to examinations for discovery, not be able to set the matter
down for trial until he did submit to examinations for discovery. There was doubt that the plaintiff
wanted to go to trial. Credibility was a live issue.⁷
A corporate defendant’s representative came to
examination for discovery uninformed and not ready to answer questions, even after a hiatus and
a warning in writing. It could be contempt in another case.⁸
It is not a defence to contempt that one’s lawyer was tied up or busy, so one did not come.⁹
The practice of not attending court, but faxing a request for adjournment the day before, was
criticized and did not work here.¹⁰
J. Undertakings
Breach of an undertaking to the court is contempt, and such undertakings have much the
same effect as court orders.¹¹
They are often given in lieu of injunctions, or as a condition of
getting an injunction or other interlocutory relief. Some undertakings are often implied, such as
the undertaking to pay damages caused by a wrongly-granted interlocutory injunction which one
has obtained, or the undertaking not to make collateral use of information one has got through the
discovery process.¹²
The same liability for contempt attaches to persons whose property is seized
for distress or to enforce a judgment, but are left in possession of the property, by the seizing
bailiff.¹³

10-158
R.10.52(3) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Long failure to answer the undertakings on questioning (examination for discovery), despite an
order to do so, may lead to a ﬁnding of contempt and striking out the defaulter’s pleadings.¹
Best
efforts are not enough compliance to avoid a ﬁnding of contempt.²
One who gave an undertaking
cannot unilaterally qualify or withdraw it, and if performance is impossible, sworn evidence of that
is necessary.³
An undertaking to pay costs is not determining costs; it is a separate matter.⁴
Courts have
always had the power to enforce undertakings to the court, by contempt proceedings. And if the
undertaking was to pay money, the court has no power to revoke or vary the undertaking, as there
is also a contract with the other party.⁵
The court also has inherent power to enforce undertakings by solicitors even if they are
not to the court and have nothing to do with litigation, e. g. trust conditions or undertakings in
conveyancing.⁶
K. Other Examples of Contempt
Besides those things speciﬁcally enumerated in R.10.52(3), many other things are contempt
of court at common law.⁷
Here are some examples:
1. deterring a witness from testifying,⁸
or deterring a party from going on with her suit or
defence, whether by bribes, threats, physical interference, or by a publicity campaign
such as biased and one-sided coverage in the media of a civil suit.⁹
That overlaps with
2. prejudicing a pending trial, especially a jury trial, by one-sided publicity or by publishing
inadmissible evidence such as a confession.¹⁰
A publication on the eve of a jury parole-
eligibility hearing was held not strong enough to be contempt, especially as most of the
contents were already known to the public.¹¹
And publication of facts likely admissible at
trial in a newspaper with little circulation in the relevant city, well before a jury would be
empanelled, is not contempt.¹²
3. publishing criticisms of the honesty or motives of the court;¹³
but it is permissible to say
that a decision which the court has already given is wrong, or stupid or ignorant, even if
in fact it is the criticism itself which is wrong, stupid or ignorant.
4. letting a court go through a whole hearing about whether to forbid an act, without revealing
that one had already committed the act.¹⁴
Failure by counsel to make full disclosure on an
ex parte motion may amount to contempt if deliberate.¹⁵

PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.52(3)
5. at some point disrespect for the court and its processes, and disrespectful speech, can
become contempt.¹
6. attempting to intimidate a court ofﬁcial.²
But breach of a Rule is not itself contempt, if there is no judge's order compelling obedience.
The court can often relieve against not obeying a Rule, especially if no prejudice results.³
L. Appellate Review
A decision that someone is guilty of contempt of court, before any court decision on penalty,
is a “decision” under R.14.8 and so can be appealed, and time to appeal guilt starts running.
Separate appeals from the guilt and the penalty are possible, and the fact that a single appeal is
also possible, does not stop time running from the ﬁnding of guilt. Contempt proceedings are often
bifurcated between the two.⁴
On appeal from a contempt ﬁnding, correctness is the test for questions of law, but otherwise
the standard is reasonableness.⁵
And palpable and overriding error for fact questions, or for mixed
questions or applying a legal standard to particular facts.⁶
A ﬁnding of civil contempt is factual and
is reviewed on appeal for palpable and overriding error.⁷
Was there no (or insufﬁcient) weight to
relevant considerations, or failure to consider some, or consideration of irrelevant considerations?
The Court of Appeal will not substitute its own discretion.⁸
M. Other Avenues to Punish or Enforce
Costs against a lawyer personally are another way for the courts to control abuse of process,
working in parallel with contempt and with a Law Society's punishments (and contempt is also an
inherent court power). But the sanctions are not mutually exclusive, and might even be imposed
concurrently for the same misconduct.⁹
Which remedy to adopt when a lawyer is guilty of abuse
of process depends in part on the consequences, as contempt has strict procedures and stricter
standard of proof, so in a given case a court may opt for personal costs instead of contempt.¹⁰
The authority of the court over a solicitor is preventative, to ensure fair trial and justice, but the
Law Society acts reactively, and each body has its own role, so the court need not rely on the
Law Society to oversee and sanction conduct before the court.¹¹
Usually personal costs are a less
harsh penalty or shorter duration than contempt or professional discipline by the Law Society.¹²

R.10.53(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Section 127 of the Criminal Code can be used against someone defying an interlocutory
injunction. The exception in s.127 only applies to a speciﬁc express statutory procedure, e. g.
deﬁning contempt and its penalty. Rules of Court setting penalty or procedure do not trigger that
exception, if the contempt order would be under the Rule or the court’s inherent power.¹
A lawyer can be sanctioned by his or her professional organization for having written a
disrespectful, insulting letter to a judge, and the Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms does
not bar such discipline.²
Non-payment of costs is not contempt because of the exception for "an order to pay money" in
R.10.52(3) (a) (i). But noncompliance with a child access order alleging unwillingness of the child
permits the Court to direct an intervention.³
Punishment for Civil Contempt of Court
10.53(1) Every person declared to be in civil contempt of Court is liable to any
one or more of the following penalties or sanctions in the discretion of a judge:
(a) imprisonment until the person has purged the person’s contempt;
(b) imprisonment for not more than 2 years;
(c) a ﬁne and, in default of paying the ﬁne, imprisonment for not more
than 6 months;
(d) if the person is a party to an action, application or proceeding, an
order that
(i) all or part of a commencement document, afﬁdavit or
pleading be struck out,
(ii) an action or an application be stayed,
(iii) a claim, action, defence, application or proceeding be
dismissed, or judgment be entered or an order be made, or
(iv) a record or evidence be prohibited from being used or
entered in an application, proceeding or at trial.
(2) The Court may also make a costs award against a person declared to be in
civil contempt of Court.
(3) If a person declared to be in civil contempt of Court purges the person’s
contempt, the Court may waive or suspend any penalty or sanction.
(4) The judge who imposed a penalty or sanction for civil contempt may, on
notice to the person concerned, increase, vary or remit the penalty or sanction.⁴

pleading, record

with rules or orders); 10.50 (costs payable by lawyer) ; 10.52 (declaration of civil contempt)

10-161
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.53(4)
A. Introduction
Generally on penalties for contempt, see the discussion and authorities in the textbooks.¹
The penalty for contempt is not limited to what is listed in R.10.53. The Court may substitute
a more comprehensive simpler ban harder to argue about.²
Contempt is intended both to punish and to end the wrong conduct.³
And to deter such
misconduct by others.⁴
One must remember that contempt is not the only way to enforce a court order. In many
cases the Civil Enforcement Act will offer a variety of remedies, either instead or in addition. And
if someone fails to convey property as ordered, the court may authorize a court ofﬁcial to sign the
conveyance.⁵
See further R.10.52 n. M.
On whether part of R.10.53 is unconstitutional, see the reference to litigation in the Turkawski
case.⁶
B. Penalties
1. Aims and Aggravation
A number of criteria for sentencing and weight of penalty are reviewed.⁷
Continuing earlier
deliberate deﬁance, with little attempt to purge the contempt, and stubbornness, and refusal to
pay any costs, were bad⁸
and led to striking out the contemnor's pleadings.⁹
Furthermore, both the common law and Rr.10.53(1) (a), 10.53(3) and 10.53(4) allow the
court to suspend, end, vary, or remit a penalty for contempt.¹⁰
That can be done to encourage
belated obedience by the contemnor and encourage his or her undoing the effects of his or
her contempt.¹¹
Or it can be done retroactively to give him or her credit for such restitution or

10-162
R.10.53(4) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
repentance.¹
Encouraging compliance (rather than punishing) is important where the contempt
was failure to give discovery.²
If there is no public deﬁance, contempt is usually more a matter of enforcement than of
punishment, though orders often properly seek to do both.³
If a penalty is imposed for a continuing
disobedience, it is useful for the court to indicate how much of that penalty is for the past and so
irrevocable, and how much is for the future and so might be reduced or discharged if there is later
obedience.⁴
But continued deliberate and dishonest disobedience produces a need for a penalty
to deter the miscreant and others tempted to do the same.⁵
Aggravating circumstances for a higher penalty include being a lawyer, the potential beneﬁts
(savings) of disobedience, public nature of the contempt, the harm done to the opposing party
(including thrown-away costs) and to the court (though compensation is not a licensing fee or
substitute for the penalty), the motive for disobeying, other consequences, the context, the degree
of culpability of each person, the nature of the contempt, whether the disobedience was reported,
knowledge and understanding, displays of deﬁance, effects on the public, need for deterrence,
and ability to pay.⁶
That a party (eventually and reluctantly) purged his contempt and ﬁnally obeyed an order, is
no reason to decline to decide whether he had been in contempt before that. Purging is relevant
only to the size of the penalty.⁷
A continuing failure to obey a mandatory order (e. g. to produce items) is not a continuing
contempt allowing repeated convictions, nor allowing additions to an existing ﬁxed-length jail
sentence. A new conviction requires a new actus reus. But if the injunction is negative, a new act
is a new actus reus.⁸
An indeﬁnite sentence until the contempt is purged may be appropriate.⁹
And breach of more court orders after the contempt conviction is relevant too.¹⁰

10-163
PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC. R.10.53(4)
2. Jail¹
It was sometimes suggested that jail is not imposed in Alberta for contempt, but that is not
correct. Most such sentences are merely not in writing or reported; some have no recorded
reasons, especially as the reasons are oral.²
A few days’ jail for breach of a restraining order is
common, and in many cases time in custody is imposed after someone has been arrested while
breaching an order.³
Ninety days in jail plus community service, was imposed on a disbarred
lawyer who very persistently for years kept practising law despite court orders forbidding that.⁴
Even indeterminate jail sentences are known, and the only limit is 5 years (because there is a
right to a jury over 5 years).⁵
There is full power to order community service as a penalty, and it need not be structured as
a condition (e. g. for remission of jail).⁶
The court has inherent power to impose other remedies or punishments not listed in the Rules
of Court for contempt, so long as they meet judicial criteria.⁷
3. Fines
Fines are important penalties for contempt of court, though ability to pay and surrounding
circumstances must be carefully looked at; some case law reviewed.⁸
Financial penalties under R.10.49(1) and R.10.53(1) (c) are to be paid to the Government of
Alberta, not to individual litigants.⁹
There is no longer any ceiling on the ﬁne which may be imposed.¹⁰
$400, 000 was upheld for
two public contempts in the United Nurses case.¹¹
$400, 000 ﬁne for a single instance of civil
contempt was too high. Its publicity and the intent are not relevant if the conviction is for civil, not

criminal, contempt. The ﬁne was cut to $200, 000.¹
For wrongly gaining revenues of $6 million, the

Breach of an Alberta injunction against pursuing proceedings abroad led to a further

the upcoming summary judgment motion by the parties would be stayed and not re-scheduled
without such payments (despite long waits to get that scheduled date).⁴
For continuing long-standing failure to obey a court order to produce certain records, a ﬁne of
$1, 500 was imposed, plus $100 extra for each future calendar day until full compliance.⁵
It was proper to use a large ﬁne paid for contempt of court in a civil matter, to pay the
psychologists involved in treating the children, but the father paying the ﬁne was not to be
considered to have paid for that counselling. He paid the court as punishment.⁶
Even with continued contempt (disobeying court orders), another judge cannot vary a contempt
penalty set by the ﬁrst judge.⁷

See also Part D below.
Pleadings are often struck out,⁸
or an action is commonly stayed,⁹
to enforce compliance, but
it is not often proceeded with once compliance has been secured.¹⁰

courts and the public are less willing to waste scarce resources on the contumelious or extremely
lax litigant; most litigation is too slow as it is.¹
Where a litigant persists in disobeying an order for
better discovery, the Court may strike out his pleading if he does not comply.²
His own discovery
may be ended.³
For the plaintiff's repeated disobedience of orders to renew letters of credit given as security,
the suit would be dismissed, unless within 5 days the security was restored.⁴
Persistent contempt
and failure to purge it, led to a penalty of dismissing the contemnor's pending application to add a
new defendant to existing litigation.⁵
The remedy of forbidding a party to adduce certain evidence⁶
is a good one which is not used
enough.⁷
A party who ﬂees and adduces no evidence risks losing even a child custody ﬁght: not
as a penalty, but because only the other side’s evidence is available.⁸
Persistent contempt can produce other enforcement measures outside the litigation, such as
vacating premises while a realtor is there to inspect or show property which the court has ordered
sold.⁹
If the contemnor is an executor or trustee, all compensation from the estate for services or
expenses may be denied him or her.¹⁰
C. Costs
If contempt is found, costs should be imposed (on top of any penalty). Usually full indemnity
is the appropriate level. Those costs are not just a sanction or a declaration; the innocent party
should not have to pay for the publicly-vital task of enforcing court orders. Otherwise the innocent
party is being punished.¹¹

R.10.53(4) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
Conviction for contempt usually leads to heavy costs as well as any penalty.¹
The costs are
remedial. Partial indemnity thrown-away party-party costs are not a sufﬁcient remedy for deliberate
breach of a court order. A substantial portion of the expenses directly caused should ordinarily
be a minimum.²
Often “solicitor-client” (full indemnity) costs are ordered.³
In one case,⁴
the hourly
rate of the lawyer applying was low, so slightly enhanced solicitor-client costs were upheld on
appeal.⁵
In another case the contemnor paid $136, 000 costs (plus a hefty ﬁne).⁶
The penalty will
be inﬂuenced by the role of counsel, motives, consequences of the acts, the history of the suit,
expense caused to others, and degree of culpability.⁷
A protestor in contempt of an injunction
though he thought he had a right to protest, may have to pay a proportionate share of the costs.⁸
A defendant found guilty of contempt partway through the suit had to pay costs at once, not
at the end of the suit, to impose discipline on the litigation process, given the nature of contempt,
because an earlier order had set dates to pay the costs. Costs are paid forthwith, and there were
no special circumstances.⁹
Contempt was found and not purged, but trial would come in a few months and the steps about
contempt and about getting to trial could not be easily separated, so the trial judge should deal
with all the costs, even those for contempt.¹⁰
Costs imposed by a justice as a penalty for contempt cannot ordinarily be reversed by another
justice later.¹¹
D. Party in Contempt Cannot Make Application
Contempt of court does more than expose the guilty person to the penalties for contempt set
out in R.10.53. It also bars that person from taking any active voluntary positive step in court
while he or she is still in contempt.¹²
The contempt does not bar self-defence, but it does bar

appeals from anything but the contempt ﬁnding.¹
An appeal over custody may be adjourned until
the appellant purges her contempt by bringing the children back to Alberta.²
An act contrary to an

Here the ﬁne levied for the contempts found had been paid. The costs had not, but failure to
pay them is for money and so not contempt. More ongoing breaches of the injunction were sworn
to, but there had been no motion for ﬁnding contempt by them, and contempt needs proof beyond
a reasonable doubt. So audience and consideration on the merits were allowed here.⁴

E. Miscellaneous
Natural justice and the audi alteram partem rule require the court to give counsel the
opportunity to address a proposed sanction.⁵
A judge asked to punish for contempt of court should alert counsel before imposing a sanction
signiﬁcantly exceeding or differing from that sought by the party asking for punishment or
enforcement, and give both counsel the chance to address the new proposed sanction.⁶
To get a stay of contempt sanctions pending appeal, it is not enough to swear that one lacks
funds to pay the sanctions; clear convincing or cogent evidence in support is needed. And an
expensive lifestyle and unexplained disappearance of assets can be evidence pointing the other
way. Harm to reputation from contempt diminishes after contempt is found.⁷
Someone convicted of contempt can purge his or her contempt only by complying with the
orders or duties disobeyed, albeit not complying perfectly.⁸
There was a ﬁnding that certain
payments were not paid for the ﬁne and did not purge contempt.⁹
Very sloppy incomplete acts (in
answering a questionnaire about assets) were not enough to purge contempt.¹⁰
Breach of a court order is not of itself a ground for damages to another party.¹¹
The court can
give damages for breach of an undertaking by a solicitor.¹²
A witness who fails to appear may also
be liable for any resulting damage.¹³
Failure to disclose facts to the court when seeking an order
ex parte might lead to damages as a remedy?¹⁴

R.10.54(1) PART 10: LAWYERS’ CHARGES, RECOVERABLE COSTS, ETC.
On appeal, the appeal court should be slow to substitute different penalties for those imposed
by a King's Bench justice;¹
the discretion encompasses penalty too.²
Rule 10.53 is discretionary
and so reviewed on appeal for palpable and overriding error.³
An appeal was not ground for
release from jail for contempt pending appeal, where there still was a serious ﬂight risk.⁴
The Supreme Court of Canada’s individual judges have power to give bail pending appeal to
the Supreme Court of Canada from contempt, even civil contempt.⁵
Conviction for contempt does not bar a later prosecution under the Criminal Code, e. g. for
harassment (stalking).⁶
As contempt is a wrong to the court and the public,⁷
it is not surprising that assigning oneself
into bankruptcy may well not stop contempt punishment.⁸
A self-proclaimed Sovereign Free-will Man and his documents and notions ran afoul of the
criminal bail régime.⁹
DIVISION 5
MEDICAL EXAMINATION
Mental Disorder
10.54(1) In this rule,
(a) “examination” means a medical examination conducted for the
purpose of determining a person’s mental state;
(b) “facility” means
(i) a facility as deﬁned in the Mental Health Act, or
(ii) a correctional institution as deﬁned in the Corrections Act.
(2) If a person is declared to be in civil contempt of Court and the judge is satisﬁed
that there are reasonable and probable grounds to believe that the person is
(a) suffering from a mental disorder, or
(b) likely to cause harm to himself or herself or others or to suffer
substantial mental or physical deterioration or serious physical
impairment,
and would otherwise be unwilling, on that person’s own initiative, to attend an
examination, the judge may order that the person be taken into custody by a
peace ofﬁcer and taken to a facility for the purpose of examination and a report
to the Court, as directed by the Court.

DIVISION 6
INHERENT JURISDICTION
Inherent Jurisdiction
10.55 Nothing in these rules prevents or is to be interpreted as preventing the
Court, as a superior court, from exercising its inherent power to cite in contempt
and punish those who disobey the Court’s lawful orders or who otherwise
display contempt for its process.²

Related Provisions
1.3 (general authority of court) ; 10.52 (contempt)

See also R.10.53(1) n. M.
